# VALUEVADE · ZERO TO ANALYST
## MODULE 19 · THE CAPSTONE

A complete study guide: 12 sessions on how elite analysts and capital
allocators actually think — first principles, second-order thinking,
probability, mental models, inversion, forensic reading, signal vs noise,
capital allocation, debiasing, decision-making under uncertainty, and
staying irreplaceable in the age of AI. India-first throughout.

96 notes cards · 120 quizzes · 120 interview questions · 12 simulations · 1 capstone
Educational material. All companies/figures are illustrative; verify against filings and screener.in. Not investment advice.


### Module Overview

The capstone of Zero to Analyst. Every earlier module gave you the tools — statements,
ratios, models, valuation. This one installs the operating system that runs them: how elite
analysts and capital allocators actually think. You will learn to break any business down to
first principles, see the second- and third-order consequences the crowd misses, think in
probabilities instead of false certainties, carry a latticework of mental models, invert
problems to dodge disaster, read financial statements and news as narrative rather than
noise, and adopt the mind of a CEO who allocates capital. The final session is the one that
matters most in 2026: how to stay irreplaceable in the age of AI by owning the judgment,
synthesis and taste a machine cannot. India-first throughout — NSE/BSE companies, SEBI, Ind
AS, real Indian market events.


### What you will learn

- Climb the ladder from data to judgment instead of stopping at information
- Break any business or problem down to first principles
- Apply second- and third-order thinking to see around corners
- Reason in probabilities, base rates and expected value rather than certainties
- Build and use a multidisciplinary latticework of mental models
- Use inversion and pre-mortems to avoid catastrophic mistakes
- Read financial statements as a story about a business, not a pile of numbers
- Separate signal from narrative when interpreting news and markets
- Think like a capital allocator and CEO, not just a number-cruncher
- Recognise and counter your own cognitive biases
- Make high-conviction decisions under uncertainty and incomplete information
- Stay irreplaceable in the age of AI by owning judgment, synthesis and taste

Prerequisite. Best taken last. Assumes comfort with the three financial
statements, ratio analysis, valuation and financial modelling (the earlier Zero to
Analyst modules). This module ties every technical skill together into a way of
thinking.

## Session 1 — The Analyst's Edge — How Elite Minds Actually Think
The meta-skill that the whole module rests on. Why, in an age where AI reads every
annual report in seconds, you are paid for judgment, not facts. The four levels of thinking
(data to judgment) and the five mental tools you are about to install.


**In this session**

- Distinguish data, information, insight and judgment
- Explain what an analyst is actually paid for in the AI era
- Name the five core thinking tools this module installs
- Spot clerk-thinking versus analyst-thinking in a real situation


### [HOOK] Same Numbers, Opposite Calls
9:05 AM, a Mumbai brokerage. Two analysts open the exact same Q3 results of an Indian
consumer company. Same revenue, same margin, same management commentary. Analyst
A writes: 'Volume growth slowing, margins peaked — DOWNGRADE.' Analyst B writes: 'Gross
margin held despite an input-cost spike, distribution is widening, and the slowdown is the
base effect of a blockbuster last year — this is the setup for a multi-year re-rating, UPGRADE.'
Same data. Opposite conclusions. The difference was not the numbers in front of them — it
was the thinking behind their eyes.

The question. In 2026, an AI can read every annual report ever filed in seconds
and compute every ratio instantly and without error. So what, exactly, are you
being paid for?

Why it matters. The honest answer is the entire subject of this module: judgment. The
machine owns the facts. You own the thinking it cannot do. Master that, and you are
irreplaceable. Skip it, and you are a slower, more expensive calculator.


### [CONCEPT] From Information to Judgment — The Real Job
There are four levels you can operate at, and almost everyone gets stuck on the wrong one.
(1) DATA — raw facts: revenue was Rs 4,200 cr. (2) INFORMATION — data with context:
revenue grew 8% YoY, below the 12% the sector did. (3) INSIGHT — the non-obvious 'so
what': the 8% is entirely volume-led while peers grew on price hikes that will reverse, so this
company's growth is more durable. (4) JUDGMENT — the decision under uncertainty: given
that durability, the stock's premium multiple is justified and the risk-reward favours holding.
Beginners proudly stop at Level 2 ('I read the whole report and know all the numbers'). AI
lives at Levels 1-2 and beats you there every time. Elite analysts are paid to live at Levels
3-4. The job was never to know more facts. It is to reason better about what the facts mean.

Analogy. WebMD contains more raw medical data than any single doctor's
memory. Yet you would never let WebMD perform your surgery. A surgeon's
value is not data recall — it is judgment under uncertainty: pattern recognition,
weighing trade-offs, and deciding when the textbook does not fit the patient on
the table. You are the surgeon. AI is WebMD. Never confuse the two.


**Key Terms**

Data — Raw, context-free facts. The lowest rung. AI's home turf.
Information — Data placed in context — comparisons, trends, ratios.
Insight — The non-obvious meaning the crowd has missed. Where edge begins.
Judgment — A decision made under uncertainty using insight. What you are paid for.
Mental model — A reusable thinking tool that helps you reason about how the world works.


### [CONCEPT] The Five Pillars of Elite Thinking — Your Toolkit
A thinker with one tool treats every problem the same way. This module hands you five tools
the best investors reach for, plus four meta-skills. The five pillars: (1) FIRST-PRINCIPLES
THINKING — reason up from fundamental truths, not by copying the consensus (Session 2).
(2) SECOND- & THIRD-ORDER THINKING — see the consequence of the consequence the
crowd ignores (Session 3). (3) PROBABILISTIC THINKING — weigh odds and expected value
instead of pretending to predict (Session 4). (4) THE LATTICEWORK OF MENTAL MODELS —
borrow the best ideas from physics, biology and psychology, not just finance (Session 5). (5)
INVERSION — solve problems by asking how they fail (Session 6). Then the meta-skills that
apply them: reading statements as narrative (S7), filtering signal from noise (S8), the capital
allocator's lens (S9), debiasing your own brain (S10), deciding under uncertainty (S11), and
staying irreplaceable in the AI age (S12). Treat this card as the map of everything you are
about to become.

Analogy. A roadside mechanic with only a hammer eventually treats every
problem like a nail — and ruins the engine. A master mechanic opens a full
toolbox and, crucially, knows which tool the problem in front of them actually
calls for. These five pillars are your toolbox. The skill is not owning them; it is
knowing which one to pick up.


**Key Terms**

First-principles thinking — Reasoning up from fundamental truths rather than by analogy.
Second-order thinking — Asking 'and then what?' — the consequences of the consequences.
Base rate — How often something happens in general, before you look at the specific case.
Latticework — Munger's idea of a connected web of mental models from many disciplines.
Inversion — Solving a problem by studying how to fail at it, then avoiding that.


### [VISUAL] The Ladder of Analytical Thinking
Visual: ladder framework
Picture a five-rung ladder. Raw inputs enter at the bottom and only the climber who reaches
the top produces a high-conviction call. Rung 1 DATA → Rung 2 INFORMATION → Rung 3
INSIGHT → Rung 4 JUDGMENT → Rung 5 CONVICTION. Draw a horizontal line across rungs 1
and 2 labelled 'AI dominates here — instant, error-free, free'. Draw a second line across rungs
3, 4 and 5 labelled 'The human edge — where careers are made'. The visual makes one thing
undeniable: the value, the pay and the moat all live in the top half of the ladder, and most
beginners never climb past rung 2.
Elements
- Rung 1 — DATA: raw facts (revenue, debt, EPS). AI fetches this instantly.
- Rung 2 — INFORMATION: data in context (growth rates, ratios, peer comparison). AI
computes this instantly.
- Rung 3 — INSIGHT: the non-obvious 'so what' the crowd has missed. Human edge starts.
- Rung 4 — JUDGMENT: a decision under uncertainty built on that insight.
- Rung 5 — CONVICTION: a sized, defendable call you can hold when the market disagrees.
- AI LINE (under rung 2): everything below is commoditised — speed and recall.
- HUMAN EDGE LINE (above rung 2): everything above is judgment AI cannot replicate.


### [DEEP DIVE] How Great Investors Actually Think

Two minds, one brain (System 1 vs System 2)
Daniel Kahneman showed the brain runs two systems. System 1 is fast, automatic, intuitive
— it fires a snap judgment before you have reasoned. System 2 is slow, effortful, deliberate.
Beginners let System 1 run the show: they see a high P/E and instantly feel 'expensive', or
read a bullish headline and feel 'buy'. Elite analysts know when to trust trained intuition
(System 1 sharpened by thousands of reps) and when to force the slow, deliberate System 2
— especially when the stakes are high or the story is seductive. The skill is metacognition:
thinking about which mode you are in.

Why multidisciplinary beats specialist (the latticework)
Charlie Munger argued that if you only have finance models, you will torture every problem
to fit them. The world is run by ideas from many disciplines at once — incentives
(psychology), compounding (mathematics), competitive moats (biology and evolution),
feedback loops (engineering). A worldly-wise thinker lays a 'latticework' of these models
across a problem and sees it in stereo. This is why the best investors are voracious,
cross-disciplinary readers, not narrow technicians.

Thinking in bets, not certainties
Howard Marks and Annie Duke make the same point from different worlds (investing and
poker): the future is a distribution of outcomes, not a single fact. The best are right perhaps
60% of the time — and win anyway, because they size positions to the odds and chase
asymmetric payoffs where being right pays far more than being wrong costs. A
confident-sounding point forecast ('the stock will hit Rs 900') is usually a sign of a weak
thinker, not a strong one.

The map is not the territory

Your DCF, your ratio sheet, your model — these are maps. The living, messy business is the
territory. Amateurs fall in love with the precision of the map and forget it is a simplification.
Elite analysts hold their models loosely, ask constantly 'where does this map lie?', and never
let a clean spreadsheet override an ugly real-world fact.
Real-world references
- Charlie Munger — the 'latticework of mental models' and the call to be worldly-wise.
- Howard Marks (Oaktree) — memos on 'second-level thinking' and thinking in probabilities.
- Daniel Kahneman — 'Thinking, Fast and Slow', System 1 vs System 2.
- Raamdeo Agrawal / Motilal Oswal — the QGLP lens (Quality, Growth, Longevity, Price) as a thinking
framework, not a formula.
- Annie Duke — 'Thinking in Bets', separating decision quality from outcome quality.

EXAMPLE
Clerk-Thinking vs Analyst-Thinking — A Live
Dissection
Company / context: Bajaj Finance (NSE: BAJFINANCE)
Situation. Through much of the early-to-mid 2010s, Bajaj Finance traded at a steep
premium to other NBFCs. The consensus reaction from a large part of the street was simple
and confident: 'It's an NBFC trading at a rich multiple — too expensive, avoid.'
Data. The visible facts: a P/E and P/B well above the lending-sector average; an NBFC (a
category the market has historically de-rated); rapid loan-book growth; rising ROA/ROE; a
widening consumer-durable and cross-sell distribution machine; and data-led underwriting
keeping asset quality in check.
Analysis. Clerk-thinking reads ONE number in ISOLATION: 'high P/E = expensive = sell.' It
stops at Level 2 of the ladder. Analyst-thinking reads the SAME multiple AGAINST the
durability and length of the growth and the improving unit economics: a premium multiple on
a business that can compound earnings for a decade-plus, with a structural edge in
underwriting and distribution, is not 'expensive' — it is the fair price of a rare compounding
machine. The clerk anchored on the ratio. The analyst asked the second-order question:
'expensive relative to WHAT runway, and for HOW long?' That single shift in thinking
separated those who sold early from those who held through one of the great Indian
wealth-creation stories.
Analyst takeaway. A ratio is never a verdict — it is a question. A high P/E does not say
'sell'; it says 'go find out what the market is paying up for, and decide whether it is real.' The
number tells you where to look, not what to conclude. (This is a teaching illustration of a
thinking pattern, not a stock recommendation — and hindsight makes every call look
obvious.)

PITFALLS         The Four Thinking Traps That Kill Beginners

### 1. Confusing data with insight — 'I read the entire 300-page annual report, so I
understand the business.'
Why it happens: Effort feels like understanding. Reading is Level 2 (information); understanding is Level
3 (insight). The brain rewards the feeling of completeness.
Fix: After every document, force two questions: 'So what does this actually mean?' and 'What does the
crowd see here that I think is wrong?' If you can't answer both, you have information, not insight.


### 2. Single-cause thinking — 'The stock fell because of the weak results.'
Why it happens: The mind craves one tidy reason. Reality is multi-causal, and one clean story is easier
to tell (and to tweet).
Fix: Assume every outcome has several causes. Ask 'what else?' at least three times. The result you
see is usually results + positioning + macro + narrative + liquidity, all at once.


### 3. The certainty illusion — speaking and thinking in absolutes ('this will definitely
re-rate').
Why it happens: Certainty sounds smart and feels safe; probabilities feel like hedging. Juniors mistake
confidence for competence.
Fix: Convert every view into a probability and a kill-switch: 'I think there's a ~65% chance of a
re-rating, and I'm wrong if margins break below X or the regulator does Y.' Certainty is a red flag;
calibrated uncertainty is the real skill.


### 4. Narrative capture — falling for the story management, the media or the bull
thread is telling.
Why it happens: Humans are wired for stories far more than for spreadsheets. A compelling narrative
hijacks System 1 before System 2 checks the numbers.
Fix: Separate the narrative from the numbers on purpose. Then ask the most powerful debiasing
question there is: 'Who benefits from me believing this story?'


### [RECAP] The Analyst's Edge — Locking It In

**Key Takeaways**

- AI owns the facts and the math; you are paid for the judgment it cannot do.
- There are four levels — data, information, insight, judgment. Stop living at information.
- Climb the ladder: the value, the pay and the moat all sit in the top half (insight →
conviction).
- Carry a toolbox of five thinking models, and know which one a problem calls for.
- Treat every number — every ratio, every headline — as a question, never an answer.
- Certainty is a warning sign; calibrated probability is the mark of a real thinker.
- The map is not the territory: hold your models loosely and respect the messy business
underneath.

Analyst mindset. An elite analyst is not the person who knows the most facts
— a machine wins that now. It is the person who, faced with the same data as
everyone else, sees one level deeper, weighs the odds honestly, and has the
conviction to make a call and own it. That is a muscle. The next eleven sessions
are the gym.


**Connections**

- Session 2 (First-Principles) — how to manufacture Level-3 insight on purpose.
- Session 3 (Second-Order Thinking) — the 'and then what?' engine behind the Bajaj Finance
dissection.

- Session 7 (Reading Financials Like a Detective) — applying this mindset to the statements
themselves.
- Session 12 (Thinking in the Age of AI) — why this entire module is your career insurance.

## Quiz

**Q1. *[EASY]*** An AI tool can instantly fetch a company's revenue, compute

every ratio, and benchmark it against peers. On the 'Ladder of
Analytical Thinking', which rungs is the AI dominating?
- A. Insight and Judgment
- **✓ B. Data and Information**
- C. Judgment and Conviction
- D. Conviction only

> Why: Fetching facts (data) and computing ratios/benchmarks (information) are Rungs 1 and 2 — the commoditised bottom half where AI is instant and error-free. The human edge begins at Rung 3 (insight) and above.


**Q2. *[EASY]*** A junior says: 'I've read the entire 250-page annual report,

so I understand the business.' What level of thinking has the
junior actually reached?
- A. Judgment
- B. Conviction
- **✓ C. Information**
- D. Insight

> Why: Reading and absorbing the facts is Level 2 (information). Understanding the business — the non-obvious 'so what' — is Level 3 (insight). Effort feels like understanding, but they are different rungs.


**Q3. *[MEDIUM]*** Two analysts see the same high P/E on a quality NBFC.

Analyst A says 'expensive, sell'. Analyst B asks 'expensive
relative to what runway, and for how long?' What is Analyst B
demonstrating?
- A. Single-cause thinking
- B. Narrative capture
- **✓ C. Treating the ratio as a question, not a verdict**
- D. The certainty illusion

> Why: Analyst B refuses to read the ratio as a final verdict and instead uses it as a prompt to investigate durability and longevity — the core lesson that a number tells you where to look, not what to conclude.


**Q4. *[MEDIUM]*** Which statement best captures 'thinking in bets' as practised by elite investors?
- A. Always wait until you are 100% certain before acting
- B. Be right more than 90% of the time or don't invest
- **✓ C. Treat the future as a distribution of outcomes and size positions to the odds**
- D. Make confident point forecasts of exact target prices

> Why: Probabilistic thinking treats the future as a range of outcomes, accepts being right ~60% of the time, and wins through position sizing and asymmetric payoffs. Confident point forecasts are usually a sign of weak thinking.


**Q5. *[MEDIUM]*** In Kahneman's framework, an analyst sees a bullish headline

and instantly feels 'buy'. Which system fired, and what should an
elite analyst do next?

- A. System 2 fired; act on it immediately
- **✓ B. System 1 fired; deliberately engage System 2 to check the numbers behind the story**
- C. System 1 fired; trust it because experience is always right
- D. System 2 fired; ignore the numbers

> Why: The instant emotional reaction is fast, automatic System 1. Because a seductive narrative can hijack judgment, the skilled move is to consciously engage slow, deliberate System 2 — especially when stakes are high.


**Q6. *[HARD]*** Why did Charlie Munger argue for a multidisciplinary

'latticework' of mental models rather than mastering finance
alone?
- A. Because finance models are always wrong
- **✓ B. Because with only finance models you torture every problem to fit them, missing forces from psychology, biology and maths**
- C. Because reading widely is a hobby unrelated to investing
- D. Because specialists always outperform generalists

> Why: Munger's point is that the world is driven by ideas from many disciplines at once (incentives, compounding, moats, feedback loops). A single-discipline thinker distorts problems to fit their one toolkit. Breadth lets you see a problem in stereo.


**Q7. *[MEDIUM]*** 'The map is not the territory' warns analysts against which

error?
- A. Building financial models at all
- **✓ B. Falling in love with the precision of a model and forgetting it is a simplification of a messy real business**
- C. Using more than one mental model
- D. Reading annual reports too carefully

> Why: Models, ratios and DCFs are maps — useful simplifications. The error is treating the clean map as if it were the messy reality, letting a precise spreadsheet override an ugly real-world fact.


**Q8. *[EASY]*** A market commentator declares: 'The stock crashed today

purely because of the weak Q3 results.' Which thinking trap is on
display?
- A. The certainty illusion
- **✓ B. Single-cause thinking**
- C. The map is not the territory
- D. Base-rate neglect

> Why: Attributing a complex market move to one tidy cause is single-cause thinking. Real outcomes are usually results + positioning + macro + narrative + liquidity together — the fix is to keep asking 'what else?'


**Q9. *[HARD]*** Which question is the single most powerful debiasing tool

against narrative capture?
- A. What is the company's P/E?
- **✓ B. Who benefits from me believing this story?**
- C. What did the stock do yesterday?
- D. What is the consensus target price?

> Why: Asking 'who benefits from me believing this story?' forces you to examine the incentives behind a narrative — management, media, or a position-talking bull — and separates the story from the numbers.


**Q10. *[MEDIUM]*** An analyst states: 'This stock will definitely re-rate; I'm

100% sure.' From an elite-thinking standpoint, this statement is
mainly a sign of:
- A. Strong analytical skill and deep work
- **✓ B. A red flag — real skill is expressing a calibrated probability plus a condition that would prove the view wrong**
- C. Appropriate confidence that should be rewarded
- D. Good risk management

> Why: Certainty is the certainty illusion. The skilled formulation attaches a probability ('~65% chance') and a kill-switch ('I'm wrong if margins break below X'). Calibrated uncertainty, not bravado, is the mark of a real thinker.

## Interview Questions

### 1. An AI can build a DCF, read every filing, and compute every ratio in seconds.
Why should I hire you as an analyst?
Opinion / pressure question. The interviewer is testing whether the candidate understands what human
value actually is in 2026 — a question every analyst now faces.

**Strong answer.** Because you're not paying me for the facts or the math — you're paying me for
the judgment the machine can't do. AI lives on the bottom two rungs of analytical thinking: data
and information. It fetches and computes brilliantly. But it doesn't decide which questions are
worth asking, it doesn't weigh ambiguous trade-offs with skin in the game, and it doesn't own a
call when the market disagrees and the pressure is on. My edge is insight and judgment —
seeing what the consensus has missed, reasoning in probabilities, and having the conviction to
act and be accountable for it. I'd actually use the AI as leverage: let it do the grunt work at the
bottom of the ladder so I spend all my time at the top, where the real value is.

> Look for: Frames human value as judgment, not facts/recall · References the data-to-judgment progression clearly · Positions AI as leverage rather than threat · Shows ownership/accountability for a call under uncertainty


### 2. Walk me through how you would start thinking about a company you know
nothing about.
Conceptual question testing whether the candidate has a structured thinking process versus diving
straight into numbers.

**Strong answer.** I'd resist jumping to the valuation first — that's the clerk reflex. I'd start with
first principles: how does this business actually make money, who pays it, why do they keep
paying, and what would have to be true for that to continue? Then I'd climb the ladder
deliberately: pull the data, put it in context against peers (information), and only then push for
the insight — what does the crowd believe about this company that I might disagree with?
Where's the gap between the narrative and the numbers? Finally I'd form a judgment as a
probability, not a verdict, with a clear test for what would prove me wrong. The numbers come
last because they only mean something once I understand the business and the consensus
around it.

> Look for: Starts with business model / first principles, not valuation · Moves explicitly from data to insight to judgment · Looks for the variant view versus consensus · Frames the conclusion as a probability with a falsification test


### 3. Tell me about a time you changed your mind about something based on
evidence.
Behavioural question. The interviewer wants to see intellectual honesty and the ability to update — the
opposite of confirmation bias.

**Strong answer.** [Candidate gives a specific personal example.] The structure of a strong
answer: I held a view, I deliberately looked for the evidence that would prove me WRONG rather

than only what confirmed me, I found something that genuinely shifted the odds, and I updated
— even though it was uncomfortable and I'd already said the view out loud. The key is showing
that I treated my opinion as a probability to be revised, not an identity to be defended, and that I
actively sought disconfirming evidence instead of waiting for it to hit me.

> Look for: Gives a concrete, specific example · Shows active search for disconfirming evidence · Demonstrates comfort updating despite ego cost · Treats beliefs as revisable probabilities, not identity


### 4. A company you cover has a much higher P/E than its peers. Is it expensive?
Trick question. The naive answer is 'yes'. The interviewer is testing whether the candidate treats a ratio
as a verdict or a question.

**Strong answer.** I can't answer that from the P/E alone — a ratio is a question, not a verdict. A
high P/E is the market telling me it's paying up for something; my job is to find out what and
decide if it's real. Expensive relative to what runway, and for how long? If the premium reflects a
business that can compound durably with a structural edge — like a high-quality lender with
data-led underwriting and a long distribution runway — then the multiple might be the fair price
of a rare compounder, not 'expensive'. If it reflects a one-off earnings spike or pure narrative, it's
a trap. The number tells me where to look, not what to conclude.

> Look for: Refuses to call it expensive on the ratio alone · Treats the ratio as a prompt to investigate · Connects the multiple to durability/longevity of growth · Distinguishes a justified premium from a narrative trap


### 5. What's the difference between a good decision and a good outcome?
Conceptual question drawn from probabilistic thinking. Tests whether the candidate separates process
quality from result.

**Strong answer.** They're independent, and confusing them is one of the most expensive errors
in investing. A good decision is one made with a sound process given the information available —
the right reasoning, honest probabilities, appropriate sizing. A good outcome is just the result
that happened to occur. You can make a great decision and lose money because a
low-probability risk hit; you can make a terrible, lucky decision and make money. If you judge
your process only by outcomes, you'll learn the wrong lessons — punishing good thinking after
bad luck and rewarding recklessness after good luck. Elite investors grade the decision, not the
dice.

> Look for: Clearly separates process from result · Explains how outcome-only judging teaches wrong lessons · References probability/sizing · Shows this maps to how to evaluate one's own track record


### 6. How do you decide whether a piece of news actually matters for a stock you
cover?
Opinion question previewing the signal-vs-noise session. Tests filtering ability.

**Strong answer.** I ask one question first: does this change the thesis, or just the mood? Most
news moves prices without touching the long-term earnings power or the competitive position —
that's noise dressed up as signal. I separate the durable from the disposable: does it alter the
company's ability to make money over years, or is it a quarter of weather, a headline, or
positioning unwinding? I also ask who benefits from the story being told the way it is. If it
genuinely shifts the long-term economics or my probabilities, I update. If it's just narrative, I note
it and move on. The market's reaction and the news's actual importance are two different things.

> Look for: Distinguishes thesis-changing news from mood/noise · Focuses on durable earnings power / competitive position · Examines incentives behind the narrative · Separates the market's reaction from the news's real importance


### 7. You and the entire market read the same results. How could you possibly
have an edge?
Conceptual / pressure question. Tests whether the candidate understands where edge in an efficient
market actually comes from.


**Strong answer.** My edge can't be informational — everyone has the same filing, and a machine
reads it faster than all of us. My edge has to be interpretational: seeing the same facts one level
deeper. That's second-level thinking — not 'the results were weak' but 'the results were weak,
AND here's why the reason is temporary and the crowd is over-extrapolating it.' Edge comes
from a differentiated, correct view of what the shared facts mean — usually because I've
reasoned from first principles, traced the second-order effects, and weighed the probabilities
more honestly than a market that's anchored on the obvious first read.

> Look for: Recognises edge is interpretational, not informational · Articulates second-level thinking with an example structure · Connects to first-principles / second-order reasoning · Understands market efficiency and what beats it


### 8. Describe your thinking process when you're under time pressure and have
incomplete information — say, a surprise announcement before market open.
Behavioural / situational question previewing decision-making under uncertainty.

**Strong answer.** First I'd separate what I know from what I'm assuming, and resist the System-1
urge to react instantly to the headline. Then I'd ask what's actually thesis-relevant versus noise,
and what the base rate is for situations like this — how do surprises of this type usually play out?
I'd form a probabilistic view rather than wait for certainty I'll never get, size any action to my
conviction, and define in advance what would tell me I'm wrong. The goal isn't to be certain — it's
to make a high-quality decision with the information available and stay ready to update.
Decisiveness without recklessness.

> Look for: Separates known facts from assumptions · Resists impulsive System-1 reaction · Uses base rates and probabilistic reasoning · Acts decisively while pre-defining a falsification condition


### 9. What does it mean to 'think like a CEO' when you're analysing a company
from the outside?
Opinion question previewing the capital-allocator session.

**Strong answer.** It means judging management the way they should judge themselves: as
allocators of capital. Every rupee a company earns can be reinvested, used for buybacks, paid as
dividends, or spent on acquisitions — and each rupee has an opportunity cost. A CEO mindset
asks: is this management deploying capital into its highest-return uses, or empire-building for
ego? Are they reinvesting where returns on capital are high and returning cash when they're not?
Great long-term value creation is usually a story of ruthless, rational capital allocation — so from
the outside I'd evaluate the track record of those decisions, not just the quarterly P&L.

> Look for: Frames management as capital allocators · References ROIC / reinvestment / opportunity cost · Judges allocation decisions (buybacks, capex, M&A) rationally · Connects allocation skill to long-term value creation


### 10. Is there any part of an analyst's job you think AI genuinely can't take over?
Be specific.
Opinion / forward-looking question. Tests self-awareness about the profession's future — central to this
module's final session.

**Strong answer.** Yes — the top of the ladder. AI can synthesise what exists, but it doesn't decide
which question is worth asking in the first place, and framing the right question is most of the job.
It struggles with genuine judgment under ambiguity where the data is thin or contradictory and
someone has to take responsibility for a call. It has no skin in the game and no accountability.
And it lacks taste — the hard-won sense for which of ten plausible narratives is the one that
matters, and the conviction to hold a non-consensus view when the market screams you're
wrong. My job is to do the thinking, asking and deciding; AI's job is to be the fastest analyst I've
ever had working under me.

> Look for: Identifies judgment under ambiguity as the human moat · Highlights question-framing and synthesis/taste · Notes accountability/skin-in-the-game · Positions AI as leverage under human judgment, not a replacement


---

## Simulation — The 9 AM Call — Conflicting Signals Before the Open

*MEDIUM · 10 min · decision making*


**Scenario.** You're an analyst at a Mumbai fund. It's 8:45 AM, fifteen minutes before market
open. Overnight, a mid-cap Indian company you cover has released TWO pieces of news at
once. FIRST: strong Q3 results — revenue up 18% YoY, operating margin at a multi-year high,
and management guiding confidently for the year. SECOND: a one-line filing that the
long-tenured CFO has resigned 'to pursue other opportunities', effective immediately, with no
successor named. The stock is indicated sharply higher on the results in the pre-open. Your
PM walks over and says: 'Great print. Should we add to the position at the open? You've got
ten minutes. Tell me what you think and WHY.' This is a thinking test, not a numbers test —
there is no 'correct' buy/sell answer, only correct and incorrect reasoning.

**Your task.** Work through the decision the way an elite analyst would, out loud and in writing.
Do not react to the headline. (1) Separate what you KNOW from what you're ASSUMING. (2)
Apply second-order thinking to BOTH pieces of news — and especially to the two TOGETHER:
why might a strong-results print and an abrupt, unexplained CFO exit arrive on the same
night? (3) State your view as a PROBABILITY, not a verdict, and size your recommendation to
your conviction. (4) Define, in advance, exactly what information would tell you that you're
WRONG. (5) Name which thinking traps you are most at risk of in this exact moment. Write
your answer as a short reasoned note to your PM.

**Objectives**

- Resist the System-1 reaction to a strong headline and an indicated-higher stock
- Separate verified facts from assumptions under time pressure
- Apply second-order thinking to the combination of the two announcements
- Express a probabilistic, appropriately-sized view rather than a binary call
- Pre-commit to a falsification condition ('what would prove me wrong')
- Identify the cognitive traps most active in this scenario (narrative capture, recency, single-cause)
Decision points
- Do you treat the strong results and the CFO exit as independent, or ask why they coincided?
- Is the CFO departure noise (genuinely personal) or signal (something the numbers aren't showing)?
- How much weight do you put on results you can see versus a risk you can only infer?
- Do you act on the indicated pop, or does the price reaction itself tell you the crowd has only read the
first headline?

How a top analyst would think it through. An elite response does NOT say 'great
results, add.' It flags the central second-order question: a sudden, unexplained CFO
exit on the very night of a suspiciously strong print is a classic tension between the
narrative (great quarter) and a possible hidden problem (why is the person who signs
off on those numbers leaving immediately?). The strong move is to size DOWN or wait
— not because the results are necessarily fake, but because the risk is asymmetric
and the information is incomplete. It states a probability ('maybe a 60-70% chance it's
benign, but the tail risk if it's not is large'), defines what would resolve it (a credible
successor, an investor call addressing the exit, no restatement signals), and names
the traps: narrative capture (the seductive results story), single-cause thinking
(reading the pop as 'just results'), and recency. The lesson: when the story and a red
flag arrive together, the thinker investigates the flag before chasing the story.

Hints
- Ask: why would these two things happen on the SAME night? Coincidence is an assumption, not a
fact.

- The stock is indicated higher — but has the market read BOTH filings, or just the exciting one?
- Who signs off on the numbers you're being asked to trust? Now re-read who just resigned.
- You will never get certainty in 10 minutes. The job is a high-quality decision, not a certain one.


---

## Session 2 — First-Principles Thinking — Stripping a Business to Its Atoms
Reason up from fundamental truths instead of reasoning by analogy. The Musk/Bezos
method applied to Indian businesses: question every assumption, rebuild the logic from
scratch, and see what the consensus inherited without checking.


**In this session**

- Decompose a business into its irreducible economic drivers
- Separate inherited assumptions from verified truths
- Rebuild an investment view from first principles
- Use first-principles reasoning to find non-consensus insight


### [HOOK] The Rule That Was Never a Rule
2016. Every telecom analyst in the country 'knows' the rules of Indian mobile: data is a
scarce, premium product, average revenue per user has a hard floor, and you simply cannot
give data away and survive. It is repeated in every report, priced into every model, taught to
every junior. Then a new entrant ignores all of it. It does not ask 'what do telecom companies
usually charge?' It asks a different question entirely: 'what does one gigabyte of data actually
COST to deliver, once you have built fibre and 4G at national scale?' The answer suggested
the famous 'floor' was an industry habit, not a law of economics. The entrant priced data as
the near-zero-marginal-cost commodity it fundamentally was — and the entire market's
economics were rewritten. The analysts who reasoned 'telecom has always worked like this'
never saw it coming. The one who asked 'but WHY is that true?' did.

The question. How many of the 'rules' you believe about an industry are actual
laws — and how many are just things everyone repeats because everyone
repeats them?

Why it matters. First-principles thinking is the single most reliable engine for a
non-consensus view. Reasoning by analogy can only ever deliver you to the consensus,
because it copies what already exists. Reasoning up from fundamental truths is the only legal
way to see what the crowd structurally cannot. This session installs the engine.


### [CONCEPT] Reasoning Up vs Reasoning Across
There are two ways to reach a conclusion, and they lead to completely different places.
REASONING BY ANALOGY says 'this is like that, so it will behave like that' — you copy an
existing case and tweak it slightly. It is fast, safe, and it can NEVER beat the consensus,
because it IS the consensus: you are deriving your answer from what everyone already
believes. REASONING FROM FIRST PRINCIPLES does the opposite — it strips the problem
down to the fundamental truths you cannot break down any further (a law of physics, an
irreducible unit economic, an actual cause) and rebuilds the conclusion upward from there.
Aristotle defined a first principle as 'the first basis from which a thing is known'. Musk made it
famous for SpaceX: instead of 'rockets cost a fortune because they always have', he asked
'what is a rocket physically made of, what do those materials cost, and why is the finished
product fifty times that?' For an analyst the shift is identical: instead of 'this should trade at

the sector's 20x because the sector trades at 20x', ask 'what does THIS business actually
earn, how durable is it, and what is that fundamentally worth?' The first answer is a
hand-me-down. The second is a conclusion you own — and the only kind that can be
non-consensus.

Analogy. A cook who can only follow recipes can reproduce dishes that already
exist. A cook who understands the CHEMISTRY — why heat, acid, salt and fat do
what they do — can invent a dish no recipe has ever described. Reasoning by
analogy is cooking from recipes. First-principles thinking is understanding the
chemistry. One faithfully copies; the other can create something new.


**Key Terms**

First principle — A fundamental truth that cannot be reduced further and is not derived from
any other assumption.
Reasoning by analogy — Concluding something will behave like a similar existing thing, by
copying the precedent.
Reasoning from first principles — Building a conclusion up from fundamental truths instead
of from precedent or consensus.
Inherited assumption — A belief you hold because it was handed to you, not because you
ever verified it.
Non-consensus insight — A correct view the crowd does not hold — reachable only by NOT
copying the crowd.

CONCEPT
The First-Principles Drill — Four Cuts to the
Atom
First-principles thinking sounds abstract until you have a repeatable drill. Four cuts. CUT 1 —
STATE THE CLAIM. Write the belief you (or the market) hold in one plain sentence: 'Quick
commerce can never be profitable in India.' CUT 2 — ASK 'WHY' UNTIL YOU HIT BEDROCK.
Keep asking why the claim is true. 'Why? Because delivery costs more than the margin on a
basket. Why? Because each order ties up a rider for several minutes. Is that fixed? No — what
actually matters is contribution per order, which is driven by order density per store and
basket size.' Three or four whys and you usually reach a real VARIABLE, not a law. CUT 3 —
SEPARATE LAW FROM HABIT. Sort every reason into two buckets: things genuinely fixed (a
law of physics, a regulation, a contractual fact) and things that are merely how it has always
been done (industry habit, legacy cost structure, an unexamined assumption). The edge is
almost always hiding in the 'habit' bucket. CUT 4 — REBUILD UPWARD. Construct your
conclusion using ONLY the genuine laws, treating every habit as a variable you are free to
question. The view you build will often differ from the consensus — and that difference is
your insight. Run this drill on any thesis, any business model, any 'everyone knows', and you
can manufacture non-consensus views on demand.

Analogy. Demolishing a building down to its foundation before rebuilding.
Reasoning by analogy merely renovates the existing structure — same
load-bearing walls, same inherited flaws. First-principles thinking tears down to
the bedrock, keeps only what is structurally necessary, and rebuilds. You end up
with a different building precisely because you refused to inherit the old floor
plan.


**Key Terms**


The 'five whys' — Asking why repeatedly to drill past surface reasons to a root variable or law.
Bedrock truth — The point where 'why' stops because you have hit something genuinely
irreducible.
Law vs habit — Distinguishing what is actually fixed from what is merely customary and
therefore changeable.
Variable — Anything in the 'habit' bucket that you can question, change, or model differently.
Rebuild upward — Constructing a fresh conclusion from verified truths rather than from
precedent.


### [VISUAL] Two Paths to a Conclusion
Visual: fork diagram
Draw one question at the top: 'What is this business worth — and will it work?' Two arrows
fork downward. The LEFT arrow, labelled REASONING BY ANALOGY, is short and flat: it hops
sideways to 'the sector trades at 20x, so this should too' and lands in a box labelled
CONSENSUS — shaded grey, crowded with stick figures all standing together. The RIGHT
arrow, labelled FIRST PRINCIPLES, is longer and dives DOWN through three layers — 'what
does it actually earn?' then 'how durable and long is that?' then 'what is that cash stream
fundamentally worth?' — before climbing back up to a box labelled YOUR OWN CONCLUSION,
shaded bright, with a single figure standing apart from the crowd. Caption underneath: 'The
left path is faster and always arrives where everyone else already is. The right path is slower
and is the only one that can arrive somewhere new.'
Elements
- TOP — the question: 'What is this worth / will this work?'
- LEFT PATH (analogy): hops sideways to the nearest precedent, inherits its answer, lands in
the crowded CONSENSUS box.
- RIGHT PATH (first principles): drills DOWN to fundamental truths, then rebuilds UP to an
independent conclusion.
- DEPTH MARKERS on the right path: earnings reality -> durability/longevity -> intrinsic
worth.
- KEY CONTRAST: analogy can only reach where others already stand; first principles is the
only route to a non-consensus view.
- WARNING label: the right path is slower and lonelier — that discomfort is the price of edge.


### [DEEP DIVE] First Principles in the Wild

Musk and the cost of a rocket
The canonical modern case. The aerospace industry 'knew' rockets cost hundreds of millions
because they always had. Musk reasoned from first principles: a rocket is mostly aluminium
alloys, titanium, copper and carbon fibre — he priced the raw materials and found they were
a tiny fraction of the finished rocket's price. The conclusion: the cost lived in the
manufacturing process and the assumption of disposability, not in the physics. SpaceX rebuilt
from that bedrock with reusable boosters and collapsed launch costs. The lesson for an
analyst is not about rockets — it is that 'the price has always been X' is never a fundamental
truth. It is an invitation to ask why.

Jio and the floor under data
An India-first parallel. For years the telecom consensus treated mobile data as a scarce
premium good with a hard revenue floor — because that is simply how the industry had

always priced it. A first-principles view asked a different question: what does a gigabyte of
data actually cost to deliver, once national fibre and 4G exist at massive scale? The answer
suggested the 'floor' was an industry habit, not a law of economics. The entrant priced data
as the near-zero-marginal-cost commodity it fundamentally was, and the market's entire
economics were rewritten. Analysts reasoning by analogy ('telecom margins work like this')
were structurally blind to it; only first-principles reasoning could see it coming.

How to manufacture a non-consensus view
Insight — Rung 3 of last session's ladder — is not luck. It is the OUTPUT of first-principles
thinking. The procedure: take any widely held belief about a company or sector, drill it with
'why' until you have separated the genuine laws from the inherited habits, then ask whether
the market has mistaken a habit for a law. When it has — when everyone is pricing in an
assumption nobody has re-checked — you have found a legal, repeatable edge. This is the
engine behind almost every great non-consensus call: not better data, but the willingness to
re-derive what the crowd merely inherited.

The discipline and its danger
First-principles thinking is powerful but expensive. It is slow, effortful (pure System 2), and
socially uncomfortable, because your conclusion will often disagree with smart people you
respect. It also has a real failure mode — re-deriving things that are genuinely settled, and
discarding accumulated wisdom out of arrogance ('I will reinvent accounting from scratch').
The master's skill is selective application: inherit what is truly bedrock (double-entry works,
cash flow is cash flow) and spend your scarce 'why' energy on the assumptions everyone has
STOPPED questioning. Knowing which is which is the whole game.
Real-world references
- Elon Musk / SpaceX — the rocket raw-materials cost argument, the popular case study in
first-principles reasoning.
- Aristotle — the original idea of a 'first principle' as the first basis from which a thing is known.
- Reliance Jio — the Indian telecom data-pricing disruption as a first-principles re-derivation of unit
economics.
- Naval Ravikant — widely-shared articulations of first-principles thinking and questioning inherited
beliefs.
- Howard Marks (Oaktree) — 'second-level thinking', the cousin discipline that turns first-principles
insight into a non-consensus call.


### [EXAMPLE] Re-deriving a 'Boring' Business
Company / context: Asian Paints (NSE: ASIANPAINT)
Situation. A common first reaction to a paint company is reasoning by analogy: 'paint is a
commodity — raw materials in, coloured liquid out, cyclical with construction, nothing
special. It shouldn't command a premium multiple.' By analogy to other commodity
manufacturers, the business looks ordinary and the rich valuation looks unjustified.
Data. The visible facts the analogy quietly ignores: an enormous dealer network built over
decades; in-store tinting machines that lock dealers in and let customers mix thousands of
shades on demand; a supply chain engineered to restock tens of thousands of outlets reliably
and frequently; and a brand homeowners ask for by name in a category they buy rarely and
badly want to get right. The returns on capital have been unusually high and remarkably
stable for something supposedly 'commodity'.

Analysis. First-principles thinking refuses the 'it's a commodity' analogy and asks what the
business FUNDAMENTALLY is. Drill with 'why': why does it earn such steady, high returns if
paint is a commodity? Because the moat is not the paint — it is the DISTRIBUTION and the
dealer lock-in. Why can't a rival simply match it? Because that network and the
tinting-machine install base took decades and enormous capital to build, and a rival would
need just as long. So the irreducible truth is this: it is not a paint company that happens to
have distribution — it is a distribution-and-supply-chain machine that happens to sell paint.
Once you rebuild the view from THAT atom, the premium multiple that baffled the
commodity-thinker becomes obvious: you are paying for a near-unassailable distribution
moat, not for coloured liquid. The analogy-thinker mispriced the business because they filed
it under the wrong category.
Analyst takeaway. When the consensus dismisses a business with a lazy analogy — 'it's
just a commodity / just a retailer / just an NBFC' — that lazy category is often exactly where
the opportunity hides. Drill to what the business fundamentally IS and where its returns
actually come from. The moat usually sits in a layer the analogy never bothers to look at.
(This is a teaching illustration of a thinking pattern, not a stock recommendation, and
hindsight flatters every example.)

PITFALLS         How First-Principles Thinking Goes Wrong

### 1. Calling lazy contrarianism 'first principles' — disagreeing with the consensus
just to feel original.
Why it happens: First-principles reasoning often LANDS on a non-consensus view, so beginners invert
the logic and assume that being contrarian IS the skill. They start from 'the crowd is wrong' and reason
backwards to justify it.
Fix: First principles is a PROCESS, not a conclusion. You must actually drill to the fundamental truths
and rebuild — and sometimes the honest rebuild CONFIRMS the consensus. If your 'first-principles view'
always conveniently disagrees with everyone, you are not reasoning, you are posturing.


### 2. Stopping the 'why' chain too early — mistaking a surface reason for bedrock.
Why it happens: Asking 'why' is uncomfortable and the first plausible-sounding answer feels like
enough. People stop at 'because the sector trades at 20x' instead of pushing to why THAT is true.
Fix: Force at least three or four 'whys' on any important claim. You have not hit bedrock until the
answer is a genuine law, a regulation, a contractual fact, or an irreducible unit economic — not just
another inherited belief wearing the costume of a reason.


### 3. Re-deriving everything from scratch, including things that are genuinely
settled.
Why it happens: Intoxicated by the method, people start distrusting all accumulated knowledge and try
to reinvent accounting, valuation or basic finance from zero — which is arrogant and burns enormous
time for no payoff.
Fix: Apply first-principles thinking selectively. Inherit the true bedrock (double-entry works, cash is
cash, compounding is real) and spend your scarce 'why' energy only on the assumptions everyone has
STOPPED questioning. Wisdom is knowing which is which.


### 4. Overriding strong base rates and lived evidence because 'I reasoned it from first
principles'.
Why it happens: A clean logical derivation feels more trustworthy than messy real-world data, so people
let an elegant chain of reasoning beat decades of evidence.
Fix: Use first principles to GENERATE a hypothesis, then check it against reality and base rates before
betting. A beautiful derivation that contradicts the weight of evidence is more likely to be wrong than
the evidence is. Reason from first principles; verify against the world.


### [RECAP] First Principles — Locking It In

**Key Takeaways**

- Reasoning by analogy copies the consensus; reasoning from first principles is the only
path to a non-consensus view.
- A first principle is a fundamental truth you cannot reduce further — a law, a regulation, an
irreducible unit economic.
- Run the four-cut drill: state the claim, ask 'why' to bedrock, separate law from habit,
rebuild upward.
- The edge almost always hides in the 'habit' bucket — something everyone treats as fixed
that is actually a variable.
- Insight (Rung 3) is the OUTPUT of first-principles thinking, not luck — you can manufacture
it on demand.
- Apply it selectively: inherit genuine bedrock, re-derive only what the crowd has stopped
questioning.
- First principles GENERATES a hypothesis; always verify it against reality and base rates
before you bet.

Analyst mindset. The first-principles thinker hears 'everyone knows X' not as
an answer but as a question: 'do they actually know it, or have they just
inherited it?' They are willing to be slower, lonelier and occasionally wrong in
order to occasionally see what no one else can. That willingness — to re-derive
what others merely repeat — is the source of almost every genuinely original
call in finance.


**Connections**

- Session 1 (The Analyst's Edge) — first-principles thinking is the engine that produces Rung-3 insight.
- Session 3 (Second-Order Thinking) — once you have re-derived the truth, trace its downstream
consequences.
- Session 8 (Signal vs Noise) — drilling 'why' is how you separate a real driver from a narrative.
- Session 10 (Debugging Your Brain) — inherited assumptions are a bias; first principles is a debiasing
tool.

## Quiz

**Q1. *[EASY]*** What does 'reasoning by analogy' mean?

- A. Building a conclusion up from fundamental truths
- **✓ B. Concluding something will behave like a similar existing thing, by copying it**
- C. Asking 'why' until you reach bedrock
- D. Disagreeing with the consensus on principle

> Why: Reasoning by analogy concludes that something will behave like a similar case by copying the existing example. Because it copies what already exists, it can only ever arrive at the consensus.


**Q2. *[EASY]*** A first principle is best described as:

- A. The most popular view in a sector
- **✓ B. A fundamental truth that cannot be reduced further**
- C. Any reason that sounds convincing
- D. The opposite of whatever the crowd believes


> Why: A first principle is an irreducible fundamental truth — a law, a regulation, or a basic unit economic — not derived from any other assumption.


**Q3. *[EASY]*** In the four-cut drill, what is the purpose of asking 'why'

repeatedly?
- A. To sound sceptical in meetings
- **✓ B. To drill past surface reasons to a genuine root variable or law**
- C. To disagree with management
- D. To deliberately slow down every decision

> Why: Repeated 'why' (the five-whys technique) pushes you past inherited surface reasons until you hit bedrock — an actual law or irreducible variable rather than another unexamined belief.


**Q4. *[MEDIUM]*** The market says 'this NBFC should trade at 20x because the

sector trades at 20x.' Which type of reasoning is that, and what
is its core limitation?
- A. First principles; it is too slow
- **✓ B. Analogy; it can only ever arrive at the consensus**
- C. First principles; it ignores base rates
- D. Analogy; it is always wrong

> Why: Pegging a stock to the sector multiple because the sector trades there is reasoning by analogy. Its structural limitation is that copying the consensus can never produce a non-consensus view — not that it is always wrong.


**Q5. *[MEDIUM]*** In the four-cut drill, why is the 'law vs habit' separation the

most valuable step?
- A. Because laws are easier to model than habits
- **✓ B. Because the edge almost always hides in something everyone treats as fixed but is actually a variable**
- C. Because habits are always wrong
- D. Because regulators only care about laws

> Why: Sorting reasons into genuine laws versus mere habits reveals where the crowd has mistaken a changeable variable for a fixed constraint — and that gap is where non-consensus insight lives.


**Q6. *[MEDIUM]*** A student says: 'I always take the opposite of what the

consensus believes — that's first-principles thinking.' What is
wrong with this?
- A. Nothing, that is exactly right
- **✓ B. First principles is a process of re-deriving truth, which sometimes confirms the consensus; reflexive contrarianism is not reasoning**
- C. Contrarianism is always more profitable
- D. The consensus is never wrong

> Why: First-principles thinking is a process, not a conclusion. Done honestly, the rebuild sometimes agrees with the crowd. Always disagreeing is posturing, not reasoning from fundamentals.


**Q7. *[MEDIUM]*** How does first-principles thinking connect to 'insight' (Rung

3) from Session 1?
- A. They are unrelated
- **✓ B. Insight is the OUTPUT of first-principles thinking — re-deriving what the crowd inherited is how you manufacture a non-obvious view**
- C. First principles replaces the need for insight
- D. Insight comes only from having better data


> Why: Rung-3 insight is not luck; it is produced by drilling a consensus belief to its fundamentals and finding where a habit has been mistaken for a law. First-principles thinking is the engine that generates insight.


**Q8. *[MEDIUM]*** The aerospace industry believed rockets must cost hundreds of millions 'because they always had.' What was the first-principles move that challenged this?
- A. Asking competitors to lower their prices
- **✓ B. Pricing the raw materials and finding the finished product cost far more than its physical inputs**
- C. Assuming the consensus was simply wrong
- D. Building a cheaper rocket by analogy to aircraft

> Why: The first-principles move was to decompose the rocket into raw materials, price those, and discover the cost lived in the process and the disposability assumption — not in the physics. 'It has always cost X' was exposed as a habit, not a law.


**Q9. *[HARD]*** A business everyone dismisses as 'just a commodity

manufacturer' earns unusually steady, high returns on capital. A
first-principles analyst should conclude:
- A. The high returns must be an accounting illusion
- **✓ B. The 'commodity' category is probably the wrong atom — the real moat likely sits in a layer the analogy never examines, such as distribution**
- C. Commodities cannot earn high returns, so the data must be wrong
- D. The market is efficient, so there is nothing to find

> Why: Persistently high returns contradict the 'commodity' label, signalling the analogy has filed the business under the wrong category. The first-principles response is to re-derive what the business fundamentally is and locate the true source of returns — often distribution or lock-in the lazy category ignores.


**Q10. *[HARD]*** Which is the BEST description of how to apply

first-principles thinking WITHOUT falling into its failure modes?
- A. Re-derive absolutely everything from scratch, trusting no prior knowledge
- **✓ B. Use it to generate a hypothesis, inherit genuine bedrock, re-derive only what the crowd has stopped questioning, and verify against base rates**
- C. Only ever use it to confirm the consensus
- D. Apply it once and never revisit the conclusion

> Why: Skilled application is selective: inherit settled bedrock, spend scarce 'why' energy on unexamined assumptions, treat the derivation as a hypothesis, and check it against real-world evidence and base rates before betting. Re-deriving everything is arrogant and wasteful; using it only to confirm the crowd wastes its power.


---

## Interview Questions

### 1. Explain the difference between reasoning by analogy and reasoning from
first principles, with an example.
Conceptual question testing whether the candidate understands the core distinction and can ground it
concretely.

**Strong answer.** Reasoning by analogy means concluding something will behave like a similar
existing thing — 'this NBFC should trade at the sector's 20x because that's what NBFCs trade at.'
It's fast and safe but it can only ever land you where the consensus already is, because you're
copying the existing case. Reasoning from first principles means stripping the problem to
fundamental truths you can't reduce further and rebuilding the conclusion up from there —
instead of pegging to the sector multiple, I'd ask what this specific business actually earns, how

durable that is, and what that cash stream is fundamentally worth. The analogy hands you an
inherited answer; first principles produces a conclusion you own. The classic example is Musk
pricing a rocket's raw materials and realising the cost was in the disposability assumption, not
the physics — 'it's always cost this much' turned out to be a habit, not a law.

> Look for: Clearly distinguishes copying a precedent from re-deriving from fundamentals · Explains why analogy cannot beat the consensus · Grounds the distinction in a concrete example · Connects first principles to owning an independent conclusion


### 2. Walk me through the practical steps you'd use to apply first-principles
thinking to a belief you hold about a company.
Tests whether the candidate has an actual repeatable procedure rather than just the vocabulary.

**Strong answer.** I'd run a simple drill. First, state the belief in one plain sentence — say 'quick
commerce can't be profitable in India.' Second, ask 'why' repeatedly until I hit bedrock: why isn't
it profitable? Delivery costs exceed margin. Why? Each order ties up a rider. Is that fixed? No —
it's really about contribution per order, driven by order density and basket size, which depend on
dark-store placement and maturity. Third, I'd sort every reason into 'genuine law' versus 'just
how it's done today' — and the edge almost always hides in the habit bucket. Fourth, I'd rebuild
my conclusion using only the real laws, treating each habit as a variable I can question. If the
rebuilt view differs from the market's, I've found a candidate insight — which I'd then test against
base rates and evidence before acting on it.

> Look for: Lays out a concrete, repeatable procedure · Includes drilling 'why' down to a root variable · Separates genuine constraints from inherited habits · Treats the output as a hypothesis to verify, not a final answer


### 3. Most investors reason by analogy. If first-principles thinking is so powerful,
why doesn't everyone do it?
Opinion question probing whether the candidate understands the real-world costs and frictions of the
method.

**Strong answer.** Because it's expensive in ways people underrate. It's slow and effortful — it
forces System-2 work when System-1 analogies feel good enough. It's socially uncomfortable:
your conclusion will often disagree with smart, senior people you respect, and being the lone
dissenter is hard. And it carries career risk — being contrarian and wrong looks worse than being
conventionally wrong with the crowd. So most people default to analogy not because they're
stupid but because analogy is faster, safer and more comfortable. That's exactly why
first-principles thinking stays a durable edge: if it were easy and comfortable everyone would do
it and the edge would vanish. The discomfort is the moat.

> Look for: Identifies concrete costs — time, effort, social and career risk · Recognises analogy as a rational default, not mere laziness · Connects the difficulty to why the edge persists · Shows mature understanding of real-world incentives


### 4. Tell me a non-consensus view you hold, derived from first principles.
Trick / pressure question. A weak candidate blurts a hot take; a strong one demonstrates the PROCESS
and is honest about confidence. The reasoning matters more than the specific view.

**Strong answer.** I'll give you one, but let me show the derivation rather than just the headline,
because the view is only as good as the reasoning behind it. I'd state the consensus belief, drill
'why' three or four times, separate the genuine constraints from the inherited habits, identify the
specific habit the market has mistaken for a law, and rebuild the conclusion from there. And I'd
hold it with calibrated confidence — maybe a 60-65% chance I'm right — with a clear test for
what would prove me wrong. Honestly, I'd be suspicious of myself if my 'first-principles view' just
happened to be edgy; the point is the re-derivation, not the contrarianism, and sometimes the
drill brings me right back to the consensus.

> Look for: Demonstrates the process, not just a hot take · Drills to a specific habit-mistaken-for-law · Expresses calibrated rather than absolute confidence · Guards against the trap of contrarianism-as-posturing


### 5. Describe a time you questioned an assumption that everyone around you
accepted. What happened?
Behavioural question assessing whether the candidate actually practises first-principles thinking or just
knows the theory.

**Strong answer.** The key is that I didn't reject the assumption to be different — I asked why it
was true. In my case, everyone accepted it because that's how it had always been done. When I
drilled into it, I found the original reason no longer held — it was a habit left over from older
conditions. I rebuilt the approach from what was actually true now, presented the reasoning
rather than just the conclusion so people could check my logic, and stayed open to being wrong.
Sometimes the re-derivation confirms the status quo and I say so; this time it didn't, and the
change worked out. What I learned is that the value isn't in being contrarian — it's in being
willing to re-check what others have stopped questioning, and to show your working.

> Look for: Gives a concrete, specific example · Shows the questioning was process-driven, not reflexive contrarianism · Demonstrates showing reasoning and openness to being wrong · Reflects on the lesson maturely


### 6. What's the relationship between first-principles thinking and finding an
analytical edge or 'insight'?
Tests whether the candidate connects this session's tool to the broader goal of generating edge.

**Strong answer.** They're cause and effect. An edge means holding a correct view the crowd
doesn't — and you can't get there by copying the crowd, which is all analogy can do.
First-principles thinking is the engine that manufactures that edge: take a belief everyone holds,
drill it to fundamentals, and look for the place where the market has mistaken an inherited habit
for a fixed law. When you find that gap — everyone pricing in an assumption nobody has
re-checked — you've located a legal, repeatable source of non-consensus insight. So insight isn't
luck or better data; it's the output of being willing to re-derive what others inherited. That's why I
treat 'everyone knows X' as a starting question, not an answer.

> Look for: Frames insight as the output of the method · Explains why analogy cannot produce edge · Identifies the habit-mistaken-for-law as the locus of edge · Connects to repeatability rather than one-off luck


### 7. When should you NOT use first-principles thinking?
Opinion / judgment question testing whether the candidate understands the method's limits — a sign of
maturity.

**Strong answer.** When the thing is genuinely settled bedrock, or when the cost of re-deriving
outweighs the payoff. I'm not going to reinvent double-entry accounting or re-derive that cash
flow is cash flow — that's arrogant and burns scarce energy. The method is expensive, so I
reserve it for the assumptions that actually matter AND that everyone has stopped questioning.
I'd also be careful using it to override strong base rates: a clean logical chain feels trustworthy,
but if my elegant derivation contradicts decades of real-world evidence, the evidence is usually
right and my chain has a hidden flaw. So the rule is: inherit genuine bedrock, re-derive the
high-stakes unexamined assumptions, and always check the derivation against reality before
betting on it.

> Look for: Recognises settled bedrock should be inherited, not re-derived · Notes the method's cost demands selective use · Flags the danger of overriding base rates with elegant logic · Shows judgment about where the tool adds value


### 8. 'I reasoned this entirely from first principles, so it must be right.' Do you
agree?
Trick question. The candidate should NOT defer to the phrase 'first principles' as a guarantee of
correctness.

**Strong answer.** No — 'I reasoned it from first principles' isn't a guarantee of being right, and
treating it as one is dangerous. The method is only as good as two things: whether you actually

hit genuine bedrock or just stopped at a surface reason that felt deep, and whether your rebuild
is logically sound. People often stop the 'why' chain too early and mistake an inherited belief for a
fundamental truth. And even a flawless derivation is just a hypothesis until it's checked against
reality and base rates. So I'd want to see the actual chain — where did 'why' stop, and why is that
bedrock? — and I'd want it tested against evidence. The method generates strong candidate
views; it doesn't certify them.

> Look for: Refuses to treat 'first principles' as a guarantee · Identifies stopping the 'why' chain too early as a failure mode · Notes a derivation is a hypothesis needing verification · Asks to inspect the actual reasoning chain


### 9. A company everyone calls 'just a commodity business' earns consistently
high returns on capital. How would you think about that?
Applied conceptual question testing whether the candidate can use first-principles thinking to resolve
an apparent contradiction.

**Strong answer.** The contradiction itself is the clue. If it were truly a commodity business it
shouldn't earn persistently high, stable returns — commodities get competed down. So the
'commodity' label is probably the wrong atom; the analogy has filed the business under the
wrong category. I'd drill into why the returns persist: what stops a competitor from eroding them?
Usually the answer is a moat sitting in a layer the 'commodity' view never looks at — distribution
reach, dealer lock-in, switching costs, a brand in a high-stakes infrequent purchase. Once I
re-derive what the business fundamentally is — say a distribution machine that happens to sell a
commodity product — the high returns and any premium multiple stop being a puzzle. The lesson
is that when the consensus dismisses something with a lazy category, that category is often
exactly where the opportunity is hiding.

> Look for: Treats the contradiction as a signal the category is wrong · Drills into the source and durability of the returns · Locates the moat in a layer the analogy ignores · Re-derives what the business fundamentally is


### 10. Does AI make first-principles thinking more or less valuable for an analyst?
Forward-looking opinion question tying the session to the module's overarching AI theme.

**Strong answer.** More valuable, clearly. AI is extraordinary at reasoning by analogy at scale —
it's trained on the entire corpus of what's already been said, so it's essentially a
consensus-synthesis engine. It'll give you the received wisdom faster and more comprehensively
than any human. But that's exactly the bottom of the analytical ladder. What AI structurally
struggles with is re-deriving a domain from fundamentals and arriving somewhere the training
data hasn't already been — which is precisely what first-principles thinking does. So as AI
commoditises analogy-based reasoning, the human edge concentrates even harder in the ability
to question inherited assumptions and rebuild from atoms. I'd actually use AI as a tool here — let
it surface the full consensus fast so I know exactly what I'm drilling against — and then do the
first-principles work it can't, to find where that consensus is wrong.

> Look for: Argues value increases, with clear reasoning · Characterises AI as a consensus / analogy engine · Identifies re-derivation as the human edge AI lacks · Positions AI as a tool that sharpens first-principles work


---

## Simulation — The Inherited 'Law' — Re-deriving a Broken Business
Model

*HARD · 10 min · decision making*


**Scenario.** You're an analyst at a Mumbai fund covering Indian consumer-tech. There is one
belief every single analyst on the street holds about the quick-commerce company you
cover: 'The model is structurally unprofitable — delivery costs always exceed the margin on a
basket, the unit economics simply don't work, and they never will.' The stock trades cheap

because the market has priced in permanent losses. Last night the company reported Q3:
another consolidated net loss, roughly in line with the gloomy consensus. On the surface, it
confirms exactly what everyone has always said. But buried in the disclosures is a detail
almost no one has flagged: the company has started reporting contribution margin by store
cohort, and the OLDEST cohort of dark stores — those open more than two years — has
crossed into POSITIVE contribution margin per order. The blended company-level number is
still negative only because a wave of newly opened stores (which always lose money early) is
dragging the average down. Order density per mature store and average basket size are
both rising quarter on quarter. Your PM glances at the headline loss and says: 'Another loss.
The model's broken, exactly like we always said. Don't waste your time on it.' You have ten
minutes before the morning meeting to decide whether you agree.

**Your task.** Do NOT simply accept the inherited consensus — and do NOT reflexively reject it
either. Re-derive it. (1) State the consensus belief as a single claim, then drill it with 'why':
WHY are the unit economics 'broken'? Push until you reach an actual variable, not a slogan.
(2) Separate genuine LAW from inherited HABIT: is 'delivery cost exceeds margin' a fixed law
of this business, or a function of variables — order density, basket size, store maturity — that
are visibly changing? (3) Decide what the cohort data fundamentally tells you that the
blended headline hides. (4) Form a PROBABILISTIC view — is the consensus mistaking a
maturation / mix effect for a structural law? — and state what specific future evidence would
confirm or kill your thesis. (5) Name the thinking traps both you and your PM are most
exposed to here. Write a short reasoned note for the morning meeting.

**Objectives**

- Resist accepting an inherited consensus belief without re-deriving it
- Drill the 'why' on 'broken unit economics' down to actual variables
- Separate a genuine structural law from a changing variable (density, basket, maturity)
- Read what the cohort data reveals that the blended headline conceals
- Express a probabilistic thesis with a clear falsification condition
- Identify the cognitive traps — anchoring on consensus, single-number thinking, confirmation bias
Decision points
- Do you accept the consensus 'law' that the model can't work, or drill into WHY it supposedly can't?
- Is 'delivery cost exceeds margin' a fixed structural truth, or a function of variables that are visibly
improving?
- Does the blended loss describe the BUSINESS, or just the current MIX of mature and immature
stores?
- If mature stores are already profitable per order, what does the company look like once today's new
stores mature?

How a top analyst would think it through. A first-principles response does NOT
echo 'another loss, model's broken' — that's reasoning by analogy from the
consensus. It drills the claim: WHY are the unit economics broken? Because per-order
delivery cost exceeds basket margin. WHY? Because each order consumes rider time.
Is that fixed? No — what matters is contribution per order, driven by order density per
store and basket size, both of which RISE as a store matures. That reframes the
question entirely: 'is the model broken?' becomes 'is the model STRUCTURALLY
broken, or just IMMATURE in the current store mix?' The cohort disclosure is the tell:
the oldest stores are already contribution-positive, so the blended loss is a MIX effect
(new stores dragging the average), not proof of a structural law. The consensus has
mistaken a maturation / mix dynamic — a variable — for a permanent law. The strong
analyst forms a probabilistic thesis ('maybe a 60-65% chance the unit economics are
genuinely maturing and the market is mispricing a mix effect as a structural flaw') and
pre-commits to what would prove it wrong: if older cohorts roll back below positive
contribution, if density and basket plateau, or if the company quietly STOPS disclosing
cohort data (a red flag). The traps to name: anchoring on the consensus 'everyone
knows it can't work', single-number thinking (reading only the blended headline),
confirmation bias (the PM seeing the loss he expected), and narrative capture. The
lesson: when 'everyone knows' a business can't work, drill whether that's a law or an
inherited habit — the cheap price exists precisely because no one re-checked.

Hints
- 'Everyone knows it can't work' is a claim to drill, not a fact to inherit. Ask why — three times.
- The blended loss mixes mature and brand-new stores. What is it actually measuring?
- If the oldest stores are already profitable per order, what happens as today's new stores age?
- Separate the law from the habit: is delivery-cost-exceeds-margin fixed, or a function of variables that
are changing?
- You won't get certainty in 10 minutes — the job is to spot whether a variable has been mistaken for a
law.


---

## Session 3 — Second- and Third-Order Thinking — Seeing Around Corners
Howard Marks' second-level thinking. Everyone sees the first consequence; edge lives in
the consequence of the consequence. Map ripple effects across a company, a sector and
the macro, and find the move the crowd has not priced.


**In this session**

- Trace second- and third-order effects of an event
- Distinguish first-level from second-level thinking
- Identify what is already priced in versus what is not
- Apply 'and then what?' to news and management actions


### [HOOK] And Then What?
A large government incentive scheme is announced for a manufacturing sector — generous,
multi-year, demand all but guaranteed. The screens light up green and every analyst on the
street rushes to upgrade the obvious beneficiaries: 'Government money is pouring in,
demand is locked, BUY the leaders.' One analyst doesn't touch the obvious names. She asks
