## Session 6 — Inversion & the Pre-Mortem — Thinking Backwards to Win
'All I want to know is where I'm going to die, so I'll never go there.' Solve problems by
asking how they fail. Run a pre-mortem on every thesis: assume it blew up, then explain
why.


**In this session**

- Apply inversion to investment and business problems
- Run a structured pre-mortem on a thesis
- Build a falsification test ('what would prove me wrong?')
- Use inversion to surface hidden risks and red flags


### [HOOK] Imagine It Already Failed
An investment committee is an hour into building the case for a large, concentrated position.
The energy is all forward-looking and bullish: here's how earnings double, here's the
re-rating, here's the path to a triple. Every voice is adding another reason it works. Then the
most experienced investor in the room stops the meeting and asks for the opposite. 'Forget
how it succeeds. Assume it's two years from now and this position has cratered — lost us
seventy percent. Walk me through exactly what happened.' The mood shifts. Forced to
explain the failure, the team surfaces things the bull case had quietly stepped around: a
single customer who is forty percent of revenue and is building in-house; a balance sheet
that only survives if refinancing stays cheap; a founder whose incentives reward swinging for
the fences with other people's money. None of it was hidden — but the forward-looking,
optimistic framing had made it invisible. By imagining the failure first, they found the fatal
flaw before it cost them, not after. They passed on the position. Eighteen months later, the
big customer left.

The question. When you build a case for a decision, you naturally ask 'how
does this succeed?' But the more powerful question — the one almost nobody
asks — is its mirror image: 'how does this fail?' Why is it so much easier to spot
the flaw when you look at the problem backwards?

Why it matters. Some of the most powerful thinking is done in reverse. 'Invert, always
invert' is, in Munger's view, one of the most underused tools in all of analysis — because
avoiding the ways to fail is often easier, and more valuable, than engineering the ways to
succeed. This session is the discipline of thinking backwards: inversion, the pre-mortem, kill
criteria, and the deliberate hunt for evidence that you're wrong.


### [CONCEPT] Invert, Always Invert
The mathematician Carl Jacobi solved hard problems with a two-word maxim: 'invert, always
invert' (man muss immer umkehren). Charlie Munger made it a cornerstone of his thinking.
The idea is that many problems are far easier to solve backwards than forwards. Instead of
asking 'how do I succeed?', ask 'how do I FAIL?' — list every way the thing could go wrong —
and then simply avoid those things. Munger's famous example: asked how to help India, he
answered by inverting — instead of asking what would help, he asked what would

GUARANTEE India stayed poor, listed those things, and said 'now avoid all of that.' The power
of inversion comes from two facts. First, it is usually easier to identify what to AVOID than
what to do: there are a few clear ways to ruin a marriage, a career, or a portfolio, and
steering clear of them gets you most of the way. Second, inversion bypasses the optimism
and confirmation bias baked into forward thinking — when you're building a case FOR
something, your mind recruits supporting evidence and waves away problems; when you
deliberately try to make it FAIL, those same problems suddenly become visible. This connects
to one of Munger's deepest principles: 'It is remarkable how much long-term advantage
people like us have gotten by trying to be consistently not stupid, instead of trying to be very
intelligent.' In investing especially, avoiding the catastrophic mistakes — the zeroes —
matters more than catching every winner, because one ruinous loss can wipe out a decade of
good decisions. Inversion is the engine of not-being-stupid.

Analogy. A good safety engineer doesn't design a bridge by asking 'how do I
make it stand?' She asks 'how could it collapse?' — wind, overload, metal
fatigue, foundation failure, resonance — and then engineers out every one of
those failure modes. The bridge stands not because she pursued standing, but
because she relentlessly eliminated falling. Building a robust thesis, or a robust
career, works the same way: you get to success by systematically removing the
ways to fail, not just by chasing the ways to win.


**Key Terms**

Inversion — Solving a problem backwards: asking how to fail (then avoiding it) instead of how
to succeed.
'Invert, always invert' — Jacobi's maxim, adopted by Munger, for attacking problems from the
reverse direction.
Avoiding stupidity — Munger's principle that consistently sidestepping big errors beats
chasing brilliance.
Confirmation bias — The mind's tendency to seek evidence FOR a view and ignore evidence
against it.
Asymmetry of ruin — One catastrophic loss can erase many good outcomes, so avoiding it is
paramount.

CONCEPT
The Pre-Mortem, Kill Criteria & the Hunt for
Disconfirmation
Inversion becomes a daily practice through three tools. TOOL 1 — THE PRE-MORTEM (from
psychologist Gary Klein). Before you commit to a decision, run a thought experiment:
imagine it is a year or two from now and the decision has FAILED — not 'might fail', but has
definitively, embarrassingly failed. Now, as a group or alone, write the story of WHY. Because
you're treating the failure as a fact rather than a possibility, people voice doubts they'd
otherwise suppress (no one wants to be the lone pessimist before a decision, but everyone is
happy to explain a 'failure' that already 'happened'). The pre-mortem surfaces risks that a
normal 'what are the risks?' discussion misses, because optimism doesn't get a vote. TOOL 2
— KILL CRITERIA. Define IN ADVANCE the specific, observable evidence that would prove
your thesis wrong and force you to exit — 'I'm wrong if same-store sales go negative for two
quarters', 'if the founder sells more than X%', 'if the regulator rules against them'. Writing kill
criteria before you're emotionally and financially committed fights the sunk-cost trap: once
you own a position, you'll rationalise every red flag, so you must decide the exit conditions
while you can still think clearly. TOOL 3 — ACTIVELY SEEK DISCONFIRMATION. Darwin had a

golden rule: whenever he encountered an observation that CONTRADICTED his theory, he
wrote it down immediately, because he'd noticed the mind conveniently forgets inconvenient
facts. Munger goes further: 'I never allow myself to have an opinion on anything that I don't
know the other side's argument better than they do.' So for every thesis, deliberately build
the strongest possible BEAR case — seek out the smartest people who disagree, steelman
their argument, and hunt for the evidence that would sink you. If you can't argue the other
side better than its believers, you don't understand your own position well enough to bet on
it.

Analogy. A good scientist doesn't try to PROVE her hypothesis — she tries to
DISPROVE it, and trusts it only after it survives her hardest attempts to break it.
A theory that can't be falsified isn't science, and a thesis you've only ever
defended isn't an investment case, it's a hope. The pre-mortem is running the
experiment that tries to kill your idea; kill criteria are deciding in advance what
result would falsify it; seeking disconfirmation is going looking for that result on
purpose. You trust the thesis that survives the assault, not the one you never
attacked.


**Key Terms**

Pre-mortem — Imagining a decision has already failed and explaining why, to surface hidden
risks before committing.
Kill criteria — Specific, pre-defined evidence that would prove your thesis wrong and trigger an
exit.
Disconfirming evidence — Facts that contradict your view; the ones the mind tends to ignore
or forget.
Steelmanning — Constructing the strongest possible version of the opposing argument, not a
weak caricature.
Sunk-cost trap — Letting prior commitment (money, time, ego) stop you acting on evidence
you're wrong.


### [VISUAL] Forwards vs Backwards
Visual: split path diagram
Split the frame in two. On the LEFT, labelled 'FORWARD THINKING — how most people
decide', show an arrow starting at 'The Decision' and travelling rightward toward a bright sun
labelled 'SUCCESS', with the path lined by little plus-signs ('reasons it works'); draw the few
warning signs along this path faded and semi-transparent, captioned 'optimism makes the
risks hard to see.' On the RIGHT, labelled 'INVERTED THINKING — the pre-mortem', show the
same journey reversed: start at a dark pit labelled 'FAILURE (assume it already happened)' on
the right and trace the arrow BACKWARD to 'The Decision', with the path now lined by vivid,
fully-visible red flags ('the customer left', 'refinancing dried up', 'the founder cashed out'),
captioned 'working back from failure makes the risks impossible to miss.' Between the
halves, a central box: 'Same decision. Same facts. Reversing the direction reveals what
optimism hid.' At the bottom, a banner: 'Kill criteria: decide NOW what evidence would prove
you wrong.'
Elements
- LEFT (forward): Decision -> Success, lined with 'reasons it works'; the risks drawn faded
and easy to overlook.
- RIGHT (inverted): Failure (assumed real) -> traced backward to Decision, with every risk
now a vivid red flag.
- THE CONTRAST: identical decision and facts; only the direction of reasoning changes what
you can see.
- THE PRE-MORTEM LABEL on the right path: 'imagine it already failed, then explain why.'
- KILL-CRITERIA BANNER: pre-define the evidence that would force you to exit, before you're
committed.
- TAKEAWAY: think backwards from failure to surface the risks forward-looking optimism
conceals.


### [DEEP DIVE] The Discipline of Thinking Backwards

Why the pre-mortem works when 'what are the risks?' doesn't
Ask a team 'what could go wrong here?' just before a decision they're excited about, and
you'll get a thin, polite list — because voicing serious doubt feels disloyal, pessimistic, or like
you didn't get the memo, and our optimism is genuinely turned up when we want something
to work. Gary Klein's insight was to change the framing from possibility to certainty: 'imagine
it's failed — now tell me why.' Treating the failure as an accomplished fact gives everyone
permission to be the detective rather than the doubter, and it engages a different mental
mode — prospective hindsight, which research finds dramatically increases the number and
specificity of risks people identify. The same person who had 'no concerns' in the forward
framing will, in the pre-mortem, vividly describe three ways the thing died. Nothing about the
facts changed; only the direction of the question did.

Kill criteria and the escalation trap
The most expensive force in investing is escalation of commitment: once you own a position,
every contrary fact becomes something to explain away, every loss becomes a reason to
'average down' rather than reconsider, and your ego fuses with the thesis. The antidote is to
decide your exit conditions BEFORE you're committed, while your judgment is still clean. Kill
criteria are specific and observable — not 'if the story changes' but 'if same-store sales are

negative two quarters running' or 'if net debt to EBITDA crosses 4x' or 'if the founder sells
more than ten percent.' Written down in advance, they convert a future emotional decision
into a pre-made rational one. When a kill criterion triggers, you don't get to renegotiate with
yourself; you act. This single discipline separates investors who cut their mistakes early from
those who ride them to zero, insisting the whole way down that the market just doesn't
understand.

Darwin, Munger and the duty to disconfirm
Charles Darwin had a habit he considered essential: whenever he came across a fact that
contradicted his theory, he forced himself to write it down at once, having learned that the
mind is brilliant at forgetting inconvenient evidence. He actively hunted for what would
disprove him. Munger turned this into an ironclad rule: 'I never allow myself to have an
opinion on anything that I don't know the other side's argument better than they do.' The
discipline is to steelman the bear case — not a weak caricature you can easily knock down,
but the strongest version the smartest opponent would make — and to go looking for the
smartest people who disagree with you. This is profoundly unnatural: the mind wants
confirmation, not challenge. But a thesis you've only ever defended is untested, and an
opinion you hold without truly understanding the counter-argument is borrowed confidence.
You earn the right to your conviction by trying hard to destroy it and failing.

Consistently not stupid
Buffett and Munger attribute much of their record not to brilliant insights but to avoiding
catastrophic errors: 'it is remarkable how much long-term advantage people like us have
gotten by trying to be consistently not stupid, instead of trying to be very intelligent.' This is
inversion applied to a whole career. Compounding is unforgiving of zeroes — a portfolio that
returns 20% a year for a decade and then loses 90% once has destroyed most of its gains, so
the single most valuable skill is not picking the most winners but avoiding the few ruinous
mistakes. Inversion, pre-mortems and kill criteria are all in service of this: they are
systematic ways of finding and sidestepping the errors that don't just cost you a bit of return,
but take you out of the game. The investor who simply avoids the blow-ups, decade after
decade, beats the brilliant one who occasionally detonates.
Real-world references
- Carl Jacobi — 'invert, always invert' (man muss immer umkehren), the mathematical origin of the
principle.
- Charlie Munger — inversion, 'consistently not stupid', and never holding a view without mastering
the other side.
- Gary Klein — the pre-mortem technique and the research on prospective hindsight.
- Charles Darwin — the 'golden rule' of immediately recording observations that contradict one's
theory.
- Karl Popper — falsifiability: a claim earns credibility by surviving genuine attempts to refute it.


### [EXAMPLE] A Pre-Mortem on a 'Forever-Hold'
Company / context: An Indian IT services major (illustrative pre-mortem)
Situation. The bull thesis writes itself: a large, profitable IT services company with decades
of steady growth, marquee global clients, prodigious cash generation, regular buybacks, and
a reputation as a 'forever-hold' compounder. Through the forward lens, it looks bulletproof —
the kind of stock you buy and never think about again. Precisely because everyone agrees
it's safe, it's the perfect candidate for a pre-mortem.

Data. The facts a pre-mortem forces into view: a business model fundamentally built on the
labour arbitrage of billing skilled engineers' time; revenue concentrated among a set of large
enterprise clients; margins sensitive to wage inflation and currency; and a sector in the
middle of a genuine technological shift in which AI tools can increasingly automate the very
coding and support work that the billable-hours model depends on.
Analysis. Invert the thesis. Assume it's seven years from now and this 'safe compounder'
has badly underperformed — what happened? Working backwards surfaces a coherent
failure story the forward case glossed over: AI tooling made large parts of routine services
work radically more productive, which sounds good but actually COLLAPSED the
billable-hours model the company's revenue depended on, so growth stalled even as the
technology 'worked'; a few large clients in-sourced or switched to AI-native vendors; and
margins compressed as the easy labour-arbitrage edge eroded. The pre-mortem doesn't
prove this WILL happen — it makes the dominant risk visible and specific, where the forward
'forever-hold' framing had rendered it invisible. From that failure story, you can now write
KILL CRITERIA in advance: I'm wrong if revenue-per-employee starts falling structurally rather
than rising; if the company's commentary admits AI is deflating (not expanding) deal sizes; if
its largest clients' spend declines for several quarters; or if it fails to build a credible AI-native
offering while peers do. These are observable triggers that would force a re-think BEFORE the
thesis quietly rots. And to complete the discipline, you'd steelman the strongest bear —
actually seek out the analysts arguing AI breaks the model — and make sure you understand
their case better than they do before you commit.
Analyst takeaway. Run a pre-mortem on your highest-conviction, 'obviously safe' ideas, not
just the speculative ones — comfort is where hidden risk accumulates. Imagine the thesis has
already failed, work backwards to the most coherent reason why, convert that reason into
specific kill criteria you write down today, and go find the smartest person who disagrees
with you. The goal isn't to talk yourself out of every idea; it's to know precisely what would
prove you wrong, before you're too committed to admit it. (Illustrative pre-mortem of a
thinking pattern, not a stock recommendation or a prediction about any company.)

PITFALLS         How Inversion and Pre-Mortems Go Wrong

### 1. Doing the pre-mortem as theatre — going through the motions but not letting it
change the decision.
Why it happens: Once a team is excited and committed, a pre-mortem can become a box-ticking ritual
where people name token risks and then proceed exactly as planned, having 'considered the downside.'
Fix: A pre-mortem only works if it can actually kill or reshape the decision. Treat the failure story as
real, dig until you find risks that genuinely worry you, and be willing to walk away or resize. If no
pre-mortem ever changes your mind, you're performing the ritual, not using the tool.


### 2. Never defining kill criteria — or defining them and then ignoring them when
they trigger.
Why it happens: Setting exit conditions feels like admitting you might be wrong, and when a criterion
actually fires, sunk-cost and ego scream to 'give it more time' and renegotiate the rules.
Fix: Write specific, observable kill criteria BEFORE you commit, while your judgment is clean — then
honour them mechanically. When a criterion triggers, act; don't renegotiate with yourself. The whole
point is to make the exit decision before emotion and money cloud it.


### 3. Seeking only weak versions of the opposing view (strawmanning) so you can
dismiss them.
Why it happens: The mind wants confirmation, so it gravitates to the dumbest version of the bear case
— easy to knock down — and treats demolishing it as proof the thesis is sound.
Fix: Steelman, don't strawman. Build the STRONGEST possible version of the argument against you,
ideally by seeking out the smartest people who actually hold it. Munger's bar: you must understand the
other side better than they do. Beating a weak counter-argument proves nothing.


### 4. Inverting into paralysis — using 'how could this fail?' to talk yourself out of
every decision.
Why it happens: Once you're good at finding failure modes, you can find them everywhere, and since
no thesis is risk-free, relentless inversion can make every opportunity look too dangerous to touch.
Fix: Inversion is for finding and managing the KILLER risks, not cataloguing every conceivable one into
inaction. After surfacing the real failure modes, ask 'are these survivable and have I priced them?' —
not 'is there any risk at all?' The goal is robust action, not perpetual hesitation.


### [RECAP] Thinking Backwards — Locking It In

**Key Takeaways**

- 'Invert, always invert' — many problems are easier solved backwards: ask how to fail, then
avoid it.
- It's usually easier to identify what to AVOID than what to do, and inversion bypasses
optimism bias.
- Run a pre-mortem: imagine the decision has already failed, then explain why — it surfaces
hidden risks.
- Define kill criteria in advance — specific, observable evidence that would prove you wrong
and force an exit.
- Honour kill criteria mechanically when they trigger; don't renegotiate with yourself (the
sunk-cost trap).
- Actively seek disconfirmation: steelman the bear case and understand the other side
better than they do.
- Avoiding the catastrophic mistakes (the zeroes) matters more than catching every winner
— be consistently not stupid.

Analyst mindset. The inverting thinker is, paradoxically, the most confident
kind — because her conviction has survived a deliberate assault. Before
committing, she imagines the failure and works backward to its cause; she
writes down exactly what would prove her wrong; she goes hunting for the
smartest people who disagree and tries to understand them better than they
understand themselves. She knows that compounding is destroyed by ruin, so
she prizes not-being-stupid above being-brilliant. She isn't a pessimist — she
acts decisively — but she earns the right to act by first trying as hard as she can
to destroy her own idea.


**Connections**

- Session 4 (Probabilistic Thinking) — a pre-mortem is how you estimate and respect the downside
probability.
- Session 5 (Latticework) — inversion is itself one of the most powerful general-purpose mental
models.
- Session 10 (Debugging Your Brain) — kill criteria and disconfirmation are direct defences against
confirmation bias.
- Session 11 (Decision-Making Under Uncertainty) — thinking backwards is core to high-stakes
decisions you can't undo.

## Quiz

**Q1. *[EASY]*** What does 'inversion' mean as a thinking tool?

- A. Reversing a company's balance sheet
- **✓ B. Solving a problem backwards — asking how to fail, then avoiding it, instead of how to succeed**
- C. Inverting a stock chart
- D. Doing the opposite of the consensus

> Why: Inversion means attacking a problem from the reverse direction — asking 'how could this fail?' and then avoiding those things, rather than only asking 'how do I succeed?' It often reveals what forward thinking misses.


**Q2. *[EASY]*** What is a 'pre-mortem'?

- A. A medical examination
- **✓ B. Imagining a decision has already failed and explaining why, before you commit**
- C. A post-mortem done early
- D. Calculating a worst-case price target

> Why: A pre-mortem (from Gary Klein) imagines that a decision has definitively failed, then asks the team to explain why. Treating failure as a fact surfaces risks that a normal 'what are the risks?' discussion suppresses.


**Q3. *[EASY]*** What are 'kill criteria'?

- A. Reasons to buy more of a stock
- **✓ B. Specific, pre-defined evidence that would prove your thesis wrong and force you to exit**
- C. The maximum you'll invest
- D. Criteria for firing an analyst

> Why: Kill criteria are specific, observable conditions, defined in advance, that would falsify your thesis and trigger an exit. Setting them before you're committed fights the sunk-cost trap and emotional rationalisation.


**Q4. *[MEDIUM]*** Why does a pre-mortem surface more risks than simply

asking a team 'what could go wrong?'
- A. It takes longer
- **✓ B. Framing failure as already-happened gives people permission to voice doubts and engages 'prospective hindsight'**
- C. It uses better data
- D. It involves more people

> Why: Asking 'what could go wrong?' before an exciting decision yields a thin list, because voicing doubt feels disloyal. Treating the failure as a fact ('it failed - why?') lets everyone be the detective and dramatically increases the risks identified.


**Q5. *[MEDIUM]*** Munger says he never allows himself an opinion unless he

knows the other side's argument better than they do. What
discipline is this?
- A. Confirmation bias
- **✓ B. Actively seeking disconfirmation and steelmanning the opposing view**
- C. Avoiding all opinions
- D. Following the consensus

> Why: It's the duty to disconfirm: you must build the strongest version of the argument against you - steelman it - and understand it better than its believers. A thesis you've only ever defended is untested.


**Q6. *[MEDIUM]*** An investor's kill criterion triggers, but sunk-cost and ego

urge them to 'give it more time.' What does the discipline
require?
- A. Renegotiate the criteria with new information
- **✓ B. Honour the criterion and act, since it was set precisely to be obeyed when judgment is clouded**
- C. Average down instead
- D. Ignore it if the story still sounds good

> Why: Kill criteria exist to convert a future emotional decision into a pre-made rational one. When one triggers, you act rather than renegotiate - that's the entire point of setting them before you were committed.


**Q7. *[MEDIUM]*** Why does Munger emphasise being 'consistently not stupid'

over being 'very intelligent'?
- A. Intelligence doesn't matter at all
- **✓ B. Because compounding is destroyed by catastrophic losses, so avoiding the ruinous mistakes matters more than catching every winner**
- C. Because smart people always fail
- D. To sound humble

> Why: One ruinous loss can erase a decade of good returns, so avoiding the few catastrophic errors compounds more than chasing brilliance. Inversion, pre-mortems and kill criteria are all tools for systematically avoiding the blow-ups.


**Q8. *[MEDIUM]*** When is it MOST important to run a pre-mortem, according to the session?
- A. Only on speculative, risky bets
- **✓ B. Especially on your highest-conviction, 'obviously safe' ideas, because comfort hides accumulating risk**
- C. Only when you're already losing money
- D. Never; it wastes time on good ideas


> Why: Hidden risk accumulates precisely where everyone agrees something is safe. Running a pre-mortem on a 'forever-hold' compounder, not just speculative bets, is where inversion adds the most value - comfort is where blind spots live.


**Q9. *[HARD]*** A team runs a pre-mortem, lists a few token risks, declares

the downside 'considered,' and proceeds with the plan exactly as
before. What's the flaw?
- A. They should have skipped the pre-mortem
- **✓ B. The pre-mortem was theatre — it only works if it can genuinely change or kill the decision**
- C. Pre-mortems are inherently useless
- D. They needed more data

> Why: A pre-mortem that can never alter the decision is a box-ticking ritual, not a tool. It must be capable of reshaping or killing the plan; if no pre-mortem ever changes your mind, you're performing the motions, not using the method.


**Q10. *[HARD]*** An analyst becomes so skilled at finding failure modes that

every opportunity looks too risky and they never act. What's the
correct calibration of inversion?
- A. Stop using inversion
- **✓ B. Use it to find and manage the KILLER, un-survivable risks — then ask 'are these survivable and priced?', not 'is there any risk at all?'**
- C. Only invert on bad ideas
- D. Find even more risks to be safe

> Why: Inversion is for surfacing and managing the fatal risks, not cataloguing every conceivable one into paralysis. After finding the real failure modes, the question is whether they're survivable and priced - robust action, not perpetual hesitation.


---

## Interview Questions

### 1. Explain inversion as a problem-solving tool and why it's so powerful.
Conceptual question testing the core idea.

**Strong answer.** Inversion means solving a problem backwards — instead of asking 'how do I
succeed?', you ask 'how do I fail?', list every way it could go wrong, and then avoid those things.
Jacobi's maxim was 'invert, always invert,' and Munger built much of his thinking on it. It's
powerful for two reasons. First, it's usually easier to identify what to avoid than what to do —
there are a handful of clear ways to wreck a portfolio or a career, and steering clear of them gets
you most of the way there. Second, it defeats the optimism and confirmation bias baked into
forward thinking: when I'm building a case FOR something, my mind recruits supporting evidence
and waves away problems, but when I deliberately try to make it FAIL, those same problems
suddenly become visible. Munger's example was being asked how to help India and inverting it —
what would guarantee India stays poor? — then saying avoid all of that. It connects to his
deepest principle: you win long-term more by being consistently not stupid than by being
brilliant, because avoiding the catastrophic mistakes matters more than catching every winner.

> Look for: Defines inversion clearly · Explains it's easier to find what to avoid · Notes it bypasses optimism/confirmation bias · Connects to 'consistently not stupid'/avoiding ruin


### 2. Walk me through how you'd run a pre-mortem on an investment thesis.
Tests the practical method.

**Strong answer.** I'd imagine it's a year or two from now and the position has definitively,
embarrassingly failed — not 'might fail,' but has failed and lost serious money. Then I'd write the
story of exactly why. The framing matters: treating the failure as a fact rather than a possibility
gets past the reluctance to voice doubt, because I'm now explaining a 'failure' that already

happened rather than being the lone pessimist before a decision. That surfaces risks a normal
'what are the risks?' list glosses over. Once I have the most coherent failure story, I'd convert it
into specific, observable kill criteria — the exact evidence that would tell me the thesis is
breaking, written down now while my judgment is clean. And I'd complete it by steelmanning the
bear case, seeking out the smartest people who disagree and making sure I understand their
argument better than they do. The output isn't 'don't do it' — it's knowing precisely what would
prove me wrong before I'm too committed to admit it.

> Look for: Describes imagining definitive failure and explaining why · Notes the framing surfaces suppressed doubts · Converts the failure story into kill criteria · Adds steelmanning the opposing view


### 3. Why set kill criteria in advance rather than just reassessing as you go?
Opinion question testing understanding of the sunk-cost/escalation problem.

**Strong answer.** Because once I own a position, my judgment is compromised in a very
predictable way — escalation of commitment. Every contrary fact becomes something to explain
away, every loss becomes a reason to average down rather than reconsider, and my ego fuses
with the thesis. 'Reassessing as I go' sounds fine but in practice it means reassessing with a brain
that's now motivated to stay in. The fix is to decide the exit conditions BEFORE I'm committed,
while I can still think clearly, and make them specific and observable — not 'if the story changes'
but 'if same-store sales go negative two quarters running' or 'if the founder sells more than ten
percent.' Written in advance, they convert a future emotional decision into a pre-made rational
one. When a kill criterion triggers, I act; I don't get to renegotiate with myself. That single
discipline is what separates investors who cut mistakes early from those who ride them to zero
insisting the market is wrong.

> Look for: Identifies escalation of commitment · Explains why in-the-moment reassessment is compromised · Stresses specific/observable criteria set in advance · Notes you must honour them mechanically


### 4. Tell me about a time you actively sought out evidence that you were wrong
about something.
Behavioural question testing whether the candidate genuinely practises disconfirmation.

**Strong answer.** The part I'd emphasise is that I went looking for the disconfirming evidence on
purpose, rather than waiting for it to find me. I had a view I was reasonably confident in, and
instead of gathering more support for it, I deliberately sought out the strongest case against it —
the smartest people who disagreed, the data that would undermine my thesis — and tried to
steelman their argument rather than dismiss a weak version of it. In that instance it actually
changed my conclusion, or at least materially lowered my confidence and reshaped how I sized
the decision. What I took from it is Darwin's discipline: the mind is brilliant at forgetting
inconvenient facts, so you have to actively hunt for them and write them down. A view I've only
ever defended is untested. I only trust a thesis after I've tried hard to break it and it survived.

> Look for: Gives a concrete example · Shows seeking disconfirmation proactively · Mentions steelmanning rather than dismissing · Reflects on changing or pressure-testing the view


### 5. I've built a really strong bull case — every reason points to buy. That's a
good sign, isn't it?
Trick question — unanimous confirmation should trigger suspicion, not comfort.

**Strong answer.** It should actually make me a little suspicious, not comfortable. If every single
reason points the same way and I've found no serious counter-argument, the most likely
explanation isn't that the idea is flawless — it's that I've been building a case FOR it and my mind
has been quietly recruiting support and waving away problems. A thesis I've only ever defended
is untested. So before I take that as a green light, I'd invert it: run a pre-mortem assuming it's
already failed and work backwards to why, and then deliberately go build the strongest possible
bear case — seek out the smartest people who disagree and try to understand their argument
better than they do. If, after genuinely trying to destroy the thesis, it still stands, THEN the

unanimity means something. But a bull case with no visible bear case usually means I haven't
looked hard enough, not that there's nothing to find.

> Look for: Treats unanimous confirmation as a warning · Identifies confirmation bias in case-building · Proposes inversion/pre-mortem and steelmanning · Earns conviction only after trying to break the thesis


### 6. What's the connection between inversion and 'being consistently not
stupid'?
Tests the link between the tool and the career-level principle.

**Strong answer.** They're the same idea at different scales. 'Consistently not stupid' is inversion
applied to a whole career: instead of asking 'how do I be brilliant?', you ask 'how do I avoid the
catastrophic mistakes?' and systematically steer clear of them. The reason this matters so much
is that compounding is unforgiving of zeroes — a portfolio that compounds at twenty percent for
a decade and then loses ninety percent once has given back most of its gains. So the single most
valuable skill isn't picking the most winners, it's avoiding the few ruinous errors that take you out
of the game. Inversion, pre-mortems and kill criteria are all tools in service of that: they're
systematic ways of finding and sidestepping the mistakes that don't just dent your returns but
end them. The investor who simply avoids the blow-ups, decade after decade, beats the brilliant
one who occasionally detonates. So inversion isn't pessimism — it's the engine of survival, and
survival is what lets compounding work.

> Look for: Frames 'not stupid' as career-scale inversion · Explains compounding's intolerance of ruin · Prioritises avoiding errors over maximising winners · Notes survival enables compounding


### 7. Can inversion be taken too far? When does it become a problem?
Opinion question testing awareness of the paralysis failure mode.

**Strong answer.** Yes, definitely. Once you get good at finding failure modes, you can find them
everywhere, and since no thesis is ever completely risk-free, relentless inversion can make every
opportunity look too dangerous to touch — you invert your way into paralysis and never act. The
other failure is doing it as theatre: running a pre-mortem, naming a few token risks, declaring the
downside 'considered,' and proceeding exactly as planned. The calibration is to remember what
inversion is FOR: surfacing and managing the killer, un-survivable risks, not cataloguing every
conceivable one. After I've found the real failure modes, the right question is 'are these
survivable, and have I priced them?' — not 'is there any risk at all?' If a risk is survivable and I'm
being paid for it, that's an acceptable bet, not a reason to freeze. So inversion should sharpen
action, not prevent it; the goal is robust decisions, not perpetual hesitation or a ritual that
changes nothing.

> Look for: Identifies paralysis as a failure mode · Also flags pre-mortem-as-theatre · Reframes the question as survivable-and-priced · Concludes inversion should enable action not prevent it


### 8. Why is it especially valuable to run a pre-mortem on your 'safest',
highest-conviction ideas?
Tests the counter-intuitive point that comfort hides risk.

**Strong answer.** Because hidden risk accumulates exactly where everyone agrees something is
safe. On a speculative bet, I'm already alert — I'm looking hard for what could go wrong. But on a
'forever-hold' compounder that the whole market treats as bulletproof, my guard is down, the
bull case feels self-evident, and nobody's stress-testing it — which is precisely the condition
under which a slow-building structural risk can creep up undetected. The forward, optimistic
framing of a 'safe' idea makes its biggest vulnerability invisible. So running a pre-mortem there
— imagining this obviously-safe business has badly underperformed in seven years and asking
what happened — forces the dominant risk into view and lets me write kill criteria for something
I'd otherwise never think to monitor. The speculative ideas get scrutiny automatically; the
comfortable ones don't, and that's where inversion earns its keep. Complacency, not obvious
risk, is what tends to blow up the 'safe' holdings.


> Look for: Notes risk accumulates where things feel safe · Explains the guard is down on high-conviction ideas · Contrasts with speculative bets getting automatic scrutiny · Connects to surfacing the dominant hidden risk


### 9. Some say focusing on how things fail is just negativity. How do you
respond?
Opinion question defending inversion against the 'negativity' critique.

**Strong answer.** I'd say it's the opposite of negativity — it's how you earn the right to act with
confidence. A safety engineer who obsesses over how a bridge could collapse isn't a pessimist;
she's the reason the bridge stands, because she engineered out every failure mode. Inversion is
the same: I imagine the failure not because I expect it, but because surfacing and managing the
killer risks is what lets me commit decisively to what survives the scrutiny. The genuinely
confident investors aren't the ones who never think about failure — they're the ones whose
conviction has survived a deliberate assault. And there's a hard practical reason: compounding is
destroyed by ruin, so avoiding the catastrophic mistakes is worth more than any amount of
optimism. So I'm not negative; I act, and often aggressively. I just insist on trying to destroy my
own idea first, because a thesis that survives that is one I can actually bet on, while a thesis I've
only ever cheered for is just a hope.

> Look for: Reframes inversion as enabling confident action · Uses the engineer/failure-mode analogy · Notes the truly confident have stress-tested their views · Ties to avoiding ruin/compounding


### 10. Does AI change how an analyst should use inversion and pre-mortems?
Forward-looking opinion tying to the module's AI theme.

**Strong answer.** It makes them more useful, and AI can actually supercharge the mechanics
while the judgment stays human. On the mechanical side, AI is a fantastic disconfirmation
engine: I can ask it to build the strongest possible bear case, run a structured pre-mortem,
generate failure modes I might be blind to, and steelman the other side — and because it has no
ego invested in my thesis, it'll happily tell me why I'm wrong, which is exactly what my own
confirmation-biased brain resists doing. That's genuinely valuable; one of the hardest disciplines
becomes one prompt away. But the human judgment remains: deciding which of the failure
modes are real and which are noise, setting kill criteria that actually fit the situation, and having
the emotional discipline to HONOUR those criteria when they trigger and money and ego are on
the line — that last part especially is a human weakness no model fixes for you. So I'd use AI to
generate the bear case and the pre-mortem far more thoroughly than I could alone, and then do
the human work of weighing, deciding and, hardest of all, acting on it. The tool removes the
excuse for not seeking disconfirmation; the discipline of using it is still on me.

> Look for: Argues AI strengthens disconfirmation/pre-mortem mechanics · Notes AI has no ego so surfaces contrary views readily · Identifies judging which risks are real and honouring kill criteria as human · Positions AI as removing the excuse not to disconfirm


---

## Simulation — The Pre-Mortem — Before the Big Bet

*HARD · 10 min · decision making*


**Scenario.** You're an analyst at a Mumbai fund. Over months of work you've developed a
genuinely strong thesis on a mid-cap company — the research is deep, the bull case is
well-constructed, and your PM loves it. He now wants to make it the fund's single largest
position by far, sizing it at nearly a quarter of the portfolio: 'This is our best idea in years.
When you have this much conviction, you bet big.' The thesis really is good. But the size
makes you uneasy, and you remember the discipline: the most dangerous moment is when
conviction is highest and everyone's excited. Before this gets committed, you call for a
pre-mortem. You have ten minutes to think backwards, not forwards. The question is not 'is
the thesis right?' — you think it is. It's 'how could this specific bet, at this size, end up doing

serious damage — and what would prove us wrong?'

**Your task.** Resist the forward, optimistic pull. Think backwards. (1) Run a PRE-MORTEM:
imagine it's two years from now and this position has badly hurt the fund. Write the most
coherent story of WHY — and separate two distinct failure types: the THESIS being wrong
(the business disappoints) and the SIZE being wrong (even a modest thesis-miss or a liquidity
shock does outsized damage because the position is so large). (2) Apply INVERSION to the
sizing decision specifically: what could make a quarter-of-the-fund position catastrophic that
a 3% position wouldn't — illiquidity if you need to exit, a single shock, the correlation with
everything else you own? (3) Define specific, observable KILL CRITERIA you'd write down
TODAY — the exact evidence that would prove the thesis breaking and force a re-think, set
now while your judgment is clean. (4) STEELMAN the bear case: what's the strongest
argument the smartest sceptic would make, and do you understand it better than they do?
(5) Make a recommendation — not 'the thesis is great' (you've agreed it is) but on the SIZE
and the conditions: how big, with what kill criteria, given the asymmetry of ruin. Write a short
note to your PM.

**Objectives**

- Run a genuine pre-mortem instead of accepting the high-conviction forward case
- Separate thesis-failure risk from position-sizing/ruin risk
- Apply inversion specifically to the concentration decision (liquidity, single-shock, correlation)
- Define specific, observable kill criteria in advance
- Steelman the strongest bear case rather than a weak caricature
- Recommend on size and conditions, weighing the asymmetry of ruin
Decision points
- Is high conviction a reason to skip the pre-mortem, or exactly when you most need one?
- What's the failure story — and is it the thesis being wrong, the size being wrong, or both?
- What makes a 25% position catastrophic that a small one wouldn't be (liquidity, single shock,
correlation)?
- What specific, observable evidence — defined now — would force you to cut?

How a top analyst would think it through. A strong response does NOT just affirm
the thesis and size it big because conviction is high — high conviction plus excitement
is precisely the most dangerous moment, and the one that most needs inversion. The
analyst runs a real pre-mortem: imagine it's two years out and this position has badly
damaged the fund, and write the coherent reasons why. Crucially, separate two failure
types. THESIS RISK: the business disappoints — a key driver doesn't materialise, a
competitor emerges, management stumbles. SIZE/RUIN RISK: this is the subtler and
more important one — at ~25% of the fund, even a moderate thesis-miss does
outsized damage, AND new risks appear that a small position wouldn't have: if the
thesis breaks you may not be able to EXIT a mid-cap without crushing the price
(liquidity), a single unexpected shock (fraud, regulation, a black-swan) is now
potentially fund-ending rather than a flesh wound, and the position may be correlated
with other holdings so the 'diversified' fund is secretly one bet. This is Session 4's
lesson — never size so a single wrong call can ruin you — made concrete. The analyst
then defines specific, observable KILL CRITERIA in advance (a named operating metric
rolling over, the founder selling beyond a threshold, net debt crossing a level, a key
client loss) so the exit is a pre-made rational decision, not a future emotional one
when sunk cost and ego scream to hold. And the analyst steelmans the bear case —
seeking the smartest sceptic's argument and aiming to understand it better than they
do — because a thesis this size that has only ever been defended is untested. The
recommendation isn't 'don't do it'; it's a reasoned position on SIZE and CONDITIONS:
likely size it meaningfully smaller than 25% so a blow-up is survivable, attach explicit
kill criteria, and ensure the asymmetry of ruin is respected — being consistently not
stupid beats being brilliant. Traps to name: optimism/confirmation bias at peak
conviction, escalation of commitment, ignoring liquidity and correlation, and conflating
a good thesis with a safe bet. The lesson: the bigger the conviction, the more you
must think backwards before you commit.

Hints
- Highest conviction plus a room full of excitement — is that when to skip the pre-mortem, or when
you need it most?
- Imagine it's two years on and this hurt the fund badly. What's the story? Is it the thesis, the size, or
both?
- If the thesis breaks, can you even exit a 25% mid-cap position without crushing the price?
- A great thesis and a safe bet are not the same thing. What would a single unexpected shock do at
this size?
- Write the exit rule NOW: what exact, observable evidence would force you to cut — before ego and
sunk cost set in?


---

## Session 7 — Reading Financials Like a Detective — The Story Beneath the Numbers
Statements are not a spreadsheet; they are a confession. Read the three statements
together as a narrative about strategy, incentives and pressure — and hear what
management is really telling you (and hiding).


**In this session**

- Read the three statements as one connected story
- Infer strategy and incentives from accounting choices
- Spot tension between the numbers and the narrative
- Form a business judgment, not just a ratio printout


### [HOOK] Profits Made of Paper
A company reports record annual profit. The press release leads with the number in bold, the
CEO calls it a 'landmark year', and the stock jumps. One analyst reads the headline, sees
'profit up 40%', and buys. Another analyst barely glances at the profit line. Instead she does
what a detective does at a crime scene: she assumes nothing and starts cross-examining the
evidence. She flips past the glossy P&L to the cash flow statement — and finds that while
reported profit hit a record, cash from operations was actually NEGATIVE. The company
earned 'profit' it never collected. She checks why: receivables have nearly doubled, meaning
the company booked sales to customers who haven't paid and may never. She reads the
footnotes most people skip and finds a large chunk of the 'profit' is a one-time gain from
revaluing an asset, dressed up inside 'other income.' And in the audit section, a quiet line:
the auditor was changed last year. No single one of these is proof of anything. But a
detective doesn't need a confession — she follows the threads, and together they tell a very
different story than the bold headline. She passes. The headline-reader owns a company
whose profits are made of paper.

The question. A financial statement looks like a spreadsheet of objective facts.
But every number in it was a CHOICE made by someone with incentives. When
you read a company's accounts, are you reading the numbers — or are you
reading the story someone wants you to believe?

Why it matters. Financial statements are not a neutral record; they are a narrative,
authored by management, full of choices about what to highlight, what to bury, and how to
present reality in the most flattering light the rules allow. The analyst who reads them like a
trusting accountant gets the official story. The one who reads them like a detective —
assuming nothing, cross-examining the evidence, following the cash — gets the truth. This
session is about developing that forensic instinct.


### [CONCEPT] The Statements Are a Story, Not a Spreadsheet
The single most important shift in reading financials is to stop treating them as objective fact
and start treating them as a NARRATIVE with an author. Within the rules of accounting there
is enormous latitude — when to recognise revenue, how fast to depreciate, what to capitalise
versus expense, how to value inventory, what counts as 'one-off', which metric to feature.

Every one of these is a choice, and management makes those choices for reasons. Recall the
most powerful model from Session 5: incentives. Management is usually rewarded for higher
reported profit, a higher share price, hitting guidance — so the default assumption should be
that, where the rules allow discretion, the accounts are presented in the most flattering light,
not the most conservative. This doesn't mean every company is committing fraud; most
aren't. It means the statements are a case being made, and your job is not to accept the case
but to test it. The detective's three habits make this concrete. First, ASSUME NOTHING —
treat every favourable number as a claim to be verified, not a fact to be accepted. Second,
FOLLOW THE CASH — profit is an opinion shaped by estimates and accruals, but cash is far
harder to fake, so when the story in the profit line and the story in the cash flow disagree,
believe the cash. Third, READ WHAT THEY DON'T WANT YOU TO READ — the footnotes, the
accounting-policy changes, the 'other income', the related-party transactions; the headline is
designed to be seen, so the real information is often in what's buried. You are not auditing for
legality; you are reading for TRUTH, and the truth is usually in the gap between what the
headline says and what the cash, the footnotes and the three statements together reveal.

Analogy. A detective interviewing a suspect doesn't take the polished
statement at face value — 'I was home all evening.' She checks it against the
other evidence: the phone records, the neighbour's account, the timestamp on
the receipt. Truth emerges from CONSISTENCY across independent sources; a
lie shows up as a discrepancy between them. Financial statements work the
same way. The P&L, the balance sheet and the cash flow are three witnesses to
the same events, and management's narrative is the suspect's statement. You
don't trust the statement; you cross-examine the witnesses against each other
and watch for where their stories don't line up. The discrepancy is the clue.


**Key Terms**

Statements as narrative — Accounts are a story shaped by management's choices and
incentives, not neutral fact.
Accounting discretion — The legitimate latitude in the rules (recognition, depreciation,
capitalisation) management chooses how to use.
'Profit is an opinion, cash is a fact' — Earnings depend on estimates; cash flow is much
harder to manipulate.
Forensic mindset — Assume nothing, verify favourable claims, follow the cash, read what's
buried.
Quality of earnings — The degree to which reported profit is backed by real, sustainable cash,
not accruals or one-offs.

CONCEPT
The Detective's Toolkit — Cross-Examining the
Three Witnesses
Reading like a detective is a set of repeatable cross-checks between the three statements
and the notes. CHECK 1 — PROFIT vs CASH (the master check). Compare net profit to cash
flow from operations over a few years. They won't match exactly — that's normal — but if
profit keeps rising while operating cash flow stagnates or turns negative, the profit is being
manufactured by accruals rather than collected in cash. Persistent divergence is the single
most powerful red flag in all of analysis. CHECK 2 — DOES THE BALANCE SHEET CONFIRM
THE P&L STORY? If revenue is growing, are RECEIVABLES growing even faster? (That means
sales are being booked but not collected.) Is INVENTORY ballooning faster than sales? (Goods
aren't selling; a write-down may be coming.) The balance sheet often confesses what the

income statement conceals. CHECK 3 — INTERROGATE 'OTHER INCOME' AND ONE-OFFS. How
much of the profit is from the actual operating business versus one-time gains, asset sales,
revaluations, or 'other income'? Strip those out to see the real, repeatable earning power. A
company that hits its target only because of a one-off has missed. CHECK 4 — READ THE
FOOTNOTES AND POLICY CHANGES. Did they change a depreciation rate, an estimate, or a
revenue-recognition policy this year — conveniently boosting profit? Are there large
related-party transactions (money flowing to entities the promoter controls)? The footnotes
are where the bodies are buried, which is exactly why most people don't read them. CHECK 5
— WATCH THE TELLS. An auditor resignation or change, a CFO departure, ever-growing
'complexity' that makes the accounts hard to follow, aggressive non-standard 'adjusted'
metrics that always exclude bad things — none is proof, but each is a thread worth pulling.
The mindset tying it all together: you are reconciling three witnesses and a narrator, and you
trust the conclusion only when their stories agree — especially when the cash agrees.

Analogy. A forensic accountant chasing a fraud famously 'follows the money' —
not the story people tell about the money, but where it actually went,
transaction by transaction, because money leaves a trail that words don't.
Reading a company is the same discipline scaled down: the income statement is
the story the company tells about its money; the cash flow statement is where
the money actually went. When the two diverge, you follow the cash, because
the cash can't give a press conference.


**Key Terms**

Profit-vs-cash check — Comparing net profit to operating cash flow; persistent divergence is a
top red flag.
Receivables/inventory build — These rising faster than sales signals uncollected sales or
unsold goods.
One-off / 'other income' — Non-operating or non-recurring gains that flatter profit and hide
weak operations.
Footnote forensics — Reading the notes for policy changes, estimates, and related-party
transactions.
Red-flag tells — Auditor/CFO changes, rising complexity, aggressive adjusted metrics; threads
worth pulling.


### [VISUAL] Cross-Examining the Three Witnesses
Visual: cross examination diagram
Draw three 'witness stands' side by side, each a statement: 'THE INCOME STATEMENT (the
story management tells)', 'THE BALANCE SHEET (what the company owns and owes)', and
'THE CASH FLOW STATEMENT (where the money actually went).' Above them, a figure
labelled 'THE NARRATOR: management's chosen story (record profit!)'. Draw double-headed
arrows BETWEEN the three witnesses labelled with the key cross-checks: between Income
and Cash Flow, 'Does the profit show up as CASH? (if not -> red flag)'; between Income and
Balance Sheet, 'Are receivables/inventory growing faster than sales? (if so -> red flag)'; and a
magnifying glass hovering over a small 'FOOTNOTES' box beneath, labelled 'where the
bodies are buried: policy changes, one-offs, related parties.' On the right, two outcome
boxes: a green one, 'Stories AGREE (esp. cash) -> earnings are real'; and a red one, 'Stories
CONFLICT -> the headline is paper.' Caption: 'Don't believe the narrator. Cross-examine the
witnesses — and when in doubt, believe the cash.'
Elements
- THE NARRATOR (management): the chosen headline story, designed to be seen and
believed.
- THREE WITNESSES: income statement (the story), balance sheet (assets/liabilities), cash
flow (where money went).
- CROSS-CHECK ARROWS: profit-vs-cash, and revenue-vs-receivables/inventory — the
discrepancies that reveal truth.
- THE FOOTNOTES (magnifying glass): policy changes, one-offs, related-party deals —
buried, so read them.
- TWO OUTCOMES: witnesses agree (especially cash) = real earnings; witnesses conflict =
paper profit.
- TAKEAWAY: truth is consistency across the three statements; when the cash disagrees
with the profit, believe the cash.


### [DEEP DIVE] Following the Money

Why cash is the lie detector
Reported profit is the end of a long chain of estimates and choices: when revenue is 'earned',
how much of a sale to provision for bad debt, how fast to depreciate an asset, what to
capitalise rather than expense. Each is a judgment, and judgments can be nudged. Cash
from operations is far harder to nudge — either the money came in the door or it didn't. This
is why seasoned analysts live by 'profit is an opinion, cash is a fact', and why the comparison
of net profit to operating cash flow over several years is the most powerful single diagnostic
in financial analysis. A healthy business converts most of its profit into cash; a company
whose profits march upward while operating cash flow stagnates or goes negative is, at best,
of low earnings quality and, at worst, manufacturing profits it will never collect. When the
income statement and the cash flow statement tell different stories, the cash flow is almost
always the true one — because the cash can't give a press conference.

The balance sheet confesses what the P&L conceals
An income statement can show a beautiful, growing profit while the trouble accumulates
quietly on the balance sheet. The classic tell is receivables growing faster than revenue: the
company is booking sales but not collecting the money, which can mean it's stuffing the

channel, extending generous credit to weak customers to hit targets, or recognising revenue
too aggressively. Inventory growing faster than sales is the cousin tell — goods are piling up
unsold, and a write-down is coming that will eventually hit profit. Rising debt funding
'profitable growth' is another: if the operations were truly generating cash, why is the
borrowing climbing? The discipline is to never read the income statement alone. Every claim
of profitable growth should be checked against the balance sheet, because the
working-capital accounts often record the reality that the headline profit is busy hiding.

The footnotes, the one-offs, and the art of the highlight
Management controls not just the numbers but the FRAMING — what goes in bold in the
press release and what gets buried in note 34. Two habits protect you. First, strip out the
one-offs: asset sales, revaluations, insurance receipts, and the catch-all 'other income' can
turn a bad operating quarter into a 'record' one, so always separate the repeatable earning
power of the actual business from the non-recurring sugar. A company that only hit guidance
because of a one-time gain has, in reality, missed. Second, read the footnotes and the
accounting policies, especially CHANGES to them. A quietly extended depreciation schedule,
a tweaked estimate, a new revenue-recognition interpretation, a large transaction with a
promoter-controlled entity — these live in the notes precisely because they're the things
you'd react to if they were on the front page. Be especially wary of companies whose own
'adjusted' or non-standard metrics consistently exclude real, recurring costs to paint a
prettier picture than the audited numbers.

Pattern recognition and the tells
A detective develops instinct — a feel for the configurations that, while never individually
conclusive, cluster around trouble. In financial statements the tells include: an auditor
resigning or being changed (auditors rarely walk away from fees without reason); a CFO
departing abruptly; accounting that grows steadily more complex and harder to follow as the
years pass (complexity is often camouflage); round-number 'just-beat' results quarter after
quarter (real businesses are lumpier than that); aggressive related-party dealings; and a
management that reacts to pointed questions with irritation and obfuscation rather than
clarity. None of these proves wrongdoing, and a single one usually means nothing. But the
forensic mind keeps a running tally, and when several tells appear together — recall the
lollapalooza from Session 5 — the probability of a problem rises sharply. The job is not to
prove the case beyond doubt; it's to notice when the threads are accumulating and to step
back before the truth arrives the hard way.
Real-world references
- 'Financial Shenanigans' (Howard Schilit) — the classic catalogue of accounting manipulation
techniques and red flags.
- 'Quality of Earnings' (Thornton O'Glove) — reading the gap between reported profit and economic
reality.
- Benjamin Graham & David Dodd — 'Security Analysis', scepticism toward reported earnings and
conservative reading.
- Famous accounting frauds (e.g. Enron, Satyam) — cases where cash flow, footnotes and
related-party tells told the truth early.
- Charlie Munger — 'show me the incentive': accounting choices follow what management is rewarded
to report.


### [EXAMPLE] The Record Profit That Wasn't Cash

Company / context: A fast-growing infrastructure/EPC company (illustrative forensic
reading)
Situation. An infrastructure and construction company is the toast of the market: revenue
up sharply, record net profit, an order book management calls 'all-time high', and a stock
that's tripled. The bull narrative is a growth story, and the headline numbers seem to back it
completely. A detective doesn't argue with the narrative — she tests it against the evidence.
Data. Cross-examining the three statements over three years reveals a pattern the headline
hides: reported net profit climbing every year, while CASH FLOW FROM OPERATIONS is
persistently negative; RECEIVABLES (and 'unbilled revenue') growing much faster than
revenue itself; DEBT rising steadily to fund the gap; a meaningful slice of profit sitting in
'other income' rather than core operations; and, in the footnotes, sizeable transactions with
entities related to the promoter.
Analysis. Run the toolkit. PROFIT vs CASH: profit is at records but operating cash flow is
negative — the company is reporting earnings it isn't collecting; the profit is accrual, not
cash. WHY? The balance sheet answers: receivables and unbilled revenue are exploding,
which for an EPC company means work is being booked as revenue (under
percentage-of-completion accounting) long before — or even without — the cash ever
arriving, a textbook way to manufacture profit. The rising DEBT is the tell that confirms it: a
genuinely profitable, cash-generating business wouldn't need to borrow ever more just to
keep operating; the borrowing is plugging the hole between paper profit and real cash. The
'other income' slice means even the reported profit overstates the operating business's true
earning power. And the related-party transactions raise the darkest question of all — whether
cash is being routed to promoter entities while revenue is recognised at the listed company.
No single item is a smoking gun. But the detective isn't looking for a confession; she's looking
at the configuration — profit without cash, receivables outrunning sales, debt filling the gap,
one-offs flattering the number, related parties in the notes — and that configuration is the
classic signature of low-quality or manufactured earnings. The growth story might be real;
but the cash says the 'profit' is, at best, not money in the bank, and the prudent reading is
deep scepticism, not celebration.
Analyst takeaway. When a company's profit looks spectacular, your first move as a
detective is not to admire it but to ask the cash flow statement whether it's real.
Cross-examine the three statements: does the profit become cash, does the balance sheet
confirm the growth story or contradict it, how much is one-off, and what's buried in the
footnotes? A great headline backed by negative operating cash flow, ballooning receivables
and rising debt is not a growth story — it's a warning, and the cash is telling you the truth the
headline is hiding. (Illustrative composite to demonstrate a forensic reading pattern; not
based on or implying any specific company, and not a stock recommendation.)

PITFALLS         How Detective-Reading Goes Wrong

### 1. Reading the income statement alone — trusting the profit line without
cross-checking cash and the balance sheet.
Why it happens: The P&L is what the press release and most coverage feature, it's the simplest single
number, and 'record profit' is emotionally satisfying and easy to act on.
Fix: Never read the income statement in isolation. The very first cross-check is profit versus operating
cash flow over several years, followed by revenue versus receivables and inventory. The income
statement makes the claim; the cash flow and balance sheet test it.


### 2. Skipping the footnotes — treating the headline numbers as the whole story.
Why it happens: Footnotes are long, dense, deliberately dull, and seem like boilerplate, so most readers
(and even many analysts) never get past the primary statements.
Fix: Read the footnotes precisely BECAUSE they're where inconvenient things are buried —
accounting-policy changes, the composition of 'other income', related-party transactions, contingent
liabilities. The headline is designed to be seen; the real information is often in the notes, which is why
so few people read them.


### 3. Crying fraud at a single red flag — treating any one tell as proof of wrongdoing.
Why it happens: Once you learn the red flags it's tempting to feel clever by 'catching' a company, and a
single divergence or one auditor change can look damning in isolation.
Fix: No single tell is proof — receivables can rise for benign reasons, auditors change for ordinary ones.
The forensic skill is in the CONFIGURATION: it's when several tells cluster together (profit without cash
AND receivables outrunning sales AND rising debt AND related parties) that the probability of a real
problem climbs. Weigh the pattern, not the isolated flag.


### 4. Accepting management's 'adjusted' numbers at face value.
Why it happens: Companies present 'adjusted', 'underlying' or non-standard metrics that strip out
'one-offs', and these are pushed hard in presentations as the 'real' picture, so it's easy to anchor on
them.
Fix: Treat company-defined adjusted metrics with suspicion, especially when they consistently exclude
recurring costs or the same 'one-offs' every single year (a cost that recurs annually is not one-off).
Always reconcile back to the audited statutory numbers and the cash. Management chooses what to
'adjust' away, and they rarely adjust away good news.


### [RECAP] The Story Beneath the Numbers — Locking It In

**Key Takeaways**

- Financial statements are a narrative authored by management with incentives, not neutral
fact.
- Where the rules allow discretion, assume the most flattering presentation, then test it.
- 'Profit is an opinion, cash is a fact' — when the P&L and cash flow disagree, believe the
cash.
- Cross-examine         three    witnesses:   profit-vs-cash,   revenue-vs-receivables/inventory,
core-vs-one-off.
- Read the footnotes and policy changes — that's where inconvenient truths are buried.
- No single red flag is proof; it's the CONFIGURATION of tells clustering together that signals
trouble.
- Reconcile 'adjusted' metrics back to the audited statutory numbers and the cash;
management chooses what to exclude.

Analyst mindset. The detective-analyst approaches every set of accounts with
respectful suspicion. She knows the statements are a case being argued, not a
verdict, so she assumes nothing, follows the cash, and reads what management
would rather she skipped. She cross-examines the three statements against
each other and against the footnotes, looking not for a single smoking gun but
for the configuration of tells that reveals low-quality or manufactured earnings.
She doesn't accuse on thin evidence, and she doesn't celebrate a headline she
hasn't tested. Above all, when the profit line and the cash flow tell different
stories, she believes the cash — because the cash can't give a press conference.


**Connections**

- Session 5 (Latticework) — 'incentives' explains WHY management makes the accounting choices it
does.
- Session 2 (First-Principles Thinking) — strip the accounts to the real, irreducible cash earning power.
- Session 8 (Signal vs Noise) — the footnotes and cash flow are signal; the press-release headline is
often noise.
- Session 10 (Debugging Your Brain) — guard against being anchored by the bold headline number.

## Quiz

**Q1. *[EASY]*** The session argues that financial statements are best

understood as:
- A. A neutral, objective record of fact
- **✓ B. A narrative authored by management, who make choices with incentives**
- C. A legal document with no flexibility
- D. A random collection of numbers

> Why: Within accounting rules there's wide discretion, and management makes those choices for reasons (incentives). The statements are a case being argued, not neutral fact — so your job is to test the narrative, not accept it.


**Q2. *[EASY]*** 'Profit is an opinion, cash is a fact' means:

- A. Cash flow is irrelevant
- **✓ B. Reported profit depends on estimates and can be shaped, while cash is much harder to manipulate**
- C. Profit is always fake
- D. Companies should only report cash

> Why: Profit is the end of a chain of judgments (revenue recognition, depreciation, provisions), so it can be nudged; cash either arrived or it didn't. When the two disagree, the cash is usually the true story.


**Q3. *[EASY]*** Where in the annual report are inconvenient details most

often found?
- A. The bold headline in the press release
- **✓ B. The footnotes and accounting policies**
- C. The cover page
- D. The CEO's photo

> Why: Management controls the framing — the headline is designed to be seen. Policy changes, the composition of 'other income', and related-party transactions live in the footnotes precisely because they're what you'd react to on the front page.


**Q4. *[MEDIUM]*** A company reports record and rising net profit, but its cash

flow from operations has been negative for three years. What's
the most likely interpretation?
- A. The company is extremely healthy
- **✓ B. The profit is being manufactured by accruals rather than collected in cash — a major red flag**
- C. Cash flow statements are unreliable
- D. It's a temporary timing issue with no significance

> Why: Persistent divergence between rising profit and negative operating cash flow is the single most powerful red flag in analysis — it suggests earnings aren't being collected as cash, indicating low quality or manufactured profit.


**Q5. *[MEDIUM]*** Revenue is growing 20%, but receivables are growing 50%.

What does this most likely signal?
- A. The company is collecting cash faster
- **✓ B. Sales are being booked but not collected — possibly channel stuffing or aggressive revenue recognition**
- C. Nothing meaningful
- D. Lower debt

> Why: Receivables outrunning revenue means the company is recognising sales it hasn't collected the cash for — a classic balance-sheet tell that the income statement's growth story may be flattered or unsustainable.


**Q6. *[MEDIUM]*** Why be suspicious of a company-defined 'adjusted' profit

metric that excludes the same 'one-off' charge every single year?
- A. Adjusted metrics are always fraudulent
- **✓ B. A cost that recurs every year is not a one-off; excluding it inflates the picture versus the audited numbers**
- C. Adjusted metrics are required by law
- D. It means profit is understated

> Why: Management chooses what to 'adjust' away and rarely excludes good news. A 'one-off' that appears annually is a recurring cost, and stripping it out paints a prettier picture than the statutory accounts — always reconcile back to audited numbers and cash.


**Q7. *[MEDIUM]*** An infrastructure company shows record profit, but

receivables and unbilled revenue are exploding while debt rises
to fund operations. How should a detective read this?
- A. As strong, self-funding growth
- **✓ B. As a configuration suggesting profit is being booked ahead of cash, with borrowing plugging the gap — low earnings quality**
- C. As irrelevant accounting noise
- D. As proof the order book is healthy

> Why: Profit without cash, receivables outrunning sales, and rising debt to fund operations together form the classic signature of manufactured or low-quality earnings — a truly cash-generative business wouldn't need ever-more borrowing just to operate.


**Q8. *[MEDIUM]*** Why does the session say no single red flag should be

treated as proof of wrongdoing?
- A. Red flags never matter
- **✓ B. Many tells have benign explanations individually; it's the CONFIGURATION of several clustering together that raises the probability of a problem**
- C. Fraud is always obvious
- D. Only the auditor can judge


> Why: Receivables can rise for ordinary reasons; auditors change for benign ones. The forensic skill is weighing the pattern — when profit-without-cash, receivable build, rising debt and related-party deals appear together, the odds of a real problem climb sharply.


**Q9. *[HARD]*** Which combination of 'tells' would MOST raise a forensic

analyst's concern, illustrating the 'configuration over single flag'
principle?
- A. A single quarter of slightly lower margins
- **✓ B. Negative operating cash flow despite record profit, receivables outrunning sales, an abrupt auditor change, and large related-party transactions**
- C. A new product launch and higher marketing spend
- D. A one-time legal settlement disclosed clearly

> Why: This is a cluster of independent red flags — paper profit, uncollected sales, a suspicious auditor change, and related-party dealings — converging at once. Like a lollapalooza, the convergence (not any single flag) is what sharply raises the probability of manufactured earnings.


**Q10. *[HARD]*** Management reports a 'landmark' profit, but a third of it

comes from a one-time asset revaluation in 'other income.' For
assessing the business's FUTURE earning power, the analyst
should:
- A. Count the full reported profit, since it's audited
- **✓ B. Strip out the one-off to find the repeatable operating earning power, treating a target 'hit' only via one-offs as effectively a miss**
- C. Ignore the operating business entirely
- D. Add the one-off to next year's forecast

> Why: One-time gains flatter the headline but don't repeat, so they must be stripped out to see the real, sustainable earning power. A company that reaches its target only because of a non-recurring gain has, in economic substance, missed on its core operations.


---

## Interview Questions

### 1. Why do you say financial statements are a 'narrative' rather than objective
fact?
Conceptual question testing the core framing.

**Strong answer.** Because within accounting rules there's enormous discretion, and every
exercise of that discretion is a choice made by someone with incentives. When to recognise
revenue, how fast to depreciate, what to capitalise versus expense, how to value inventory, what
counts as a 'one-off', which metric to feature in the press release — all of these are choices, and
management usually makes them in the most flattering direction the rules allow, because they're
rewarded for higher reported profit and a higher share price. That doesn't mean most companies
are committing fraud; they aren't. It means the statements are a case being argued, not a verdict
handed down. So my job isn't to accept the numbers as fact — it's to test the narrative. I read the
accounts the way a detective reads a suspect's statement: assume nothing, verify the favourable
claims, and look for where the story doesn't hold together. The numbers are real, but the picture
they paint was composed by someone who wanted you to see it a certain way.

> Look for: Explains accounting discretion as choices · Links the choices to incentives · Notes it's not an accusation of fraud · Frames the analyst's job as testing the narrative


### 2. Walk me through how you'd check whether a company's reported profit is
real.
Tests the practical toolkit, especially profit-vs-cash.


**Strong answer.** My first and most important check is profit versus cash flow from operations
over several years. They won't match exactly, but if profit is climbing while operating cash flow
stagnates or turns negative, the profit is being manufactured by accruals rather than collected as
cash — that's the single most powerful red flag there is. Then I cross-check the balance sheet: if
revenue is growing, are receivables growing even faster, which means sales booked but not
collected, or is inventory ballooning, which means goods piling up unsold? Next I interrogate the
composition of profit — how much comes from the actual operating business versus one-offs,
asset sales, revaluations or 'other income' — and strip the non-recurring stuff out to find the real
earning power. Finally I read the footnotes for accounting-policy changes and related-party
transactions, and I watch for tells like an auditor or CFO change. The through-line is that the
income statement makes a claim and the cash flow, balance sheet and notes test it. When the
cash disagrees with the profit, I believe the cash.

> Look for: Leads with profit-vs-operating-cash-flow · Cross-checks receivables/inventory on the balance sheet · Strips out one-offs for real earning power · Reads footnotes and watches for tells


### 3. Of the three financial statements, which do you trust most and why?
Opinion question; a strong answer favours cash flow with reasoning.

**Strong answer.** The cash flow statement, specifically cash from operations. Reported profit is
the end of a long chain of estimates and choices — revenue recognition, provisioning,
depreciation, capitalisation — and every one of those is a judgment that can be nudged in a
flattering direction. Cash from operations is far harder to fake: either the money came in the door
or it didn't. That's why the saying goes 'profit is an opinion, cash is a fact.' The income statement
is the story the company tells about its money; the cash flow statement is where the money
actually went. So when the two diverge, I follow the cash, because the cash can't give a press
conference. That said, I don't read any one statement alone — the balance sheet often reveals
what both of the others hide, like receivables outrunning sales. But if you forced me to anchor on
one, it's operating cash flow, because it's the hardest of the three to manipulate and therefore
the closest to economic truth.

> Look for: Chooses cash flow with clear reasoning · Explains profit is estimate-laden and manipulable · Uses the 'opinion vs fact' principle · Notes the statements should be read together


### 4. Tell me about a time you dug past the headline numbers and found
something others had missed.
Behavioural question testing genuine forensic instinct.
