## Interview Questions

### 1. Why do you say the biggest threat to an analyst is their own mind?
Conceptual question testing the core framing.

**Strong answer.** Because we run on cognitive hardware shaped for survival, not for rational
investing, and it's riddled with systematic, predictable bugs — cognitive biases. Kahneman's
framework explains it: we have a fast, automatic, intuitive System 1 and a slow, deliberate, lazy
System 2, and biases are errors System 1 makes that System 2 fails to catch. These aren't signs
of stupidity; they're universal, the same in a novice and a Nobel laureate, and they fire below
conscious awareness, especially under the fear and greed that markets generate. So the data
can be good and the analysis sound, and the decision still gets wrecked because the mind
making it is biased. And the most uncomfortable part is that knowing about the biases barely
helps — Kahneman himself admitted he stayed as susceptible as anyone. That's why I think of
my own mind as the least trustworthy instrument in my toolkit, and why the whole discipline is
about building systems that protect me from it rather than trusting myself to be rational.

> Look for: Identifies the mind as the core threat · Explains System 1/System 2 · Notes biases are universal and unconscious · States knowing doesn't cure them


### 2. Walk me through the key biases that wreck investment decisions.
Tests knowledge of the bug list.


**Strong answer.** The big ones I watch for: ANCHORING — over-relying on the first number I see,
like my purchase price or a stock's high, even though the market doesn't care what I paid.
CONFIRMATION BIAS — seeking evidence for my view and dismissing the rest, so 'research'
becomes a search for reassurance. LOSS AVERSION — losses hurt about twice as much as equal
gains feel good, which makes me hold losers too long to avoid realising the pain and sell winners
too early to lock in the gain, the disposition effect. RECENCY BIAS — overweighting recent events
and extrapolating them forever, so a few bad months feel permanent. OVERCONFIDENCE —
overestimating my knowledge and accuracy, which is dangerous because it drives oversized
bets. And HERDING — following the crowd because standing apart is uncomfortable, mistaking
consensus for safety. There are others — hindsight bias, availability, the narrative fallacy — but
those six do most of the damage in investing. The key thing is that each of them feels, from the
inside, like good judgment: loss aversion feels like conviction, confirmation feels like
thoroughness, herding feels like prudence. That's what makes them so hard to catch.

> Look for: Names multiple key biases accurately · Explains the effect of each · Notes loss aversion's disposition effect · Observes biases feel like good judgment from inside


### 3. If knowing about biases doesn't fix them, what actually works?
Opinion question testing the systems-over-willpower thesis.

**Strong answer.** Systems — external structures that catch the error mechanically, outside my
biased in-the-moment judgment. Since I can't fix the hardware and can't reliably catch a bias in
real time, especially under emotion, the only thing that works is to move the decision into a
structure I build in advance, in calm, by my most rational self. Concretely: a checklist I run on
every decision, so I can't skip the step a bias wants me to skip — the same way surgeons and
pilots defeat their own fallible memory under pressure. Kill criteria written before I buy, so the
exit is a pre-made rational decision rather than a live emotional one. A decision journal that
records my reasoning, confidence and emotional state, so I can catch hindsight bias and see my
own patterns. And a cooling-off rule, so I never act in the grip of fear or euphoria, which is exactly
when the biases fire hardest. The meta-principle is that I don't try to be unbiased — I assume I'm
biased right now and build the machine that decides well anyway. Willpower fails under pressure;
systems don't depend on me being calm exactly when I can't be.

> Look for: States systems are the answer · Explains why external structures work · Gives concrete systems (checklist, kill criteria, journal, cooling-off) · Articulates 'assume you're biased' meta-principle


### 4. Tell me about a time you caught yourself in a cognitive bias — or failed to.
Behavioural question testing genuine self-awareness.

**Strong answer.** The structure I'd highlight is the gap between how the bias felt and what it
actually was. In the case I'm thinking of, my analysis was pointing one way and my gut was
resisting, and the resistance felt like legitimate conviction or caution — but when I ran it through
a system rather than trusting the feeling, I recognised it as a bias. For instance, a reluctance to
sell that felt like conviction was actually loss aversion and anchoring once I asked myself whether
I'd buy the position at today's price with fresh money, and the honest answer was no. The key
lesson was that I could NOT have caught it by introspection alone, because from the inside it felt
completely rational — I needed the external test to surface it. I've also had times I failed to catch
a bias in the moment and only saw it later in review, which is exactly why I keep a decision
journal: it shows me my recurring patterns and turns generic knowledge of biases into knowing
the specific ones I'm personally prone to. The honest version of self-awareness, for me, is
distrusting a mind that always feels rational.

> Look for: Gives a concrete example · Shows the bias felt like good judgment · Notes a system (not introspection) surfaced it · Reflects on patterns / the journal


### 5. You've studied all the biases, so you're pretty much protected against them
now, right?
Trick question — the belief that knowledge protects is itself a bias.


**Strong answer.** No — and that belief is actually one of the most dangerous ones, because it's a
bias itself. Studying the biases produces a satisfying feeling of mastery, but knowing about a bias
does almost nothing to prevent it in the moment; the biases fire automatically, below conscious
awareness, especially under fear and greed, which is exactly when my rational mind is least able
to intervene. Kahneman spent his whole career on this and won a Nobel for it, and he freely
admitted he stayed as susceptible as anyone. There's also the bias blind spot: I can see biases
easily in others but I'm nearly blind to them in myself, because my own reasoning feels perfectly
rational from the inside. So thinking 'I'm protected because I know the list' is precisely the
overconfidence that leaves me exposed. The honest stance is the opposite: assume I'm biased
right now, especially when I feel most objective, and rely on external systems — checklists, kill
criteria, a decision journal, cooling-off rules — to catch the errors my self-awareness can't.
Knowledge is a reason to build the systems, not a substitute for them.

> Look for: Rejects the premise firmly · Notes knowing doesn't prevent biases · Cites the bias blind spot / Kahneman · Concludes with assume-you're-biased + systems


### 6. How would you counter loss aversion in your own decisions?
Tests the practical counter to the costliest bias.

**Strong answer.** The core technique is a reframe that bypasses the emotion: the decision to
HOLD a position is economically identical to the decision to BUY it at today's price. So for any
holding, especially one that's down, I ask 'knowing what I know now, would I buy this at today's
price with fresh money?' If the answer is no, then holding isn't conviction — it's loss aversion and
anchoring wearing conviction's clothes, and I should sell. That question neutralises the
purchase-price anchor, because the market has no memory of what I paid and 'getting back to
even' is a purely emotional target. I'd back that up with kill criteria set in advance, so the exit on
a broken thesis is a pre-made rational decision rather than a live emotional one, and a cooling-off
rule, because the pain of a realised loss is exactly when loss aversion fires hardest. And I'd
journal the decision, including the reluctance I felt, so I can see my own loss-aversion pattern
over time. The thing I'd never do is trust the feeling of conviction in the moment right after a loss,
because that's the bias's favourite disguise.

> Look for: States the 'would I buy it here?' reframe · Explains it neutralises the purchase-price anchor · Adds kill criteria and cooling-off · Notes the feeling of conviction is the bias's disguise


### 7. Aren't checklists and rigid processes the enemy of good judgment and
intuition?
Opinion question steelmanning the anti-systems view.

**Strong answer.** It's a fair worry, but I'd argue systems PROTECT good judgment rather than
replace it. The point of a checklist isn't to make decisions for me mechanically — it's to make
sure I actually apply my judgment to every relevant factor instead of skipping the one a bias
wants me to skip. Surgeons and pilots are highly skilled experts, and checklists make them
better, not worse, precisely because expertise doesn't protect you from a fallible memory under
pressure. Intuition has real value — it's pattern recognition built from experience — but the
problem is that from the inside, a genuine expert intuition and a bias feel exactly the same, so I
can't tell them apart by feel in the moment. The systems are how I distinguish them: if my
intuition survives the checklist, the disconfirmation step and the 'would I buy it here?' test, then
it's probably real signal and I should trust it. If it can't survive those, it was probably a bias. So I'm
not anti-intuition; I'm anti-trusting-intuition-blindly. The system is a filter that lets the good
judgment through and catches the bias, and the best investors are process-driven not because
they lack judgment but because they've accepted their in-the-moment judgment is corruptible.

> Look for: Concedes the concern about rigidity · Reframes systems as protecting/filtering judgment · Notes intuition and bias feel identical from inside · Concludes systems let good judgment through and catch bias


### 8. What's the difference between being really self-aware and just 'knowing
about biases'?
Tests the distinction between genuine and illusory self-awareness.

**Strong answer.** Just 'knowing about biases' is reciting the list and feeling protected by it —
which, ironically, is itself a bias, the overconfidence that leaves you most exposed. Everyone in
finance will say 'I know I'm biased', and it changes almost nothing about their behaviour. Genuine
self-awareness is different in two ways. First, it's humbler: instead of 'I know my biases', it's the
working assumption that 'I am biased right now, especially when I feel objective', and the
discipline to distrust a mind that always feels rational from the inside. Second, it's specific and
earned, not generic: through a decision journal reviewed over time, you discover the particular
biases YOU are personally most prone to — maybe you anchor heavily, or you're especially prone
to herding — which is the only path from textbook knowledge to real self-knowledge. And
crucially, genuine self-awareness expresses itself in SYSTEMS, not in introspection — because the
truly self-aware person knows they can't catch their own biases by looking inward, so they build
external structures to do it for them. So the test isn't whether you can name the biases; it's
whether you've built the machine that protects you from a mind you've admitted you can't trust.

> Look for: Notes 'knowing biases' is shallow / itself a bias · Reframes genuine self-awareness as humility (assume biased now) · Notes it's specific/earned via the journal · Connects it to building systems not introspection


### 9. Which bias do you think is most dangerous for investors, and why?
Opinion question; a strong answer reasons toward loss aversion or overconfidence.

**Strong answer.** I'd argue loss aversion is among the most dangerous, with overconfidence
close behind. Loss aversion is so costly because the asymmetry — losses hurting about twice as
much as equal gains feel good — produces exactly the wrong behaviour systematically: holding
losers all the way down to avoid the pain of realising them, and selling winners too early to lock
in the relief of a gain. That single pattern, the disposition effect, quietly destroys returns, and it
hides behind the most expensive sentence in investing, 'I'll sell when it gets back to even,' which
has trapped investors in failing positions to zero. Overconfidence is the other contender because
it's the bias that makes all the others worse: it inflates your conviction, drives oversized bets, and
underlies the dangerous belief that knowing about biases protects you. If I had to pick one to
defend against most rigorously, it'd be loss aversion, because it operates at the exact moment of
maximum emotion — a realised loss — when judgment is most corrupted, and because it has a
clean structural counter in the 'would I buy it here?' test. But honestly the deeper answer is that
they rarely act alone; like Munger's lollapalooza, they cluster and reinforce each other, which is
what makes a falling position you own so dangerous.

> Look for: Makes a reasoned choice (loss aversion/overconfidence) · Explains the disposition effect or overconfidence's amplifying role · Notes the structural counter · Observes biases cluster/reinforce (lollapalooza)


### 10. How does AI change the bias problem for analysts?
Forward-looking opinion tying to the module's AI theme.

**Strong answer.** It's genuinely promising as a debiasing tool, but with a catch. The promise is
that AI is, in a sense, an external system that doesn't share my biases — it has no money in my
position, no ego attached to my thesis, no pain from my losses — so it can be a powerful
debiasing partner. I can ask it to build the bear case I'm avoiding, to challenge my anchoring, to
point out where my reasoning looks like confirmation bias, to run my decision against a checklist
— and it'll do it without the loss aversion or wishful thinking that distorts my own judgment.
That's a real edge, because one of the hardest disciplines, genuinely seeking disconfirmation,
becomes available on demand from something that won't flinch. The catch is twofold. First, AI
has its own failure modes — it can be confidently wrong, and it can tell me what it thinks I want
to hear if I prompt it leadingly, which just launders my bias back to me with false authority.
Second, and more importantly, the hardest part of debiasing was never generating the contrary

view — it's having the emotional discipline to ACT on it when fear and ego are screaming
otherwise, and no AI installs that discipline for me; I still have to sell the loser myself. So I'd use
AI aggressively as an unbiased external check — a tireless devil's advocate and checklist-runner
— while remembering that the final, emotional act of overriding my own bias remains stubbornly
human. The tool can show me the bias; only I can choose to act against it.

> Look for: Notes AI is a useful external debiasing tool without my stake · Gives concrete uses (bear case, challenge anchoring, run checklist) · Flags AI's own failure modes / leading prompts laundering bias · Concludes the emotional discipline to act remains human


---

## Simulation — The Genius Feeling — Debugging a 5-Bagger

*HARD · 10 min · decision making*


**Scenario.** You're an analyst at a Mumbai fund. Eighteen months ago you made a
high-conviction call on a stock that has since gone up 5x — it's the best idea of your career,
it's made the fund's year, and you feel, frankly, brilliant. The whole desk is euphoric. The
stock is now the most talked-about name in the market, retail and institutions are piling in,
and the recent gains have everyone convinced it's going much higher. Your PM is energised:
'We've been spectacularly right on this — let's add aggressively and let it run. We clearly
understand this better than anyone.' You're tempted to agree and double down. But a quiet
discomfort makes you pause: the stock, after a 5x, now trades at a valuation that prices in
years of flawless execution, and the feeling in the room is exactly the euphoria the bias
session warned about. You have ten minutes to debug your own mind before deciding. The
question is not 'were we right?' (you were) — it's whether the urge to add aggressively NOW
is analysis, or a cluster of biases firing at the top.

**Your task.** Don't trust the genius feeling. Run the debugging systems. (1) Name the biases
the euphoria is triggering: OVERCONFIDENCE (a great OUTCOME is making you feel you have
great skill and understanding — distinguish luck/being-right-once from a repeatable edge),
RECENCY (extrapolating the recent 5x run forever), HERDING (everyone piling in feels like
confirmation), and RESULTING in reverse (feeling smart because of the result, not the
reasoning). (2) Run the 'WOULD I BUY IT HERE?' test in the UPSIDE direction: forget that you
own it and that it's up 5x — with fresh money, knowing the current valuation prices in years
of perfection, would you BUY a full new position at today's price? If not, then 'add
aggressively' is bias, not analysis. (3) Apply the CAPITAL-ALLOCATION / valuation discipline
(Session 9): a wonderful company is not a wonderful purchase at any price — what does
today's price imply about future growth, and is that realistic? (4) Apply the COOLING-OFF
rule: euphoria is the up-side twin of panic, and biases fire hardest at emotional extremes — is
this the moment to make a big, irreversible add? (5) Give your PM a debiased
recommendation — not 'we're geniuses, double down' but what a cold, purchase-price-blind,
systems-driven analysis says about adding, holding, or trimming at this valuation — and what
would change your view. Write a short note.

**Objectives**

- Recognise that biases fire in euphoria/booms, not just in losses
- Name the upside biases — overconfidence, recency, herding, resulting-in-reverse
- Run the 'would I buy it here?' test on a big winner to separate analysis from bias
- Apply valuation/capital-allocation discipline — a great company isn't a great buy at any price
- Use the cooling-off rule before a big, hard-to-reverse add at an emotional extreme
- Deliver a debiased recommendation distinguishing being-right from a repeatable edge
Decision points

- Is the urge to 'add aggressively' driven by fresh analysis, or by the feeling of being right
(overconfidence/resulting)?
- After a 5x, with fresh money, would you buy a full new position at today's valuation?
- Does the current price imply realistic future growth, or years of perfection?
- Is making a big, hard-to-reverse add at peak euphoria the right moment, or exactly when biases fire
hardest?

How a top analyst would think it through. A strong response does NOT agree to
'double down because we're clearly geniuses' — that's the euphoria pole of bias, and
the key insight this sim tests is that biases fire just as hard in booms as in busts, only
they feel like justified confidence instead of fear. The analyst debugs the cluster.
OVERCONFIDENCE / RESULTING-IN-REVERSE: a spectacular OUTCOME is creating the
FEELING of great skill and understanding, but being right once (which always involves
some luck) is not proof of a repeatable edge or that the analyst 'understands this
better than anyone' — feeling smart because of the result is resulting, the same error
as feeling stupid after an unlucky loss, just inverted and more pleasant. RECENCY:
extrapolating the recent 5x run forever is textbook recency bias; the recent past is not
the future. HERDING: everyone piling in feels like confirmation but is actually the
social-proof signal that the trade is crowded and the price may reflect mania, not
value. The decisive system is the 'WOULD I BUY IT HERE?' test run in the UPSIDE
direction: stripping away the ownership and the 5x gain, with fresh money and
knowing the valuation now prices in years of flawless execution, would the analyst
BUY a full new position at today's price? If the honest answer is no — and at a
valuation pricing in perfection it likely is — then 'add aggressively' is bias, not analysis,
because adding is economically identical to buying at today's price. Then the
valuation/capital-allocation discipline from Session 9: a wonderful company is not a
wonderful purchase at any price, so the analyst asks what today's price implies about
future growth and whether that's realistic, recognising that paying up for even a great
business at a price that embeds perfection is a poor expected-value bet. The
COOLING-OFF rule applies: euphoria is panic's twin, and a big, hard-to-reverse add at
an emotional extreme is exactly the decision to slow down. The debiased
recommendation is likely: do NOT add aggressively at this valuation, consider
trimming to a sensible position size (taking some of a career-best win off the table is
prudent, not disloyal to the thesis), hold a core if the long-term thesis genuinely
justifies the price, and journal the euphoria and the reasoning so the process can be
reviewed regardless of where the stock goes next. The falsification condition: if a cold,
purchase-price-blind valuation shows the price still reasonable versus realistic growth,
adding could be justified. Traps to name: overconfidence from a good outcome,
resulting-in-reverse, recency, herding, and confusing a great company with a great
purchase. The lesson: the genius feeling at the top is a bias cluster in disguise — trust
the systems, not the feeling, in euphoria as much as in fear.

Hints
- Feeling brilliant after a 5x — is that evidence of skill, or the pleasant version of resulting?
- Forget you own it and that it's up 5x. With fresh money, would you BUY a full position here?
- The crowd piling in feels like confirmation. Is that signal, or social proof that the trade is crowded?
- A great company is not a great purchase at any price. What does today's valuation imply about
future growth?
- Biases fire hardest at emotional extremes — euphoria as much as panic. Is now the moment for a
big, irreversible add?


---

## Session 11 — Decision-Making Under Uncertainty — The High-Stakes Call
You will never have all the information. Frameworks for deciding anyway: conviction
versus flexibility, how much certainty is enough, and how to act decisively without being
reckless.


**In this session**

- Decide and act on incomplete information
- Calibrate conviction to evidence
- Balance commitment with willingness to update
- Apply a repeatable decision framework to a live call


### [HOOK] The Call You Make in the Fog
This is the call of your career. After months of work, you've found what looks like a genuinely
asymmetric opportunity — a beaten-down company where, if your thesis is right, the upside
is enormous, and if you're careful about how much you commit, the downside is survivable.
But there is a chasm at the centre of the thesis that no amount of further analysis can close:
it hinges on something genuinely unknowable — how a regulatory decision lands, whether a
turnaround takes hold, how a new technology plays out. You have done the work. You have
read everything. And you are still, irreducibly, uncertain. Now you feel the pull every analyst
knows: 'Let me wait for just a little more clarity before I commit.' It feels prudent. But here is
the trap — the clarity you are waiting for will only arrive AFTER the opportunity has passed;
by the time it's obvious, the price will already reflect it, and the asymmetry you found will be
gone. Certainty and opportunity exist at opposite ends of time. So you face the real test of an
analyst, the one this entire module has been preparing you for: not how to be certain — you
cannot be — but how to make a high-stakes, hard-to-reverse decision WELL, in the fog,
knowing you might be wrong, in a way that wins big if you're right and lets you survive if
you're not. The analyst who waits for certainty never makes the call. The great one decides
well without it.

The question. Every decision that matters is made with incomplete information
about an unknowable future, where being wrong has a real cost — and waiting
for certainty just means missing the opportunity, because certainty never comes
in time. So how do you make the high-stakes call WELL when you cannot make it
with certainty?

Why it matters. This is where everything comes together. Real analyst decisions — the
ones that define a career — are never made with complete information or a knowable future;
they are made under irreducible uncertainty, with real consequences for being wrong. The
instinct to wait for certainty is a trap, because the certainty arrives only after the opportunity
has gone. The defining skill of a top-tier analyst is therefore not predicting the future or
eliminating uncertainty, but making high-conviction, high-stakes decisions WELL despite it —
synthesising every tool in this module into a single, repeatable framework for the calls that
matter most.


### [CONCEPT] You Decide in the Fog, or You Don't Decide

There is a fundamental distinction at the heart of all serious decision-making, drawn by the
economist Frank Knight: the difference between RISK and UNCERTAINTY. RISK is when the
odds are knowable — a dice roll, a roulette wheel — and you can compute the right bet.
UNCERTAINTY is when the odds are genuinely unknown and unknowable — and almost every
decision that matters in investing lives here. You cannot compute your way to the answer
because the probabilities themselves are estimates in the fog; the future is not a known
distribution but a genuinely open question. This has a liberating and a demanding
implication. The liberating part: since certainty is impossible, you are FREED from the
impossible standard of being right — you cannot be expected to predict an unknowable
future. The demanding part: you must therefore make decisions, real and consequential
ones, WITHOUT the certainty your mind craves, and the waiting-for-certainty instinct must be
recognised as the trap it is. Here three ideas reframe the whole problem. FIRST, indecision is
itself a decision — usually a bad one. Choosing not to act, or waiting for more information, is
a choice with its own cost: opportunities pass, and as we saw, the clarifying information
arrives only after the opportunity is gone. There is no neutral 'wait and see' that escapes the
decision; you are deciding either way. SECOND, not all decisions deserve the same caution —
Jeff Bezos's distinction between one-way and two-way doors. A REVERSIBLE decision (a
two-way door) should be made fast and cheaply, because if it's wrong you simply walk back
through; agonising over reversible decisions is a waste. An IRREVERSIBLE, high-stakes
decision (a one-way door) deserves real care, a higher bar, and more deliberation — and this
session is about those. THIRD, and most important, conviction is not certainty. High
conviction under uncertainty does NOT mean you know you're right; it means you've done
the work, the asymmetry favours you, and you're willing to act — while holding,
simultaneously, the humility that you might be wrong. That dual stance — act with
conviction, prepare to be wrong — is the entire art, and it's why the great decision-maker
both commits decisively AND sets kill criteria, sizes for survival, and judges herself on
process rather than outcome. You will decide in the fog. The only question is whether you
decide well.

Analogy. A surgeon operating on a patient never has complete information —
the scans are imperfect, the body holds surprises, and the textbook can't tell her
exactly what she'll find when she opens up. She cannot wait for certainty; the
patient is on the table and not deciding is itself a fatal decision. So she does
what the great decision-maker does: she prepares meticulously, she has a plan
and contingency plans, she commits to the cut with full conviction, and she
stays ready to adapt the instant reality contradicts the plan. She is not certain;
she is prepared and decisive. That is the posture of every high-stakes call made
under uncertainty — not the false confidence of knowing, but the earned
confidence of having done the work and being ready to be wrong.


**Key Terms**

Risk vs uncertainty (Knight) — Risk has knowable odds; uncertainty has unknowable ones,
and most real decisions are uncertain.
The certainty trap — Waiting for certainty fails because the clarifying information arrives only
after the opportunity is gone.
Indecision is a decision — Choosing not to act is itself a consequential choice, not a neutral
'wait and see'.
One-way vs two-way doors (Bezos) — Reversible decisions made fast; irreversible,
high-stakes ones made with a higher bar.

Conviction is not certainty — High conviction means the work is done and the asymmetry
favours you, held with the humility you may be wrong.


### [CONCEPT] The High-Stakes Decision Framework
Here is a repeatable framework for the high-stakes call — a synthesis of this entire module,
run as a sequence on any decision that matters. STEP 1 — FRAME IT (first principles, Session
2). Before anything, ask what you are ACTUALLY deciding. Strip the decision to its core
question, free of the narrative and the noise. Most bad decisions are answers to the wrong
question; getting the frame right is half the work. STEP 2 — MAP THE ASYMMETRY
(probabilistic thinking, Session 4). Estimate the rough odds and base rates, but focus above
all on the PAYOFF ASYMMETRY: what do you gain if right, what do you lose if wrong? The goal
is to find bets where the downside is limited and the upside is large — you do not need to be
more right than wrong, you need to win much more when right than you lose when wrong.
STEP 3 — THINK AROUND CORNERS (second-order, Session 3). Ask 'and then what?' Trace
the consequences of the consequences, the reactions of other players, the second- and
third-order effects the first-level view misses. STEP 4 — RUN THE PRE-MORTEM (inversion,
Session 6). Assume the decision has failed catastrophically and ask why. Surface the ways it
breaks, then set KILL CRITERIA in advance — the specific, pre-committed conditions under
which you will exit — so that being wrong is a managed event, not a catastrophe. STEP 5 —
DEBUG FOR BIAS (Session 10). Run your systems: are you anchored, overconfident, seeking
confirmation, herding? Is this conviction or is it a bias wearing conviction's clothes? Use the
checklist and the disconfirmation step before you commit. STEP 6 — SIZE FOR SURVIVAL
(position sizing, Sessions 4 & 6). This is the step most people skip: the decision is not binary
(do it / don't), it is 'HOW MUCH?' Size the commitment to BOTH your conviction AND the
consequence of being wrong, under one inviolable rule — never make a bet that, if it goes
wrong, takes you out of the game. Survival comes first; an asymmetric upside is worthless if
a bad outcome is fatal. STEP 7 — DECIDE, ACT, AND MONITOR. Once the process is run,
COMMIT — decisively, without agonising further, because the analysis is done and indecision
is its own (bad) decision. Then monitor the position against your kill criteria, ready to act if
they trigger. And crucially, judge yourself afterwards on whether you ran this PROCESS well,
not on how the uncertain outcome happened to land (Session 4's resulting). That is the whole
framework: frame, map asymmetry, think around corners, pre-mortem, debias, size for
survival, commit and monitor. It does not make you certain. It makes you DECIDE WELL
under uncertainty — which is the most you can ever do, and the thing that separates the
top-tier analyst from everyone waiting for a clarity that will never come.

Analogy. A mountaineer deciding whether to make a summit push faces
exactly this: incomplete information (the weather forecast is a probability, not a
promise), an unknowable future, and a real cost of being wrong (the mountain
kills the reckless). The great mountaineer doesn't wait for a guarantee of good
weather — it never comes — nor charge blindly upward. She runs the
framework: she frames the real question (summit or survive?), weighs the
asymmetry (a summit is glory; a storm at altitude is death), thinks ahead (if the
weather turns at the top, then what?), pre-commits to a turnaround time (her kill
criterion — the hardest discipline on the mountain, because ego fights it),
checks her own summit-fever bias, sizes the risk to what she can survive, and
then commits to the push with full conviction while watching the sky. Most who
die on mountains die not from bad luck but from skipping a step — usually the
turnaround time. The framework is how you climb the hard decisions without
falling off them.


**Key Terms**

Frame it — Strip the decision to its real core question (first principles); most bad decisions
answer the wrong question.
Map the asymmetry — Seek limited-downside, large-upside bets; win big when right rather
than merely being right more often.
Pre-mortem & kill criteria — Assume failure, find why, pre-commit exit conditions so being
wrong is managed, not fatal.
Size for survival — The decision is 'how much?', sized to conviction and consequence, under
the inviolable rule of avoiding ruin.
Decide on process, accept the outcome — Judge the quality of the process, not how the
uncertain outcome happened to land.


### [VISUAL] The High-Stakes Decision Framework
Visual: decision framework flow
Draw a vertical flow of seven connected boxes, each a step of the framework, with the
contributing module noted on each: STEP 1 'FRAME IT — what am I really deciding? (first
principles, S2)'; STEP 2 'MAP THE ASYMMETRY — gain if right vs lose if wrong (probability,
S4)'; STEP 3 'THINK AROUND CORNERS — and then what? (second-order, S3)'; STEP 4
'PRE-MORTEM + KILL CRITERIA — assume it failed; set exits (inversion, S6)'; STEP 5 'DEBUG
FOR BIAS — conviction or bias? run the systems (S10)'; STEP 6 'SIZE FOR SURVIVAL — how
much? never risk ruin (sizing, S4/S6)'; STEP 7 'DECIDE, ACT & MONITOR — commit; watch the
kill criteria; judge the process (S4)'. To one side, draw two contrasting figures: 'WAITS FOR
CERTAINTY -> never decides -> opportunity passes' (greyed out, stuck) and 'DECIDES WELL
IN THE FOG -> acts with conviction + humility -> wins big or survives' (active, moving
forward). At the bottom, a banner with the survival rule: 'SURVIVE FIRST: an asymmetric
upside is worthless if being wrong is fatal.' And a final line: 'The framework doesnt make you
certain — it makes you decide WELL.'
Elements
- THE SEVEN STEPS: frame, map asymmetry, think around corners, pre-mortem + kill
criteria, debug bias, size for survival, decide & monitor.
- MODULE SYNTHESIS: each step draws on a prior session (S2, S4, S3, S6, S10, S4/S6, S4) —
the whole module in one process.
- TWO PATHS: the one who waits for certainty (stuck, misses it) vs the one who decides well
in the fog (acts, wins big or survives).
- THE SURVIVAL RULE (banner): never make a bet that ruins you if wrong; survival precedes
upside.
- CONVICTION + HUMILITY: act decisively AND prepare to be wrong (kill criteria, sizing) —
the dual stance.
- TAKEAWAY: the framework doesn't deliver certainty; it delivers a good decision under
irreducible uncertainty.


### [DEEP DIVE] The Anatomy of a High-Stakes Call

Survive first: the one rule that overrides all others
Of every principle in decision-making under uncertainty, one sits above the rest: SURVIVE.
Never make a bet that, if it goes wrong, takes you out of the game — because in a world of
genuine uncertainty, where any single outcome can surprise you, the player who gets wiped
out doesn't get to play the next hand, and it's the next hand, and the thousand after it, where
the edge compounds. This is why an asymmetric upside, however dazzling, is worthless if the
downside is fatal: a bet that offers a hundredfold return but carries a real chance of ruin is a
bad bet, because ruin is forever and you only need to hit it once. Nassim Taleb's framing is
sharpest here — before you ask 'what's the expected return?', ask 'can this kill me?', and if
the answer is yes, the expected return is irrelevant. Practically, this means sizing every
high-stakes decision so that being wrong is painful but survivable, never catastrophic;
keeping reserves; avoiding the leverage and concentration that turn a recoverable mistake
into a terminal one. The greatest analysts are not the ones who made the boldest single bet
— those people are mostly forgotten, having eventually bet too big and blown up. They are
the ones who stayed in the game for decades by never letting a single decision, however
confident, threaten their survival. First survive; then, and only then, thrive.

Asymmetry: how to win without being right more often
The most liberating idea in high-stakes decision-making is that you do not need to be right
more often than you are wrong — you need to WIN MORE when you're right than you lose
when you're wrong. This is the principle of asymmetry, and it changes everything about how
you choose decisions. A bet that loses a little most of the time but wins enormously
occasionally can be hugely profitable even with a low hit rate; a bet that wins a little most of
the time but loses catastrophically occasionally is a slow path to ruin, regardless of how often
it 'works'. So the high-stakes decision-maker hunts specifically for asymmetric situations:
limited, defined downside and large, open-ended upside. She is happy to be wrong often if
the structure means her losses are small and her wins are large. This connects directly to
survival: the ideal bet is one where the downside is capped at something survivable and the
upside is many times that. Framing decisions this way also dissolves the paralysis of needing
to be certain — if the asymmetry is strong enough, you can act on a thesis you're only
moderately confident in, because the math works even when you're frequently wrong. You
are no longer trying to predict; you are trying to find favourable asymmetries and size them
to survive.

Conviction without certainty, and the courage to act
The high-stakes call demands a psychological posture most people find almost impossible: to
hold strong conviction and genuine humility at the same time. Too much certainty and you
bet too big, ignore disconfirming evidence, and blow up when the unknowable goes against
you. Too much humility and you never act, paralysed by the uncertainty that can never be
removed, watching every opportunity pass while you wait for a clarity that won't come. The
resolution is to redefine what conviction means under uncertainty. Conviction is NOT the
belief that you're right; it's the judgment that you've done the work, the asymmetry favours
you, and the bet is worth making AT A SIZE YOU CAN SURVIVE — held together with the full
awareness that you might be wrong, which is precisely why you've set your kill criteria and
sized for survival. This dual stance is what lets you act decisively without being reckless. And
act you must: once the framework has been run, further deliberation is not prudence, it's
paralysis, and paralysis is itself a decision with a cost. The courage to commit in the fog —
having done the work, found the asymmetry, and protected your survival — is the final,
hardest, and most defining skill of the top-tier analyst. The market does not reward the most
certain; it rewards those who decide well, repeatedly, under conditions where certainty was
never on offer.

Decide on process, accept the outcome
Under irreducible uncertainty, there is no escaping the cruel truth from the probability
session: a great decision can have a bad outcome, and a terrible decision can have a great
one, because the outcome is the decision filtered through chance you couldn't control. This
has a profound consequence for how you must judge a high-stakes call. You CANNOT judge it
by its outcome, because the outcome was partly luck; you can only judge it by the quality of
the PROCESS — did you frame it right, map the asymmetry honestly, think around corners,
pre-mortem it, debug your biases, size for survival, and commit? If you ran the process well
and the unknowable went against you, you made a GOOD decision that got unlucky, and the
correct response is to repeat the process, not abandon it. If you skipped the steps and got
lucky, you made a BAD decision that the market happened to reward — and learning the
wrong lesson from that luck is how people blow up later. This 'process over outcome'

discipline is what makes decisiveness under uncertainty psychologically possible: you are not
promising yourself or anyone else that you'll be right — you can't, the future is unknowable
— you are promising only to decide WELL, again and again, trusting that a sound process
applied to favourable asymmetries, sized for survival, compounds into success over a career
even though any single outcome is uncertain. That promise you can actually keep, and it's
the only one worth making.
Real-world references
- Frank Knight — 'Risk, Uncertainty and Profit', the foundational distinction between measurable risk
and true uncertainty.
- Nassim Taleb — survival, ruin, and asymmetry; 'never cross a river that is on average four feet
deep'.
- Annie Duke — 'Thinking in Bets', decision quality versus outcome quality under uncertainty.
- Jeff Bezos — the one-way vs two-way door framework for reversible and irreversible decisions.
- Howard Marks — 'you can't predict, you can prepare'; investing as decision-making under irreducible
uncertainty.


### [EXAMPLE] Running the Framework on a Binary Bet
Company / context: A beaten-down pharma company facing a binary regulatory overhang
(illustrative)
Situation. A mid-sized Indian pharmaceutical company has been hammered — the stock is
down 60% — because its key manufacturing facility received a regulatory observation that
could escalate to an import ban, or could be resolved after remediation. The entire
investment case hinges on a genuinely unknowable binary: will the regulator clear the facility
after the company's remediation, or not? If cleared, the stock could double or more as the
overhang lifts; if banned, it could fall much further. You've done months of work. You still
cannot know how the regulator will rule. You must decide whether to make this high-stakes,
hard-to-reverse call — and if so, how.
Data. What the work has established: the business ex-overhang is sound and cheap (the
market is pricing in a high probability of the bad outcome); the company has a credible
remediation track record and the observations appear addressable, though resolution is
genuinely uncertain and not in your control; the timeline to resolution is months, not years;
and the position, if taken, would be hard to exit quickly without the overhang resolving. The
one thing you cannot get, no matter how much more you analyse, is certainty about the
regulator's decision.
Analysis. Run the framework. STEP 1 FRAME (S2): the real decision is not 'is this a good
company?' (it is) but 'is the asymmetric payoff on an unknowable binary, sized for survival,
worth making?' STEP 2 ASYMMETRY (S4): map the payoffs — if cleared, perhaps a
double-plus; if banned, a large further loss. Critically, because the market has already priced
in a high chance of the bad outcome, the stock is cheap enough that the upside if cleared
may far exceed the additional downside if banned — a favourable asymmetry — and you
don't need to know the outcome, only that the odds and payoffs are skewed in your favour
relative to the price. STEP 3 AROUND CORNERS (S3): and then what? If cleared, does the
re-rating hold, or are there second-order issues (capacity, pricing)? If banned, how much is
already in the price, and could it fall further as forced sellers exit? STEP 4 PRE-MORTEM +
KILL CRITERIA (S6): assume it's a disaster a year out — why? Most likely the ban came AND
you'd sized it too big OR had no exit plan. So set kill criteria in advance: a maximum position
size, and a pre-committed response to each resolution (e.g. trim into strength if cleared, exit
discipline if the situation deteriorates further before resolution). STEP 5 DEBUG BIAS (S10):

am I anchored to the pre-crash price (it's 'cheap' versus a number that no longer matters)?
Am I seeking only the bullish remediation takes? Run the disconfirmation step — steelman
the ban case honestly. STEP 6 SIZE FOR SURVIVAL (S4/S6): THE crucial step — because the
bad outcome is a real possibility and the position is hard to exit, size it SMALL enough that a
ban is painful but survivable, never a position that ruins the portfolio if the binary goes
against you. The asymmetric upside is only worth capturing at a size you can survive being
wrong on. STEP 7 DECIDE, ACT, MONITOR (S4): if the asymmetry is favourable and you've
sized for survival, COMMIT — take the position decisively rather than waiting for a clarity that
will only come after the opportunity is gone — then monitor against the kill criteria, and judge
yourself afterwards on whether this process was sound, NOT on whether the regulator
happened to rule your way. The framework converts what looks like a reckless gamble on an
unknowable binary into a well-made decision: a favourable asymmetry, sized so that being
wrong doesn't end you, committed to decisively, with pre-set exits.
Analyst takeaway. A high-stakes call on an unknowable binary is not a gamble if you run
the framework: frame the real question, confirm the asymmetry is in your favour (especially
when the market has over-priced the bad outcome), pre-mortem it and set kill criteria, debug
your biases, and above all size it so that being wrong is survivable — then commit decisively,
because waiting for certainty means missing it. You will not know how the binary resolves;
you can still make the decision WELL. That distinction — between a gamble and a well-made
uncertain decision — is the whole craft. (Illustrative scenario to demonstrate the decision
framework; not a comment on any specific company, and not a recommendation.)

PITFALLS         How High-Stakes Decisions Go Wrong

### 1. Waiting for certainty before committing — mistaking indecision for prudence.
Why it happens: Uncertainty is deeply uncomfortable, and gathering more information feels responsible
and safe, so we keep waiting for a clarity that feels just around the corner.
Fix: Recognise that the certainty you're waiting for arrives only after the opportunity has gone, and
that indecision is itself a decision with a cost. Once you've run the framework, commit — decisiveness
under uncertainty is the skill, not a guarantee of being right. Reserve heavy deliberation for genuinely
irreversible decisions; act fast on reversible ones.


### 2. Sizing the bet to conviction alone, ignoring the consequence of being wrong.
Why it happens: Strong conviction makes a big bet feel justified, and the upside is exciting and vivid
while the possibility of ruin feels abstract and unlikely, so people bet big on their best ideas.
Fix: Size every high-stakes decision to BOTH conviction AND consequence, under the inviolable rule of
survival: never make a bet that, if wrong, takes you out of the game. An asymmetric upside is worthless
if the downside is fatal. Bet enough to matter if right, never enough to ruin you if wrong — because
under uncertainty, any bet can go against you.


### 3. Confusing conviction with certainty — and so ignoring disconfirming evidence
and skipping kill criteria.
Why it happens: It's psychologically easier to feel certain than to hold conviction and doubt at once, and
certainty feels like strength while preparing to be wrong feels like weakness or disloyalty to the thesis.
Fix: Redefine conviction: it means you've done the work and the asymmetry favours you, NOT that you
know you're right. Hold the strong view AND the humility together — which is exactly why you set kill
criteria, seek disconfirmation, and size for survival. Acting with conviction and preparing to be wrong
are not contradictory; together they are the entire art.


### 4. Judging the decision by its outcome rather than its process (resulting).
Why it happens: The outcome is concrete, emotional and final, while the quality of the process is
abstract, so we instinctively conclude a good outcome meant a good decision and a bad outcome a bad
one.
Fix: Under uncertainty, separate decision quality from outcome quality: a sound process can get
unlucky and a reckless one can get lucky. Judge a high-stakes call on whether you framed, mapped the
asymmetry, pre-mortemed, debiased, and sized for survival — not on how the unknowable happened to
land. Keep a decision journal so you can review the process honestly, and repeat good processes
regardless of single outcomes.


### [RECAP] The High-Stakes Call — Locking It In

**Key Takeaways**

- Real decisions are made under irreducible uncertainty; waiting for certainty means
missing the opportunity.
- Distinguish risk (knowable odds) from uncertainty (unknowable) — and indecision is itself
a decision with a cost.
- Reserve heavy deliberation for irreversible (one-way door) decisions; act fast on reversible
ones.
- The framework: frame it, map the asymmetry, think around corners, pre-mortem + kill
criteria, debug bias, size for survival, commit and monitor.
- Survive first: never make a bet that ruins you if wrong — an asymmetric upside is
worthless if the downside is fatal.
- Seek asymmetry: you don't need to be right more often, only to win much more when
right than you lose when wrong.
- Conviction is not certainty; decide on process, accept the outcome — you promise to
decide well, not to be right.

Analyst mindset. The top-tier analyst has made peace with the fog. She knows
certainty will never come in time, so she doesn't wait for it — she runs the
framework instead: framing the real question, hunting favourable asymmetries,
thinking around corners, pre-morteming the disaster and setting her exits,
debugging her own biases, and above all sizing every call so that being wrong is
survivable. She holds conviction and humility together, acts decisively once the
work is done, and judges herself on the quality of her process rather than the
verdict of a single uncertain outcome. She is not trying to predict the future or
eliminate uncertainty — she is trying to decide well, repeatedly, under
conditions where certainty was never on offer. That, in the end, is what thinking
like a top-tier analyst means.


**Connections**

- Session 2 (First-Principles) — framing the decision's real question is the first step of every
high-stakes call.
- Session 4 (Probabilistic Thinking) — asymmetry, position sizing, and process-over-outcome are the
core of the framework.
- Session 6 (Inversion & Pre-Mortem) — the pre-mortem, kill criteria, and avoiding ruin make being
wrong survivable.
- Session 10 (Debugging Your Brain) — the bias systems are run before committing to any high-stakes
decision.
