## Interview Questions

### 1. As AI automates analysis, what makes a human analyst irreplaceable?
The central question of the session.

**Strong answer.** The key is to separate analysis into two layers. The MECHANICS — gathering
data, building models, summarising filings, screening, computing, drafting — is being
commoditised by AI, becoming fast, cheap and abundant, and competing there is a losing game
because the machine is simply better at it. What remains durably human is the JUDGMENT layer
above the mechanics: framing the right questions, reasoning from first principles about
genuinely new situations, thinking in second- and third-order effects, weighing truly uncertain
situations, filtering the signal that matters from the noise, judging the character and incentives of
management, debugging your own biases, taking a contrarian view and having the courage to
act on it, and owning a consequential decision under uncertainty. These are the things AI can't do
— and not coincidentally, they're exactly the skills a good analyst develops. So I'd say what

makes a human irreplaceable is judgment, taste and the courage of ownership — the ability to
know which question matters, to reason where there's no precedent, to be correctly contrarian,
and to own a high-stakes call with your name on it. The machine can do the analysis; only a
human can do the thinking, and the thinking is the job.

> Look for: Separates mechanics (commoditised) from judgment (durable) · Lists specific human edges · Notes these are the analyst's developed skills · Concludes judgment/ownership is the irreplaceable core


### 2. How should an analyst actually use AI day to day?
Tests the practical human+AI division of labour.

**Strong answer.** As the most powerful tool I've ever had — a tireless junior that does my legwork
so I can spend my energy on judgment. Concretely, I'd delegate the mechanics to it:
summarising long filings and transcripts, building and stress-testing models, screening across a
universe, pulling together the existing research and news, doing the computation. That makes
me an order of magnitude faster at everything that used to eat my days. I'd also use it WITHIN
some of the judgment work — as a devil's advocate to challenge my thesis and surface what I
might have missed, as a way to generate the bear case I'm avoiding, as a sounding board for
second-order consequences. But I'd hold a hard line: AI does the work, I do the thinking. I apply
my own judgment to everything it produces, I own the conclusion, and I never let its fluent,
confident output substitute for my own reasoning — because it can be confidently wrong, it
reflects its training biases, and it'll tell me what I want to hear if I ask leadingly. So the model is:
machine does the mechanics and challenges me; I do the framing, the judgment, the contrarian
calls and the decision. I stand on the AI; I don't compete with it, and I don't surrender to it.

> Look for: Delegates mechanics to AI for leverage · Uses AI within judgment work (devil's advocate) · Holds the line: AI does work, human does thinking · Notes AI's failure modes


### 3. Are you worried AI will replace analysts like you?
Opinion question; a strong answer reframes the threat.

**Strong answer.** Not worried — but only because I understand where the threat actually is. AI
absolutely WILL replace one kind of analyst: the one who defines himself by the mechanics, who
competes by being a more thorough, faster version of what the machine does. That person is
racing the machine on the machine's home ground and will lose, and honestly should. But AI
won't replace the analyst who moves up to the judgment layer and uses AI as leverage — if
anything, it makes that analyst far more valuable, because it amplifies their judgment and frees
them from the grunt work. The way I think about it is the line: AI won't replace analysts, but
analysts who use AI will replace those who don't. So my job isn't to fear the machine; it's to make
sure I'm the analyst who stands on it rather than the one who competes with it — to keep
developing the questions, the first-principles thinking, the contrarian courage and the
decision-ownership that the machine can't touch, and to use AI relentlessly for everything else.
The threat is real, but it's specific and avoidable, and the response to it is exactly the kind of
thinking I've been training. Fear is the wrong reaction; moving up is the right one.

> Look for: Reframes the threat as specific to mechanics-competers · Notes AI elevates judgment-focused analysts · Uses the 'use AI / replace those who don't' framing · Frames the response as moving up not fearing


### 4. Tell me how you've used AI to make your work better — and where you've
been careful with it.
Behavioural question on real, judicious AI use.

**Strong answer.** The structure I'd highlight is using AI to compress the mechanical work and
reinvesting that time in judgment. For instance, I'd describe having it summarise a large volume
of filings or transcripts, build or check a model, or pull together the existing research, which
turned days of legwork into an hour and let me spend my real time on the questions that
mattered. I'd also mention using it as a challenger — asking it to argue the opposite of my view,
or to find the holes in my thesis, which is genuinely useful for fighting my own confirmation bias.

Where I've been careful is in never taking its output at face value: I've caught it being confidently
wrong, fabricating a detail, or simply giving me the consensus dressed up as insight, so I always
verify the facts it gives me and apply my own judgment to its conclusions rather than adopting
them. I'd also be honest that I watch for the subtler risk — letting it do my thinking — and
deliberately make sure I'm still reasoning things through myself rather than just passing along
what the machine said. So the pattern is: lean on it hard for the work, use it to challenge me, but
own the judgment and verify everything. It makes me faster and sharper, but the conclusions are
mine.

> Look for: Gives concrete uses (compress mechanics, challenger) · Reinvests freed time in judgment · Describes verifying output and not taking it at face value · Notes guarding against outsourcing thinking


### 5. AI gave you a thorough, confident analysis recommending this stock. That's
a solid basis for the call, isn't it?
Trick question — AI output is the consensus, not edge, and must be judged.

**Strong answer.** I'd be cautious about that, for two reasons. First, a thorough, confident AI
analysis is essentially a distillation of what's already been written and is widely believed — it's
the CONSENSUS, available to anyone with the same tool, not proprietary edge. If I make a call
simply because the AI articulated the consensus well, I'm doing what everyone else can do, and
edge comes from being correctly DIFFERENT from the crowd, not from echoing it. Second, AI's
confidence is not the same as being right — it can be confidently wrong, reflect biases in its
training, or tell me what I prompted it to conclude — so the fluency and confidence of the output
are not evidence of its correctness. So I wouldn't treat the AI's analysis as a basis for the call; I'd
treat it as a well-organised summary of the consensus and the available facts, which is genuinely
useful as a STARTING point. Then the real work begins: I apply my own judgment on top — is the
consensus the AI captured actually right? what's the question it didn't think to ask? what's the
first-principles read, the second-order consequence, the contrarian possibility? — and I own the
conclusion myself. The AI's confident recommendation is where I start thinking, not where I stop.
Outsourcing the call to it would be trading away the only thing that makes my judgment worth
anything.

> Look for: Notes AI output is consensus not edge · Notes confidence isn't correctness · Treats it as a starting point not a basis · Applies own judgment and owns the conclusion


### 6. Why is 'asking the right question' more valuable than ever in the AI age?
Tests the questions-over-answers idea.

**Strong answer.** Because answers have become nearly free. You can ask the machine almost
anything and get a fluent, instant, often excellent response — so producing answers is no longer
scarce or valuable. What IS scarce is knowing which questions to ask, because an answer is only
ever as good as the question that prompted it, and the machine doesn't know which questions
matter. The analyst who asks an obvious, mediocre question gets an obvious, mediocre answer
that's no better than anyone else's; the analyst who asks the sharp, non-obvious, exactly-right
question unlocks insight the machine could always have provided but no one else thought to
request. So the human skill of framing — taking a messy, complex real situation and identifying
the one or two questions whose answers actually determine the outcome — becomes the
highest-leverage capability there is. It's also the hardest to automate, because it requires
understanding the situation deeply enough to know what's worth asking, which is judgment, not
retrieval. In a world of abundant answers, the quality of your questions becomes the clearest
expression of the quality of your thinking — and the questioner, not the answerer, is where the
value moves.

> Look for: Notes answers are now abundant/free · Notes an answer is only as good as the question · Frames question-asking as judgment/framing · Concludes the questioner holds the value


### 7. Some say judgment and 'soft skills' are just a comforting story analysts tell
themselves, and AI will eventually do those too. Your view?

Opinion question steelmanning the strong-AI / no-human-edge view.

**Strong answer.** It's a serious challenge and I won't wave it away — AI capabilities are
advancing fast, and I'd be foolish to claim any edge is permanent or that I know exactly where
the line will sit in a decade. So I hold this with humility. But I'd make a few points. First, even if AI
eventually encroaches on judgment, the RELATIVE value of human judgment rises as the
mechanics get automated — whatever remains hardest to automate becomes the most valuable
thing a human can do, so moving toward judgment is the right direction regardless of how far AI
advances. Second, some of these edges are hard for AI specifically because of how it's built: a
model trained on the consensus and the past structurally struggles to produce the
correctly-contrarian, genuinely novel view, and that's not a temporary gap, it's close to inherent.
Third, there's the matter of OWNERSHIP and accountability — someone has to take responsibility
for a consequential decision, put their name on it, and be answerable for it, and that's as much a
social and ethical role as a cognitive one; we may not want a machine owning those calls even if
it could make them. And fourth, even granting all the uncertainty, the practical response is the
same: develop the judgment, use AI as leverage, and stay adaptable. So my honest view is: I
don't claim human judgment is permanently safe, I claim it's the most durable and valuable place
to stand, the right direction to move, and the thing most worth developing — held with the
humility that the frontier will keep moving and I'll need to keep moving with it.

> Look for: Engages the challenge with humility · Notes relative value of judgment rises as mechanics automate · Notes consensus-training makes contrarian/novel hard for AI · Adds ownership/accountability and adaptability


### 8. How do you avoid your own thinking atrophying as you rely more on AI?
Tests awareness of the outsourced-judgment / atrophy risk.

**Strong answer.** By drawing a deliberate line between using AI for the work and using it for the
thinking, and protecting the thinking as mine. The risk is real and insidious: judgment is a muscle
that weakens without use, so an analyst who lets the machine reason for him gradually loses the
ability to reason himself, becoming a hollowed-out operator who can only relay what the machine
says. To avoid that, I use AI for the legwork — the gathering, computing, summarising, drafting —
and as a challenger, but I make sure I'm still doing the actual reasoning: forming my own view
first or independently, framing the questions myself, working through the first-principles and
second-order logic in my own head, and owning the conclusion rather than adopting the AI's. I
treat its output as an input to my judgment, never a substitute for it. Practically, that means not
just accepting a fluent answer because it sounds right — verifying it, interrogating it, and being
able to explain the reasoning myself without leaning on the machine. The test I'd apply is: could I
defend this conclusion, and the reasoning behind it, if the AI vanished? If the answer is no — if I
can only point to what the machine told me — then I've outsourced my judgment and I need to
do the thinking myself. Use AI to sharpen the blade, never to replace the hand that wields it.

> Look for: Names the atrophy risk explicitly · Separates AI-for-work from AI-for-thinking · Does own reasoning / forms own view / owns conclusion · Gives a test (could I defend it without the AI?)


### 9. After everything in this module, what do you think it really means to 'think
like a top-tier analyst'?
Capstone synthesis question — closing reflection on the whole module.

**Strong answer.** I've come to see that it was never about any single technique — not a model, a
ratio, or a trick — it's about becoming a particular kind of thinker. A top-tier analyst frames the
right questions and reasons from first principles instead of accepting the given narrative. They
think in second- and third-order effects, in probabilities and asymmetries rather than false
certainties. They carry a latticework of mental models from many disciplines, and they invert
problems and pre-mortem their decisions to find what would prove them wrong. They read the
truth beneath the numbers and filter the rare signal from the deafening noise. They judge
management as an owner judges a steward. They debug their own minds, knowing that's the
least trustworthy instrument they hold. And they decide well under irreducible uncertainty, sizing

for survival, holding conviction and humility together, and owning the call with their name on it.
Underneath all of it is a temperament — rigorous, independent, self-aware, decisive, and
humane. What strikes me most is that this is exactly what makes a human irreplaceable in the
age of AI: the machine can do the analysis, but this kind of thinking is the job, and only a person
can do it. So thinking like a top-tier analyst means being that thinker — and using every tool, AI
included, in service of a judgment that's genuinely your own. That's the whole thing, and it's a
lifelong practice, not a destination.

> Look for: Synthesises the module into a way of thinking not a technique · Names multiple edges across the module · Identifies the underlying temperament · Connects it to AI-age irreplaceability


### 10. Ten years from now, what separates the analysts who thrived from those
who didn't?
Forward-looking capstone question — the AI spine's final payoff.

**Strong answer.** I think the dividing line will be exactly the one this module points to: whether
they moved up to judgment and used AI as leverage, or stayed down in the mechanics and either
competed with the machine or ignored it. The ones who DIDN'T thrive will fall into two groups —
those who tried to out-work AI at modelling, summarising and screening and lost because the
machine was simply better, and those who refused to use AI out of fear or pride and were
out-produced by those who embraced it. The ones who THRIVED will have done the harder, more
human thing: conceded the mechanics to the machine, used it relentlessly as a tireless tool and
challenger, and poured their freed energy into the judgment that compounds — asking sharper
questions, reasoning from first principles, thinking around corners, being correctly contrarian
when all the machines converged on the consensus, judging people, and owning the high-stakes
calls. And crucially, they'll have kept their own judgment sharp rather than letting it atrophy,
never outsourcing the thinking to the machine. Underneath it all, what separates them is
temperament and the willingness to keep developing judgment in a world that makes it easy to
stop. The tools will be unrecognisable in ten years; the analysts who thrived will be the ones who
understood that the tools were never the point — that the quality of their own thinking was the
one edge that endured, and they invested in it relentlessly. That's the bet I'm making with my
career, and it's the one this whole course has been preparing me to make.

> Look for: Divides on moving-up-with-AI vs competing/ignoring · Names both failure modes · Emphasises judgment that compounds + not letting it atrophy · Closes on temperament/judgment as the enduring edge


---

## Simulation — The AI Says Buy — Being the Irreplaceable Analyst

*HARD · 10 min · decision making*


**Scenario.** You're an analyst at a Mumbai fund that has just adopted a powerful AI research
platform. Your PM is excited by it and hands you its latest output: a thorough, confident,
well-reasoned BUY recommendation on a popular large-cap that's been a market darling —
the kind of stock everyone owns and loves. 'The AI has done all the work,' he says. 'Look at
this analysis — it's better than what most analysts produce in a week. Just confirm it and let's
size it up. This is the future; we can move so much faster now.' You're impressed by the AI's
output too — it really is comprehensive and the model is clean. But something nags at you
about what your actual job is in this moment. You have ten minutes to decide how to handle
this — not just whether to buy the stock, but how to be a HUMAN analyst worth employing
when the machine can produce an analysis like this. The question is: what do YOU add that
the machine and the consensus it reflects do not?

**Your task.** Don't just rubber-stamp the AI's recommendation — but don't dismiss the tool
either. Be the irreplaceable analyst. (1) RECOGNISE WHAT THE AI OUTPUT IS: a thorough,
confident analysis of a consensus-darling is, by AI's nature (trained on the consensus and the

past), essentially a well-organised distillation of the BULLISH CONSENSUS — the view
everyone already holds — not proprietary edge. Confidence is not correctness. (2) DON'T
OUTSOURCE THE JUDGMENT: use the AI's work as a starting point and for the mechanics, but
apply your own judgment on top and own the conclusion — never let the machine make the
call. (3) ADD THE JUDGMENT LAYER (the module's edges): what's the right QUESTION the AI
didn't ask? What does FIRST-PRINCIPLES reasoning say about the darling's durability? What
are the SECOND-ORDER risks of a crowded consensus? Is the loud bullishness SIGNAL or
NOISE? What does owning it at today's valuation imply (S9)? (4) CONTRARIAN COURAGE: the
edge comes from being correctly DIFFERENT from the crowd — and on a consensus-darling
that all the machines and investors love, the valuable human work may be to scrutinise the
bull case the AI just echoed, not to pile onto it. (5) Decide how to respond to the PM — adding
genuine value as a thinker (the independent judgment, the questions, the risks the
consensus is missing) rather than either blindly confirming or being a mere obstacle — and
what you'd actually recommend. Write a short note.

**Objectives**

- Recognise the AI's confident output as the consensus, not proprietary edge
- Refuse to outsource the call to the machine while still using it for the mechanics
- Apply the judgment layer — the right question, first-principles, second-order, signal-vs-noise,
valuation
- Deploy contrarian courage — scrutinise the consensus the AI echoed rather than piling on
- Respond to the PM by adding value as a thinker, not by rubber-stamping or obstructing
- Articulate what a human analyst adds that the machine and the consensus do not
Decision points
- What IS the AI's confident output on a consensus-darling — proprietary edge, or a distillation of the
bullish consensus?
- Should you confirm the call, or apply your own independent judgment and own the conclusion?
- What does the judgment layer (right question, first-principles, second-order, valuation) add that the
AI didn't?
- On a stock all the machines and the crowd love, where does a human's contrarian edge lie?

How a top analyst would think it through. A strong response does NOT just
'confirm it and size it up' — rubber-stamping the machine's output is precisely the
outsourcing of judgment that makes an analyst replaceable, and it's the central failure
this finale warns against. Nor does it dismiss the AI, which would forfeit a powerful
tool. The analyst threads the needle by being the irreplaceable human. FIRST,
recognise what the AI output actually is: a thorough, confident analysis of a popular
darling is, by the nature of how AI is built (trained on the consensus and the past),
essentially a well-organised distillation of the BULLISH CONSENSUS — the view the
whole market already holds — not proprietary insight; and the AI's confidence and
polish are not evidence of correctness (it can be confidently wrong and it reflects its
training). So the AI has given the PM the crowd's view dressed up beautifully, which is
available to everyone and is therefore not edge. SECOND, refuse to outsource the
judgment: the AI's work is a fine STARTING POINT and handles the mechanics (the
model, the summary), but the analyst must apply her own judgment on top and own
the conclusion rather than letting the machine make the call. THIRD, add the
judgment layer — the module's edges: ask the right QUESTION the AI didn't pose (not
'is the bull case well-argued?' but e.g. 'what is everyone, including the machine,
assuming about this darling that might be wrong?'); reason from FIRST PRINCIPLES
about whether the company's advantages are as durable as the consensus assumes;
think SECOND-ORDER about the risks of a crowded consensus (everyone already owns
it, so who's left to buy, and what happens if the narrative cracks?); separate SIGNAL
from NOISE in the ubiquitous bullishness the AI amplified; and apply
VALUATION/capital-allocation discipline (S9) — a wonderful company is not a
wonderful purchase at any price, and a beloved darling is often priced for perfection.
FOURTH, CONTRARIAN COURAGE: edge comes from being correctly DIFFERENT from
the crowd, and on a consensus-darling that all the machines and investors love, the
valuable human contribution is usually to scrutinise and stress-test the bull case the AI
just echoed — to look hard for what the consensus is missing — rather than to add one
more confirming voice. This doesn't mean reflexively shorting the darling; it means
doing the independent work to judge whether the consensus (and its valuation) is
right, and being willing to reach a different conclusion. FIFTH, respond to the PM well:
not by blindly confirming (rubber-stamping), and not by being a mere obstacle
(dismissing the tool or the idea), but by adding genuine value as a THINKER — using
the AI for the mechanics, then bringing the independent questions, the second-order
risks, the valuation scrutiny and the contrarian stress-test that the machine and the
consensus can't, and owning a real recommendation (which might be 'the AI has given
us the consensus; here's my independent read and the risks it's missing, and here's
what I'd actually do and at what size'). The meta-point the analyst should articulate:
what a human adds is precisely the judgment layer the machine can't occupy, and the
way to be worth employing is to stand on the AI, not to compete with it or surrender to
it. Traps to name: rubber-stamping the confident output (outsourcing judgment),
mistaking the AI's consensus for edge, mistaking confidence for correctness, piling
onto a crowded darling, and on the other side dismissing a powerful tool out of pride.
The lesson — and the close of the whole module: the machine does the analysis; you
do the thinking; and the thinking, especially the independent, contrarian,
judgment-laden thinking, is what makes you irreplaceable.

Hints
- A confident AI analysis of a stock everyone loves — is that proprietary edge, or the consensus
beautifully organised?
- Confidence and polish are not correctness. What's your job once the machine has produced this?
- What question didn't the AI ask? What is everyone — including the machine — assuming that might
be wrong?

- Edge comes from being correctly different from the crowd. On a darling all the machines love,
where's the human edge?
- Don't rubber-stamp it and don't just dismiss it — stand ON the AI: use it for the mechanics, add the
judgment, own the call.

## Capstone Project
The Analyst's Verdict — One Company, The Whole Mind

This is not a long initiation report — it is one sharp, owned JUDGMENT that proves you can
think like a top-tier analyst. You will pick a single real NSE/BSE-listed company and produce a
tight Analyst's Verdict: a buy / avoid / hold call that visibly deploys every thinking edge from
this module on one decision. Crucially, you will use AI exactly as the module teaches —
delegating the MECHANICS (gathering filings, summarising, building a quick model,
screening) to the machine, and reserving the JUDGMENT for yourself. You will frame the real
question (S1-2), reason from first principles (S2), think second-order (S3) and in asymmetry
(S4), read the truth beneath the numbers (S7), judge management as a capital allocator (S9),
apply a couple of mental-model lenses (S5), run a pre-mortem and set kill criteria (S6), filter
signal from noise (S8), debug your own biases (S10), then make the call under uncertainty —
sized for survival, held with conviction and humility, with your name on it (S11). Finally you
will write a short Human Edge log (S12) showing what the machine did versus what only your
judgment could do, and why your verdict is more than the consensus the AI would have
produced. The whole point: the machine can do the analysis; this capstone proves YOU can
do the thinking. (Educational exercise. Your verdict and all figures are your own illustrative
analysis for learning, not a Valuevade recommendation; anchor figures to screener.in and the
company's filings, and treat every number as illustrative.)

Estimated time: ~10 hours

Deliverable
A single 4-6 page 'Analyst's Verdict' decision memo on one real NSE/BSE-listed company of
your choice, plus two short supporting appendices. (1) THE VERDICT MEMO: states the one
question the decision hinges on, a first-principles read of the business, the asymmetry (what
you win if right vs lose if wrong), a forensic note on earnings quality (profit-vs-cash and one
footnote check), a one-line capital-allocation judgment on management, two mental-model
lenses, and a clear buy/avoid/hold call sized for survival and held with conviction and
humility. (2) THE PRE-MORTEM & KILL-CRITERIA BOX: a half-page 'assume this failed in two
years — why?', plus the specific pre-committed conditions under which you would exit, and
the one piece of evidence that would prove you wrong. (3) THE HUMAN EDGE / AI
COLLABORATION LOG: a one-page table listing what you delegated to AI (the mechanics)
versus the judgment you owned, and a short paragraph on why your verdict differs from the
consensus an AI would have echoed. Keep it tight — this is a test of judgment per page, not
length.

Choosing your company. Choose a single company listed on the NSE or BSE
that you can actually get data on via screener.in and its annual report / filings.
Pick one that is genuinely contestable — where a thoughtful person could argue
either side — rather than an obvious slam-dunk, because the whole point is to
show JUDGMENT, not to recite a consensus. Avoid anything you cannot find real
filings for. All figures you use are illustrative and anchored to screener.in / the
company's disclosures; this is a learning exercise, not a recommendation.

The five phases

Phase 1 — Frame the Question & Reason from First Principles
Draws on: S1 The Analyst's Edge, S2 First-Principles Thinking
~2 hrs
AI does: Delegate the MECHANICS: ask AI to summarise the last few annual reports and
recent results, pull the key segment/economics data, and give you the consensus view in
two lines. This is the grunt work — let the machine do it fast.
You own: The machine cannot tell you which QUESTION matters or reason up from
fundamentals on a novel situation — that's yours. Frame the ONE question the whole
decision hinges on, and strip the business to its first-principles economics in your own
words.
- Name the company and write, in ONE sentence, the single question the buy/avoid/hold
decision actually turns on (not 'is it a good company?' but the specific crux).
- Use AI to compress the filings and surface the consensus view — then set that
consensus aside as the crowd's position, not your conclusion.
- Write a 1-paragraph FIRST-PRINCIPLES read: strip away the narrative and describe
what this business fundamentally is, how it actually makes money, and what truly
drives its long-term earning power.

Phase 2 — The Analytical Core — Asymmetry, the Numbers, and
Management
Draws on: S3 Second-Order Thinking, S4 Probabilistic Thinking, S7 Reading Financials, S9
Capital Allocator's Mind
~3 hrs
AI does: Delegate the MECHANICS: have AI build or check a quick model, compute the
profit-vs-cash comparison and key working-capital trends over a few years, and screen
the peer set.
You own: The JUDGMENT on top is yours: the second-order consequences, the payoff
asymmetry, the forensic read of what the cash and footnotes say versus the story, and
the verdict on whether management allocate capital like owners.
- SECOND-ORDER: write 2-3 'and then what?' chains — the consequences of the
consequences (competitor reactions, sector/macro ripples) the first-level view misses.
- ASYMMETRY: in a few lines, map what you WIN if the thesis is right versus what you
LOSE if it's wrong, and state whether the payoff is favourably asymmetric at today's
price.
- FORENSIC READ: run the master profit-vs-operating-cash-flow check over a few years
and read ONE footnote/policy item (e.g. 'other income', receivables, related parties) —
note whether the cash and notes confirm or contradict the headline.
- CAPITAL ALLOCATION: in one or two lines, judge management as an owner would —
are they rational allocators (reinvestment returns, sensible M&A, price-sensitive
buybacks, returning cash) or empire-builders chasing size?

Phase 3 — Stress-Test & Debias — Lenses, Pre-Mortem, Signal,
and the Devil's Advocate
Draws on: S5 Latticework, S6 Inversion & Pre-Mortem, S8 Signal vs Noise, S10 Debugging
Your Brain
~2.5 hrs
AI does: Use AI as a DEVIL'S ADVOCATE here: ask it to build the strongest bear case
against your emerging view and to point out where your reasoning looks like confirmation
bias. Let it attack the thesis — that's its most valuable role in this phase.
You own: Only you can choose which mental-model lenses fit, find the courage to take
the disconfirming evidence seriously, separate the durable signal from the loud noise,
and honestly debug your own biases. Acting on what the devil's advocate surfaces is
yours.
- LENSES: apply TWO mental models from your latticework (e.g. incentives, competitive
moats, base rates, scale economics) and note what each reveals that a single lens
would miss.
- PRE-MORTEM + KILL CRITERIA: write 'assume this call failed badly in two years —
why?', then set the specific, pre-committed conditions under which you would EXIT,
and name the one piece of evidence that would prove you wrong.
- SIGNAL vs NOISE: identify the one quiet thing that genuinely changes the thesis, and
the loud thing(s) that are just noise the market (and the AI) are amplifying.
- DEBIAS: name the bias you are personally most at risk of on THIS stock (anchoring?
confirmation? recency? loss aversion?) and the system you'll use to counter it — then
run the 'would I buy it here?' test honestly.

Phase 4 — The Verdict — Sized for Survival and Owned
Draws on: S11 Decision-Making Under Uncertainty
~1.5 hrs
AI does: Minimal AI here — at most, ask it to stress-test your sizing logic. This step is
irreducibly human: committing to a consequential call in the fog is not something to
outsource.
You own: Make the call. Under irreducible uncertainty, hold conviction and humility
together, size it so that being wrong is survivable, and own it with your name on it.
- State the VERDICT clearly: buy / avoid / hold, with the 2-3 reasons it follows from your
work — not from the consensus.
- SIZE IT: say how big a position this would be and why, sized to BOTH your conviction
AND the consequence of being wrong, under the survive-first rule (never a bet that
ruins you if wrong).
- Write the closing CONVICTION + HUMILITY line: what you believe, and the honest
acknowledgement of what you might be wrong about — then put your name on it (you
are owning this call).

Phase 5 — The Human Edge — What the Machine Couldn't Do
Draws on: S12 Thinking in the Age of AI
~1 hrs
AI does: No new AI analysis — this phase is your reflection on the human+AI division of
labour you just practised.
You own: Articulate, concretely, what you delegated to the machine versus the
judgment only you could provide — and why your verdict is more than the consensus an
AI would echo.
- Build the AI COLLABORATION LOG: a short table with two columns — 'Delegated to AI
(the mechanics)' vs 'My own judgment (the edge)' — listing what each actually did
across the project.
- Write a short paragraph: how does your verdict DIFFER from the consensus view an AI
would have produced, and which thinking edge (the right question, the contrarian
read, the second-order insight, the forensic catch) created that difference?

Grading rubric (out of 100)
Category                      Wt       What great looks like

Framing &                     15       Identifies the single real crux of the decision (not a generic 'is it
First-Principles                       good?') and reasons up from the business's fundamental
economics in original words, not borrowed narrative.

Asymmetry &                   15       Maps win-if-right vs lose-if-wrong honestly and judges whether
Probabilistic                          the payoff is favourably asymmetric at today's price, rather
Reasoning                              than just predicting an outcome.

Forensic Read &               15       Runs the profit-vs-cash check and a footnote read that
Capital-Allocation                     genuinely informs the view, and judges management as an
Judgment                               owner would — allocator vs empire-builder.

Pre-Mortem, Kill              15       A real assume-it-failed analysis, specific pre-committed exit
Criteria &                             conditions, and a genuine engagement with the bear case (not
Disconfirmation                        a strawman) — including what would prove the thesis wrong.

Bias-Debugging &              10       Names the learner's own most-likely bias on this stock with a
Signal vs Noise                        concrete counter-system, and separates the quiet
thesis-changing signal from the loud noise the market and AI
amplify.

The Verdict:                  20       A clear, owned call that follows from the learner's own work;
Conviction, Humility                   sized to both conviction and consequence under survive-first;
& Survival-Sizing                      and held with explicit conviction AND honest humility about
what could be wrong.

The Human Edge &              10       Used AI for the mechanics but visibly owned the judgment;
AI Collaboration                       clearly articulates what the machine did vs what only human
judgment could do, and why the verdict beats the consensus.

Quality bands
Job-ready analyst (85-100) — Reads like a real IC memo from a sharp analyst — a genuinely
owned, contestable judgment with the whole framework visibly at work and AI used as
leverage, not a crutch. You could defend this in an interview.
Strong (70-84) — Most edges deployed well and a clear owned call, but one or two phases are
thin — e.g. the asymmetry is asserted not argued, or the bear case is a strawman. Tighten
the weak phase.

Developing (55-69) — The structure is there but the judgment is mostly consensus — it reads
like what an AI would say. Push harder on the contrarian read, the second-order, and the
forensic catch that makes it YOURS.
Rebuild (below 55) — Either the call isn't owned (it's the AI's), the framework isn't visible, or
survival-sizing/kill-criteria are missing. Re-run the phases and make the judgment your own.

Going further
- Write the same verdict on the OPPOSITE side (steelman the call you didn't make) and notice how
strong the other side really is — the best disconfirmation drill there is.
- Six months later, re-open the memo and grade your PROCESS, not the outcome (S4 resulting): was
the reasoning sound regardless of how the stock moved?
- Pick a current market darling everyone (and every AI) loves and write the contrarian risk memo —
practise the S12 sim for real.
- Turn the verdict into a 3-minute spoken pitch you could deliver in an interview, defending the
judgment without leaning on the AI.

Author's note. Coach the learner that the grade is judgment per page, not
length — a tight, owned, contestable call beats a long consensus summary
every time. The single most important discipline to enforce is the human+AI
division of labour: AI does the mechanics, the learner owns the judgment, and
the test of success is whether the learner could defend every conclusion if the AI
vanished. Push them toward a genuinely contestable company and a genuinely
non-consensus angle, because the whole module culminates in being correctly
different from the crowd. Remind them this is an educational exercise — their
verdict and figures are illustrative learning work anchored to screener.in and
filings, not a recommendation — and that owning a call with their name on it,
sized so being wrong is survivable, is exactly what thinking like a top-tier analyst
means.
